## WHAT THIS EDITION IS, AND WHAT IT REFUSES TO DO

This is a Mains-focused edition, not a second comprehensive package. Topic 14 is the only topic in
this lane that owns **no question at all** — and it is also the topic whose two owner files contain
the highest density of substantive error yet measured here. Those two facts are connected, and the
edition is built on the connection.

Six things this edition does that a conventional treatment of "Köppen" does not.

1. **It disproves its own owner's grounding claim rather than inheriting it.** C1 line 4 states that
   the file is *"Grounded in: G.C. Leong, Certificate Physical & Human Geography + Majid Husain,
   Indian & World Geography."* **Both halves fail for Köppen, and the failure was established by
   reading the books, not by failing to find a search hit.** Leong's entire classification chapter
   is book pages 113–115, it prints a **latitudinal-zone scheme of twelve types**, and **Leong's
   index carries no Köppen entry at all** — the K-block runs *Kalmuk · Karst Lakes · Karst (region)
   · Kazakhs · Khamsin · Kirghiz · Knick point · Koum* and then jumps to L. Majid Husain's *Indian &
   World Geography* has **no classification section**: Chapter Three (Climatology) runs scan PDF
   74–127 without one, and a term sweep of the whole 488-page book returns exactly one
   classification-adjacent hit — `isohyet` on scan PDF 124 — which is a **Topic 13** object. **The
   only local book that teaches Köppen is D.R. Khullar, which C1 does not cite.** The source line is
   rewritten here and the negative findings are printed, because a student who opens Leong looking
   for Köppen must be told in advance that it is not there.
2. **It restores two whole schemes that the lane routed in and both owners dropped.** The Topic 13
   freeze routes **Stamp, Kendrew and R.L. Singh** into Topic 14 by name. Neither owner mentions any
   of the three. Both local books carry them at full numeric detail — Khullar printed folios 156–157
   and 164–165, Majid *Geography of India* printed folios 4.33 and 4.36. **Lessons 13 and 14 exist
   because of a routing instruction, not because of an owner sentence**, and they are the two
   lessons with no owner text to lean on anywhere.
3. **It separates two Thornthwaites that both owners merge into one.** C2 and C1 describe the
   **1948** moisture-index scheme. Khullar dates the scheme **1931**, revised **1933** and **1948**,
   says the 1948 was *"markedly different"*, and then **tabulates the 1931 one** across two folios.
   The owners therefore describe one version and cite a book that prints another. **Lesson 11
   teaches the scheme the books tabulate; Lesson 12 teaches the scheme the owners describe.** The
   merge is the error; teaching it once would reproduce it.
4. **It corrects the single most examinable fact in the topic, which both owners get backwards.**
   C2 line 49 says, ✅-marked, *"India has Koppen A, B, C and H in standard applied schemes."* The
   A/B/C/H result is **Trewartha's**, printed at Khullar folio 163. Khullar's **Köppen** India is
   **A, B, C, D and E across nine regions with no H** — and **Dfc**, **Et** and **E** are the three
   regions C2's seven-row table destroys. **Dfc is the row most likely to be examined and is absent
   from both owners.**
5. **It puts a primary official document under the maps for the first time in this topic.** Neither
   owner mentions a **climatological normal**. Every classification in this file is a threshold test
   applied to a normal, and the normals India's climate maps rest on were retrieved as a document:
   **IMD, *Climatological Tables of Observatories in India 1991-2020*, Ninth Edition (2022)**, 844
   pages, re-read at generation time. It is also where the edition's hardest discipline lies: **three
   official IMD surfaces give three different station counts — 405, 416 and 433 — and this edition
   prints all three with their URLs and computes nothing between them.**
6. **It refuses the owner's own most quotable sentence.** C1 line 105 asserts, ✅-marked, that
   *"Thar's BWh belt is projected to expand eastward and southward"*. It has no dataset, no
   baseline, no scenario and no period — and it sits directly above C1 line 106, which forbids
   quoting a shift without exactly those things, and C1 lines 183–186, which forbid quoting a
   projected boundary for a given year. **The claim is refused. Lesson 16 teaches the mechanism that
   makes such a claim tempting, and withholds the magnitude on every arrow it draws.**

**The structural fact that shapes everything below.** Topic 14 owns nothing the examiner has ever
asked. That is not a reason to teach it thinly; it is the reason the lesson count is **seventeen**
rather than twelve. With no PYQ scaffolding, **every practice item in this file is original**, every
rubric is built from scratch, and the verified-PYQ-linkage section of the final arc is a
**routing-and-contribution table**, not an answer bank. The topic earns its place because it is the
**grammar** of Topics 15 and 17–25, which *are* tested: 2021 Prelims Q60, 2021 Q61, 2023 Q63, 2024
Q13 and 2021 GS-I Q15 are all type-identification demands that a classification-literate candidate
reaches faster.

## READING CONVENTIONS — FROZEN

| Mark | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ✅ | Read from a named local book at a printed folio, or from a named official document |
| 📄 | Read from a named official live surface, with its access date |
| ⚠️ | Standard physical geography; **no local book and no retrieved page states it**; never attributed to a source |
| ✗ | A source defect, quoted so that it can be recognised, never asserted |
| ⇄ | A live conflict between two sources, both shown, **neither resolved** |
| ↪ | A route-out to the owning file, by exact path |
| 🇮🇳 | The lesson's mandatory India hook, each one carrying its own stated limitation |

**Citation conventions, frozen.**

- **D.R. Khullar, *India — A Comprehensive Geography*** is a **two-up rotated scan with no text
  layer**. It is cited as **"Khullar printed folio N (scan PDF p.M)"**, where `M = (N + 34) // 2`.
  Every folio used here was confirmed by reading its printed number in the rendered page image.
  **No Khullar figure number is ever cited alone**, because **FIG. 5.27 is printed twice** — once
  for the Stamp map at folio 157 and again for the Köppen map at folio 159 — while the Köppen body
  text at folio 160 cites **Fig. 5.28** for what appears to be the same map.
- **Khullar's contents page is never cited.** It lists *Köppen 158 · Thornthwaite 160 · R.L. Singh
  162 · Trewartha 164*, but the body has **Trewartha at 162–163** and **R.L. Singh at 164–165**. The
  two entries are swapped. Every folio in this file is a **body** folio.
- **Majid Husain, *Geography of India*** is cited as **"Majid *GoI* printed folio C.P (scan PDF
  p.M)"**, using the book's chapter-dot-page numbering. **The local 160-page file is a sampled
  extract, not the complete book**, and within Chapter 4 it keeps roughly every fifth folio. Verified
  anchors: scan PDF 73 = folio **4.28**, PDF 74 = **4.33**, PDF 75 = **4.36**. **Folios 4.29–4.30
  (Köppen) and 4.34–4.35 (Trewartha) are not in the local file** and are never quoted anywhere in
  this edition. Every page of this book carries the watermark `http://www.visionias.net/` as its
  only extractable text; all content was read from page renders.
- **G.C. Leong, *Certificate Physical and Human Geography*** is cited as **"Leong book page N (scan
  PDF p.N+7)"**. Leong appears in this file for exactly two purposes: as the **rival zonal scheme**
  at pages 113–114, and as a **negative finding** at the index page, scan PDF 196. **Leong is cited
  for no Köppen content anywhere.**
- **Majid Husain, *Indian & World Geography*** appears once, as a cross-reference, and its single
  classification-adjacent entry is routed to Topic 13. Its OCR is degraded (`3,36`, `pj4om ayy`) and
  it is used for term sweeps, never for quotation.
- **IMD** is cited as **"IMD, *Climatological Tables of Observatories in India 1991-2020*, Ninth
  Edition (2022), PDF page N"**, with the retrieval date. **No station-level normal appears anywhere
  in this file**, because no station page was re-read for a value at generation time.
- **Units.** Khullar and Majid are metric and are reproduced as printed. **Leong's twelve-type table
  is wholly imperial** — its rainfall totals are in inches — and is reproduced in inches with the
  metric equivalent supplied by this edition and marked as supplied.

**A note on what "✅ from source book" is worth in this topic.** Both owners mark their most serious
errors ✅. C2's A/B/C/H misattribution is ✅. C2's wrong-version Thornthwaite is ✅. C1's unsourced
Thar projection is ✅. **In this file a ✅ means a named folio or a named document, and nothing
else.** Nineteen corrections below exist because the mark was used without the folio.

## THE NINETEEN CORRECTIONS — REGISTER

Every correction is applied **in the lesson where the material is taught**, with the source's own
sentence quoted first and the contradiction shown second. **None is applied to the owner files.**

| # | Where | What the source says | Why it fails | Lesson |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **C1** | Khullar folio 159 | *"h (heiss) — average annual temperature **under 18 °C**"* | *h* = *heiss* = hot; the hot member cannot be the one below the threshold. **Khullar's own folio 160 puts BWhw in western Rajasthan and BShw on the Thar margin** — impossible under his printed rule | **L4** |
| **C2** | Khullar folio 160, regions 8 and 9 | **Et** 0–10 °C and **E** warmest month 0–10 °C | **The same interval twice.** By Khullar's own T/F rule at folio 159, a polar region whose warmest month reaches 0–10 °C is **ET**, not **EF** | **L9** |
| **C3** | Khullar folio 163 | *"**Bsh** — tropical and subtropical **desert**"*, 50–100 cm | **S = steppe.** The 50–100 cm figure is itself steppe-consistent — internal evidence that the word is the slip and the number is sound | **L10** |
| **C4** | Khullar folio 163 | *"**Bwh** — **middle latitude** desert"*, west Rajasthan and Kachchh, to 20 cm | **h = hot / low latitude.** A middle-latitude desert is **BWk**. Khullar's own Köppen section calls the same tract **BWhw**, *hot* desert | **L10** |
| **C5** | Khullar folio 158 vs C1 line 74 | Khullar gives **−3 °C** for C/D; C1 notes many modern maps use **0 °C** | **Not an error — a convention.** C1 handles it correctly and the edition keeps C1's formulation, attaches the Khullar folio to the −3 °C half, and marks the 0 °C half ⚠️ | **L3** |
| **C6** | Khullar folio 159 vs C1 line 34 | Khullar lists *"a, c, f, h, m, g, s and w"* and defines only **a** and **c**; C1 asserts *"a/b/c/d for C-D"* and *"h/k for B"* ✅ | **a and c alone do not partition C and D.** A learner holding only Khullar's list cannot read a code containing **b** or **d** | **L5** |
| **C7** | Khullar folio 161 | **120 combinations**, **32** world types, **12** Indian types | **5 × 4 × 6 = 120 is derivable and must be shown as a derivation; 32 and 12 are source counts and must be attributed, not justified** | **L11** |
| **C8** | Khullar folio 162 | India type **12. EA′d**, western Rajasthan | The twelve-type claim **is** fully enumerated across folios 161–162. An early reading of folio 161 alone suggested eleven; **that hypothesis is disproved and appears nowhere as a defect** | **L11** |
| **C9** | Khullar folio 161, item 5 | Printed **CA′w′**, described as *"rainfall occurs in winter"* for Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra | By Khullar's own rules **w = deficient in winter**; a winter-rain region is deficient in **summer** and should read **CA′s**. Items 10 and 11 also carry bare **D′** and **E′** with no humidity prefix | **L11** |
| **C10** | C2 lines 36, 45, 55, 59; C1 line 160 | Thornthwaite described as a **moisture index using precipitation and potential evapotranspiration**, ✅ | That is the **1948** scheme. Khullar dates the scheme **1931**, calls 1948 *"markedly different"*, and **tabulates 1931**. C2's trap *"Thornthwaite is only temperature-based → it is moisture-index based"* is **false of 1931**, which uses both a P/E and a T/E index | **L11 + L12** |
| **C11** | C2 line 35 | Trewartha groups listed as *"tropical, dry, **subtropical**, temperate, boreal, polar and highland"* | Khullar folio 162 gives **A/B/C/D/E/F plus H**. "Subtropical" belongs to **Trewartha and Horn (1980)**, which Khullar's bibliography cites separately from Trewartha 1954 | **L10** |
| **C12** | Khullar folios 158 and 166; Majid 4.28 | Köppen **1901 · 1918 · 1931 · 1936**; Majid gives *"1918, 1931, 1936"* | Majid omits 1901; Khullar supplies it. The **Köppen–Geiger** pairing enters through the 1936 *Handbuch* editorship — the only sourced route to a phrase C1 uses twice with no source | **L2** |
| **C13** | Majid 4.28 vs Khullar 156 and 167 | Majid: *"L.D. Stamp and W.G. Kendrew, **1953**"*. Khullar: map *"After **Stamp**"* alone, bibliography **Stamp 1958** and **Kendrew 1961** separately, body at folio 164 pairing *"Kendrew and Stamp"* | **Present as "Stamp (and Kendrew)" with both datings and their folios. Do not resolve the date** | **L13** |
| **C14** | C2 line 49, ✅ | *"India has Koppen A, B, C and **H**"* | **A/B/C/H is Trewartha's** result (Khullar 163). Köppen-India is **A B C D E, nine regions, no H** (Khullar 158, 160). The claim imports H, deletes **Dfc**, and deletes **Et** and **E** | **L7–L9 build it; L10 closes it** |
| **C15** | C2 lines 20–28 | A **seven-row** Köppen-India table whose row 7 collapses *"E / H and local cold types"* and offers *"code varies by map convention"* | Khullar folio 160 gives **nine** regions and names **Dfc** explicitly for Sikkim, Arunachal Pradesh and parts of Assam | **L9** |
| **C16** | C1 line 142 vs C2 lines 27, 42, 54, 61 | C1 says **Cwa** for the northern plains; C2 says **Cwg** | Khullar folio 160 prints **Cwg**; folio 159 defines **g** as the *"Ganges type of annual march of temperature"*. **Teach Cwg with g explained; present Cwa as the modern-map equivalent, naming the convention** | **L9** |
| **C17** | Khullar FIG. 5.27 at folio 157 | **Region 4** and **region 8** both labelled *"Moderate Rainfall"* | The two lie in different halves of Stamp's split. Disambiguate by half; **say that the printed legend does not.** Majid 4.33 supplies figures for the **Tropical** member only; **the Temperate member's figures are supplied by no local folio and are not invented** | **L13** |
| **C18** | Khullar FIG. 5.31 legend vs his own body | Legend prints *"HUMID **SOUTH WEST**"* for division 3; body says *"Humid **South-East**"* | Majid's **Fig. 4.24** legend prints **"Humid South-East"**, and the geography settles it — Bengal, Odisha, Chhattisgarh and Jharkhand are eastern. **This is the only owner-adjacent defect a second local source resolves outright** | **L14** |
| **C19** | Khullar folio 164 vs Majid 4.36 | Khullar: *"**Per Humid** North-East"*. Majid body and map: *"**Humid** North-East"* | **Give both with citations; do not choose.** Majid additionally supplies the division's figures, which Khullar's folio does not | **L14** |

## REFUSALS — CLAIMS THIS EDITION DOES NOT MAKE

1. **No percentage of India's area reclassified**, by any scheme, in any period.
2. **No projected date or year** for any zone boundary.
3. **No statement that the Thar's BWh belt *is* expanding**, in any direction, without a named
   dataset, baseline period and scenario — none of which exists in the evidence base.
4. **No arithmetic reconciling IMD's 405, 416 and 433.** No subtraction, ratio, trend or explanation.
5. **No station-level normal** quoted from the IMD tables. No station page was re-read for a value.
6. **No Köppen-Geiger map version, resolution or release year.**
7. **No answer key, option letter or correctness judgement** for any question anywhere.
8. **No claim about Majid's Köppen-India or Trewartha-India text**, because folios 4.29–4.30 and
   4.34–4.35 are not in the local file.
9. **No "IMD classification of India into climatic zones."** The normals publication tabulates
   **stations**; the Hazard Atlas maps **hazards**. Neither classifies India.
10. **No reconciliation of Stamp 1953 against Stamp 1958 and Kendrew 1961.**

the bank, and no sentence in this file depends on a bank row.**

## Lesson 1 — Why climate is classified, and the schemes that compete

Progress: 1 / 17 · Stage: Foundation · Visual grammar: **decision tree** · India hook: **Blandford, 1889**

### The intuition, before any term

A classification is not a description. It is a **decision about what to measure**, taken before any
measuring happens, and every later disagreement between two climate maps of the same country is a
disagreement about that first decision.

Put it concretely. Two geographers stand at the same place in the Deccan. The first asks, *what
grows here?* The second asks, *how far from the equator am I?* They will draw different boundaries,
and neither is lying. One has decided that **vegetation is the honest summary of climate**; the
other has decided that **latitude is the honest cause of climate**. A classification is that
decision, written down and applied consistently.

This is why the topic begins with a question rather than a code. A candidate who starts at "A is
tropical, coolest month above 18 °C" has memorised an **answer** to a question they were never
shown. When the examiner asks for a *critical* examination, that candidate has nothing to be
critical about, because they have never seen the alternative that Köppen's choice ruled out.

**The alternative is held locally, in a book C1 itself names.** It is in Leong.

### The primary visual — the decision tree every classifier stands inside

```text

   evidence base, and the branches do not compete on accuracy — they compete on
   WHICH QUESTION THEY ANSWER.

                        ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
                        │  "Climate varies. Divide it HOW?" │

   ║  the signature) ║        ║  organising fact)    ║        ║  circulation)  ║
   ╚════╤════════════╝        ╚══════════╤═══════════╝        ╚═══════════╤════╝
        │                                │                                │

   Khullar folio 158:               scheme, book p.114:          No local source holds

   accepted as the best             0-10° · 10-30° · 30-45°      idea is named here and
   expression of the                45-65° · 65-90° · Alpine     never quantified
   TOTALITY of climate"             → TWELVE types, 1-12
        │                                │

   the scheme.
```

**Caption.** Four branches, four questions, and only the first one produces letter codes. A
candidate who can draw this tree can answer *"critically examine Köppen"* without having memorised a
single criticism, because the criticism is structural: Köppen answers the vegetation question
superbly and the causation question not at all.

### The rival that is actually in the book — Leong's twelve latitudinal types

This matters for two reasons. It is a **genuine alternative logic** held locally, and it is the
place where **C1's grounding claim collapses**.

✅ **Leong, book page 113 (scan PDF p.120)**, opening the chapter *"Climatic Types and Natural
Vegetation"*: *"It is necessary to divide the world into several climatic zones, each with its own
climatic characteristics, natural vegetation (forests, grasslands or deserts), crops, animals and
human activities… **Fig. 120** gives the scheme of the world's climatic types with their seasonal
rainfall and natural vegetation."*

✅ **Leong, book page 114 (scan PDF p.121)** then prints a table headed **"WORLD CLIMATIC TYPES"**
with the columns *Climatic Zone · Latitude (approximate) · Climatic Type · Rainfall Regime (with
approx total) · Natural Vegetation*.

```text
   LEONG'S SCHEME — ✅ book p.114. Rainfall as printed, in INCHES.
   Metric equivalents in [brackets] are SUPPLIED BY THIS EDITION, not printed.

   ZONE                       LAT.       TYPE                            RAIN   VEGETATION
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   EQUATORIAL   0-10° N & S   ──  1  Hot, wet equatorial            all yr  80" [203 cm]  equatorial rain forest
   HOT          10-30°        ──  2a Tropical Monsoon               summer  60" [152 cm]  monsoon forest
                              ──  2b Tropical Marine                summer  70" [178 cm]
                              ──  3  Sudan Type                     summer  30" [76 cm]   savanna
                              ──  4  Desert (a) Saharan             little   5" [13 cm]   desert scrub
                                         (b) Mid-latitude
   WARM TEMP.   30-45°        ──  5  W. Margin, MEDITERRANEAN       WINTER  35" [89 cm]   Medit. forest & shrub
                              ──  6  Central Cont., STEPPE          summer  20" [51 cm]   temperate grassland
                              ──  7  E. Margin (a) China (b) Gulf   summer  45" [114 cm]  warm wet forest, bamboo
                                                 (c) Natal
   COOL TEMP.   45-65°        ──  8  W. Margin, BRITISH             aut/win 30" [76 cm]   deciduous forest
                              ──  9  Central Cont., SIBERIAN        summer  25" [64 cm]   evergreen conifer
                              ── 10  E. Margin, LAURENTIAN          summer  40" [102 cm]  mixed forest
   COLD         65-90°        ── 11  Arctic or Polar                summer  10" [25 cm]   tundra, moss, lichen
   ALPINE       (by height)   ── 12  Mountain climate               heavy, variable       alpine pasture, conifer

   TWELVE TYPES, NUMBERED 1-12. Fig. 120 is captioned "Scheme of the world's
   climatic types (with seasonal rainfall and natural vegetation also indicated)".
   Book page 115 is "QUESTIONS AND EXERCISES" — THE CHAPTER ENDS THERE.
```

**Read the architecture, not the list.** Leong's scheme has **three organising axes and no
thresholds**: the **latitude band**, the **continental position** (western margin / central
continental / eastern margin), and the **season in which rain falls**. It produces *named types*,
not *computed classes*. It is reproducible by a reader with an atlas; Köppen's is reproducible by a
clerk with a station register. That difference is the whole argument of this topic in one sentence.

### ✗ U1, applied — the negative finding, printed because a student will go looking

C1 line 4 names Leong as a source for a Köppen file. **Leong contains no Köppen classification at
all**, and this was established by reading the book rather than by failing to find a search hit:

| Where the audit looked in Leong | What is actually there |
|---|---|
| Book pp. 113–114, the entire classification chapter | The **latitudinal-zone twelve-type scheme** above. No letter codes, no A/B/C/D/E groups, no 18 °C, no 10 °C |
| Book p. 115 | *"QUESTIONS AND EXERCISES"* and *"SELECTED QUESTIONS FROM CAMBRIDGE OVERSEAS SCHOOL CERTIFICATE PAPERS"* — the chapter ends |
| The index, K-block, scan PDF p.196 | *Kalmuk 152 · Karst Lakes 68 · Karst (region) 62 · Kazakhs 152 · Khamsin 143 · Kirghiz 151 · Knick point (or rejuvenated head) 44 · Koum (sandy desert) 56* — **then L. No Köppen entry** |
| Majid *IWG*, 488 pp, whole-book term sweep | **One** classification-adjacent hit: `isohyet`, scan PDF p.124 — a **Topic 13** object |

**The correction.** The Köppen content in this topic comes from **D.R. Khullar, *India — A
Comprehensive Geography*, printed folios 156–167**, which C1 does not cite and C2 does. Leong's
contribution to Topic 14 is **the rival scheme and nothing else**. This is said once, here, and
every later lesson honours it.

### The lineage the owners never give — ten authorities, one folio

✅ **Majid *GoI* printed folio 4.28 (scan PDF p.73)**, under the heading **CLIMATIC REGIONS OF
INDIA**, prints the canonical list of those who have divided India's climate:

```text
   TEN AUTHORITIES — ✅ Majid *GoI* folio 4.28, reproduced as printed

    1. H.E. BLANDFORD ................ 1889      ← the first attempt
    2. W. KÖPPEN ..................... 1918, 1931, 1936
    3. C.W. THORNTHWAITE ............. 1931, 1933, 1948
    4. L.D. STAMP and W.G. KENDREW ... 1953
    5. S.P. CHATTERJI ................ 1953
    6. G.T. TREWARTHA ................ 1954
    7. V.P. SUBRAMANYAM .............. 1956
    8. B.L.C. JOHNSON ................ 1969
    9. K.L. RAO et al. ............... 1971
   10. R.L. SINGH ................... 1971      ← the last, and a modification of #4

   THIS EDITION TEACHES FIVE OF THE TEN IN FULL: 2, 3, 4, 6 and 10.
   The other five are NAMED here and taught nowhere, because no local folio
   carries their content. That is a stated limit, not an omission.
```

### 🇮🇳 India hook 1 — Blandford, 1889, and the first Director General of IMD

✅ **Majid *GoI* folio 4.28** records that *"a systematic study of the climatic divisions of India
was attempted for the first time by H.E. Blandford — **the first Director General of the India
Meteorological Department** — in **1889**, who discovered that **all types of climates found in the
world are present within the subcontinent of India**"*, and adds that this classification, *"based on
temperature and rainfall of a few selected stations of India, was **almost an overgeneralisation**."*

This single sentence does four jobs at once, and it is the reason the lesson opens where it does.

1. It gives the topic a **date and a person**, so the first line of a Mains answer can be a fact.
2. It gives the topic its **India claim** — that the subcontinent is a compressed sample of the
   world's climates — which is precisely why India is the standard test case for every scheme that
   follows.
3. It supplies the topic's **first criticism from inside the source itself**, which is the rarest
   and most useful kind.
4. It explains, without saying so, **why Köppen was needed**: Blandford's divisions rest on *a few
   selected stations*. A scheme that rests on a few stations cannot be checked by anyone who does not
   hold those stations. Köppen's thresholds can be checked by anyone holding any station.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** The source's own verdict is that Blandford *overgeneralised*.
> The claim that *all* the world's climate types occur in India is therefore reported as **Blandford's
> finding as Majid records it**, not asserted as a fact — and the later lessons show exactly how it
> fails: Khullar's Köppen-India has **nine** regions drawn from **five** groups, not all of them, and
> Trewartha's India has **four** of **seven** types.

### The complete causal chain — the chain every later lesson is a segment of

```text
   OBSERVATION  →  NORMAL  →  THRESHOLD  →  CLASS  →  MAP  →  USE
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   a reading at    a 30-yr     a number      a letter   a line    a crop
   0830 and        arithmetic  chosen by     code       drawn     plan, a
   1730 IST        mean        a person      assigned   on a      forest
   (Lesson 6)      (Lesson 6)  (Lesson 3)    (L5, L7-9) sheet     type map
                                                         (L13-15) (L15)

   EVERY ARROW IS A DECISION, AND EVERY DECISION IS CONTESTABLE.
   Lesson 16 is simply this chain read backwards: if the NORMAL moves, the
   CLASS moves, and the USE built on the old class is invalidated — without
   anyone having changed the SCHEME.
```

**Assumptions the chain makes, stated as assumptions.** That a thirty-year mean is a stable
description of a place. That a boundary drawn through a gradient is useful despite being false at
the line. That vegetation is a reliable integrator of climate. **All three are defensible and none is
true**, and an evaluative answer says so.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ Köppen (Khullar 158) is **empirical and threshold-based**; ✅ Leong (p.114) is **zonal and positional**; ✅ Thornthwaite (Khullar 161) is **water-balance based**; ⚠️ genetic schemes are **causal**. Four questions, four answers |
| **Objection** | *"If vegetation is the signature, why not simply map vegetation and skip the climate codes?"* |
| **Reply** | Because vegetation records **disturbance as well as climate**. A cleared, cropped or grazed surface tells you about land use; a station register tells you about air. Köppen's thresholds were **chosen to fall where vegetation falls**, which lets the scheme be read from numbers and checked against plants — ✅ Khullar folio 158 is explicit that *native* vegetation is the criterion, which already concedes that non-native cover will not serve |
| **Unresolved residual** | **The genetic branch is unquantified in this evidence base.** No local book prints a genetic classification table and no retrieved page supplies one. It is named here as a fourth logic and **never given numbers anywhere in this file** |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating classification as a list to memorise rather than a choice to defend.** Every
  *"critically examine"* question in this topic is asking which question the scheme answers.
- ❌ **Citing Leong for Köppen** because C1's source line says so. Leong's chapter has no letter code
  in it and his index has no Köppen entry (U1).
- ❌ **Saying "all the world's climates occur in India" as a fact.** It is **Blandford's 1889
  finding**, and the same folio calls his scheme *almost an overgeneralisation*.
- ❌ **Calling Leong's scheme "wrong".** It is a different question, answered consistently. It is
  weaker only if the question you care about is reproducibility from station data.
- ❌ **Dating Köppen from Majid's list alone.** Majid gives *1918, 1931, 1936*; **1901 comes from
  Khullar folio 158** and is corrected in Lesson 2 (C12).

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** This lesson serves the *salient features of world's physical geography* clause by
establishing **why the world is divided into climatic classes at all**, which is the precondition for
Topics 15 and 17–25, where each of Leong's twelve types is taught in its own right.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** This lesson supports
`Geography/basic/15_Hot-Wet-Equatorial-Climate` and `Geography/basic/17_Savanna-Sudan-Climate`,
which own **2021 Prelims Q60** (*"Tropical rain forest vegetation structure identification"*,
`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` line 453) and **2021 Prelims Q61** (*"Savanna biome conditions
limiting tree development"*, line 454). Both ledger rows record *"Routed; key unavailable locally"*.
**No key, option letter or correctness judgement is recorded here or anywhere in this file.** The
contribution Topic 14 makes to those owners is precisely the point of this lesson: Köppen's
vegetation premise is **not a biome theory**, and a candidate who confuses the two will read a
savanna question as a climate question and lose the ecological content it actually wants.

**Answer use.** Open an evaluative answer by naming the **question the scheme answers**, not by
naming the scheme. One sentence does it: *"Köppen classifies by the thresholds at which native
vegetation changes; Leong classifies by latitude band and continental position; the two maps differ
because the two questions differ."* That sentence converts a descriptive answer into an analytical
one before the second line.

### Revision points

1. **A classification is a decision about what to measure, taken before measuring.** Disagreements
   between climate maps are disagreements about that decision.
2. **Four branches, four questions:** vegetation-anchored (Köppen) · latitude-and-position-anchored
   (Leong) · water-balance-anchored (Thornthwaite) · causal/genetic (⚠️ unquantified here).
3. ✅ **Khullar folio 158:** Köppen accepted *"native vegetation as the best expression of the
   totality of climate"*. The word **native** is doing work — it concedes that disturbed cover fails.
4. ✅ **Leong book p.114:** six zones — **0–10° · 10–30° · 30–45° · 45–65° · 65–90° · Alpine** — and
   **twelve** numbered types. **No thresholds, no letter codes.**
5. ✗ **U1: Leong contains no Köppen.** The index K-block runs *Kalmuk · Karst · Kazakhs · Khamsin ·
   Kirghiz · Knick point · Koum* and jumps to L. **Khullar is the Köppen source.**
6. ✅ **Majid *GoI* folio 4.28:** ten Indian authorities — Blandford 1889 · Köppen 1918/1931/1936 ·
   Thornthwaite 1931/1933/1948 · Stamp and Kendrew 1953 · Chatterji 1953 · Trewartha 1954 ·
   Subramanyam 1956 · Johnson 1969 · K.L. Rao et al. 1971 · R.L. Singh 1971.
7. 🇮🇳 **Blandford, 1889, first Director General of IMD** — the first systematic Indian climatic
   division; found *all* world climate types present in the subcontinent; the same folio calls it
   *"almost an overgeneralisation"* built on *"a few selected stations"*.
8. **The causal chain:** observation → normal → threshold → class → map → use. **Every arrow is a
   decision.** Lesson 16 reads it backwards.
9. **Three assumptions, all defensible, none true:** that a thirty-year mean is stable; that a line
   through a gradient is useful; that vegetation integrates climate reliably.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "Climate classification began with Köppen, who divided the world by
vegetation." Identify every element of that sentence that the local evidence contradicts, and state
what a defensible opening line would be instead.

**Model answer:** Three elements fail. **First, the chronology.** ✅ Majid *GoI* folio 4.28 places
the first systematic Indian climatic division with **H.E. Blandford in 1889**, the first Director
General of IMD, and lists ten authorities of whom Köppen is the second; Köppen's own first
publication is **1901** (✅ Khullar folio 158). Classification did not begin with him. **Second, the
criterion.** Köppen did not divide the world *by vegetation*; he divided it by **thresholds of
temperature and precipitation chosen to fall where native vegetation changes** (✅ Khullar folio
158). The distinction is the whole scheme: the inputs are numbers, the justification is plants, and
that is why a code can be computed from a station register without anyone visiting the site.
**Third, the implied monopoly.** ✅ Leong, book p.114, prints a complete rival — six latitude zones
and twelve types with no thresholds and no letter codes — and ✅ Khullar folio 161 prints a third
logic in Thornthwaite's water balance. A defensible opening line names the question rather than the
man: *"Climatic classification asks which measurable property best summarises a place; Köppen's
answer, from 1901, was the thresholds at which native vegetation changes."*

**Misconception to avoid:** That the schemes are competitors to be ranked. They answer different
questions — what grows, where you are, how much water plants get, what air mass is overhead — and an
answer that declares one "best" without naming its question has skipped the analysis the directive
was asking for.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 1

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Justify* the need for a formal climatic classification,
with reference to at least two competing bases of division.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** Climate varies continuously; administration, agriculture and comparison
require discrete, reproducible units. A classification supplies them by fixing, in advance, the
property that counts. The competing answers are real. Köppen fixed **thresholds of temperature and
precipitation chosen to fall where native vegetation changes**, making every class computable from a
station register and checkable by anyone. Leong fixed **latitude band and continental position**,
producing twelve named types from six zones with no threshold anywhere. Thornthwaite fixed **water
actually available to plants**, setting precipitation against evaporation. India shows why the
formalism is needed at all: Blandford's 1889 divisions, the first attempt, rested on *"a few
selected stations"* and were, by the source's own verdict, *"almost an overgeneralisation"* — a
scheme nobody else could verify. Classification is justified not because it is true at the boundary,
but because it is **reproducible away from it**. *(142 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Purpose clarity **3.0** · Rival-scheme contrast **3.0** · Source attribution
**2.0** · Conclusion **2.0** *(dimension weights 30 · 30 · 20 · 20)*

| Material | Owner, by exact path |
|---|---|
| Climate versus weather as an **observational** distinction; averaging period as a measurement convention | `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` |
| Each of Leong's twelve types taught in its own right | `Geography/basic/15_Hot-Wet-Equatorial-Climate`, `Geography/basic/17`–`25` |
| Natural vegetation and forest types as biomes | `Geography/advanced/15_India-Evergreen-Forests`; Environment-and-Ecology forest owners |

## Lesson 2 — Köppen: the man, the four editions, and the vegetation premise

Progress: 2 / 17 · Stage: Foundation · Visual grammar: **timeline strip, two rails** · India hook: **`g`, the Ganges type**

### The intuition, before any term

Schemes have editions, and editions matter in an examination for one reason: **the numbers move
between them**, and a candidate who cannot say which edition they are quoting cannot defend a
threshold when the examiner's map disagrees.

Köppen revised his scheme for thirty-five years. The thresholds taught in Lesson 3 are the ones that
survived; the C/D boundary at **−3 °C** is the one that did not survive universally, which is why
Lesson 3 has to name a convention rather than state a fact. Learn the chronology here and the
convention problem stops being a surprise.

### The primary visual — a two-rail timeline

A timeline is chosen deliberately. A table would imply the editions are alternatives; **a strip shows
revision without implying improvement**, which matters because Köppen's later editions are not
agreed to be better, only later.

```text
   RAIL 1 — KÖPPEN'S OWN EDITIONS                ✅ Khullar folio 158

     1901              1918              1931              1936
      │                 │                 │                 │
   FIRST            MAJOR            MAJOR            LATEST WORK
   PUBLISHED        REVISION         REVISION         ✅ "Das Geographische
                                     ✅ "Grundriss      System der Klimate",
                                     der Klimakund"     Handbuch der
                                     [sic], Walter      Klimablogie [sic],
                                     de Gruyler Co.,    "Ed. W. Koppen and
                                     Berlin             R. GEIGER, Vol. I"
      ▲                                                        ▲
      │                                                        │
   ✅ KHULLAR ONLY.                              ◄── THE ONLY SOURCED ROUTE
   Majid folio 4.28 lists                            TO THE PHRASE
   "1918, 1931, 1936" and                            "KÖPPEN-GEIGER".
   OMITS 1901 (C12).                                 Geiger is a CO-EDITOR of
                                                     the 1936 Handbuch, not a
                                                     co-author of the scheme.

   RAIL 2 — EVERYTHING ELSE IN THIS TOPIC, ON THE SAME CLOCK
   ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
   1889          1931  1933   1948   1953    1954   1958  1961  1971   1980
    │             │     │      │      │       │      │     │     │      │

   first       1st scheme    1948   KENDREW  "An Intro  [Khullar  [Khullar SINGH  & HORN
   Indian      (Khullar      "mark- (Majid   to climate" biblio]   biblio]  ten    (Khullar
   division    tabulates     edly   4.28)    McGraw Hill                    divi-  biblio
   (Majid      THIS one)     diff-           ✅ Khullar                      sions  folio
   4.28)                     erent"          folio 162                             167)
                                                                                     ▲
   ⇄ NOTE THE TWO LIVE CONFLICTS ALREADY VISIBLE ON RAIL 2:                         │
   · STAMP is 1953 in Majid and 1958 in Khullar's bibliography (C13) ─────────────┐ │
   · C2's Trewartha group names come from HERE, the 1980 revision, ───────────────│─┘
     not from Khullar's 1954 six-group list (C11)                                 │
```

**Caption.** Two rails on one clock. Rail 1 is the scheme; rail 2 is everything the scheme is
compared against in Lessons 10–14. Every conflict this edition refuses to resolve is already visible
as a gap between two marks on rail 2.

### ✗ C12, applied — the edition chronology, and where "Köppen-Geiger" legitimately comes from

✅ **Khullar printed folio 158 (scan PDF p.96)** states that Köppen's classification was **first
published in 1901**, that **major revisions came in 1918 and 1931**, and that his **latest work was
1936**. ✅ **Majid *GoI* folio 4.28** lists *"W. Koppen, **1918, 1931, 1936**"* — **omitting 1901**.

| Source | Köppen's dates | What it adds |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Khullar folio 158 | **1901** · 1918 · 1931 · 1936 | The institution: **University of Graz, Austria** |
| ✅ Majid *GoI* folio 4.28 | 1918 · 1931 · 1936 | Places Köppen **second of ten** in the Indian lineage |
| ✅ Khullar folio 166 (bibliography) | **1931** and **1936** only | The two titles, and the **Geiger** co-editorship |

**The correction.** Teach **1901 · 1918 · 1931 · 1936**, and say that Majid's list begins at 1918
while Khullar supplies the first date. This is not a conflict to refuse — one source is simply fuller
than the other — and it is the model for how a candidate should handle two reading lists that
disagree by omission rather than by assertion.

**And now the phrase C1 uses without a source.** C1 writes *"Köppen-Geiger"* at lines 87 and 99 with
no citation of any kind. ✅ **Khullar's bibliography at printed folio 166** supplies the honest
route, and it is worth quoting exactly as printed, errors included, because the errors are the proof
that the page was read:

> ✅ **Köppen, Wladimer** *[sic — for Wladimir]* **(1931): *Grundriss der Klimakund*** *[sic — for
> Klimakunde]*, Walter de Gruyler Company, Berlin.
>
> ✅ **— (1936): *Das Geographische System der Klimate*, Handbuch der Klimablogie** *[sic — for
> Klimatologie]*, **Ed. W. Koppen and R. Geiger, Vol. I, Berlin.**

**So: Geiger is a co-editor of the 1936 *Handbuch*, and that is the whole sourced basis for the
compound name.** ⚠️ **No local book and no retrieved official page supplies a Köppen-Geiger map
version, a resolution or a release year**, and none is stated anywhere in this file (refusal 6, B2).

### The vegetation premise, stated precisely

✅ **Khullar folio 158** gives Köppen's design in one clause: the scheme is **empirical**, built on
**annual and monthly means of temperature and precipitation**, with **native vegetation accepted as
the best expression of the totality of climate**.

Unpack it slowly, because three different claims are compressed in there.

```text
   WHAT THE PREMISE ACTUALLY ASSERTS — three claims, stacked

   CLAIM 3 ── "…the TOTALITY of climate."
      │       Vegetation integrates EVERYTHING — not just mean temperature but
      │       its seasonal march, not just rainfall total but its timing, plus
      │       evaporative demand, frost incidence and length of growing season.
      │       A plant is a multi-variable instrument that has already done the
      │       integration.                     ⚠️ This is the strongest claim and
      │                                           the one Thornthwaite attacks (L11-12)
      ├── CLAIM 2 ── "NATIVE vegetation…"
      │               The adjective concedes the limit in advance. Cropped,
      │               grazed, cleared or planted cover records LAND USE.
      │               ✅ The word is Khullar's, at folio 158.
      │
      └── CLAIM 1 ── "…EMPIRICAL, from annual and monthly MEANS."
                      The inputs are numbers from a register. No field visit,
                      no botany, no causal theory. This is what makes the scheme
                      OPERATIONAL — and what makes it causally silent (L17).
```

**Why this ordering matters for an answer.** Candidates routinely write *"Köppen is based on
vegetation"*. That is Claim 3 stated as if it were Claim 1, and it invites the examiner's obvious
follow-up: *then why does it use temperature numbers?* The accurate formulation is: **the inputs are
thresholds on temperature and precipitation; the justification for where those thresholds sit is
vegetation.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook 2 — `g`, the Ganges type: the only Köppen letter named after an Indian river

✅ **Khullar printed folio 159 (scan PDF p.96)** defines, among Köppen's small letters, **`g` — the
*Ganges type* of annual march of temperature**, in which **the hottest month comes *before* the
solstice and the summer rainy season**.

This is introduced here, in the provenance lesson, for a reason: it is the single clearest evidence
that **Köppen's scheme was built against real regional observations, not derived from a theory**. A
purely deductive classifier would have no reason to need a letter for "the temperature peak arrives
early because the rains then cool the surface". Köppen needed one because north India does it.

The full code is taught in Lesson 5 and applied in Lesson 9, where **Cwg** is the northern plain.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** `g` describes the **timing of the temperature maximum**, not
> rainfall. A candidate who reads it as a rain letter — alongside `f`, `w`, `s`, `m`, which *are*
> moisture letters — will misclassify. It sits in the same alphabet doing a different job, which is
> exactly the kind of displacement a statement question is built on.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | Köppen revised across **four** editions over 35 years; Thornthwaite across **three** (1931, 1933, 1948) over 17; Trewartha produced a **1954** scheme and a **1980** Horn revision. **Every scheme in this topic has editions, and every owner error in this topic is an edition error** |
| **Objection** | *"If the scheme was revised four times, which edition is the examinable one?"* |
| **Reply** | The one the **source in front of you prints**. ✅ Khullar folio 158's group definitions and folio 159's letters are the examinable set here, and they are stated as Khullar's. Where a modern map differs — the C/D boundary at 0 °C instead of −3 °C (C5), **Cwa** instead of **Cwg** (C16) — the edition names **both** and names the convention. **It never silently picks one** |
| **Unresolved residual** | **The content of the 1918 and 1901 editions is not in this evidence base.** Khullar gives their dates and nothing else. No statement is made anywhere in this file about what changed between them |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing "Köppen-Geiger" as though Geiger co-authored the scheme.** ✅ The only local evidence
  is a **co-editorship of the 1936 *Handbuch*** (Khullar folio 166).
- ❌ **Attaching a version, resolution or release year to a Köppen-Geiger map.** No source in this
  evidence base supplies one (refusal 6).
- ❌ **Dating Köppen to 1918 because Majid's list starts there.** ✅ Khullar folio 158 gives **1901**.
- ❌ **Saying "Köppen classified by vegetation".** He classified by **temperature and precipitation
  thresholds justified by vegetation**.
- ❌ **Dropping the word *native*.** It is in the source, and it is the scheme's own concession.
- ❌ **Reading `g` as a rainfall letter.** It is the **annual march of temperature**.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Provenance is not decoration here: the *salient features of world's physical geography*
clause is examined through **thresholds**, and a threshold without an edition is a number without a
warrant.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** This lesson supports
`Geography/basic/22_Cool-Temperate-Western-Margin-British-Type`, which owns **2024 Prelims Q13** —
*"Marine West Coast climate identified from its low annual and daily temperature range and
year-round precipitation"* (`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2024-2025.md` line 39, recorded as *"Key available
locally (official Set-A answer key present); answer not recorded here"*). **That ledger neutrality is
preserved here verbatim and no key is disclosed.** It is the nearest thing to a classification demand
in twenty-two ledgers, and note what it actually does: it describes a type by **range and
seasonality** and asks the candidate to name it. **That is Köppen's grammar being used without
Köppen's name** — which is precisely the contribution this topic makes to a topic it does not own.

**Answer use.** When a question asks for the *basis* of a classification, give the **date, the
institution and the premise** in one line before any threshold: *"Köppen, University of Graz, first
published 1901 and revised to 1936, fixed empirical thresholds on monthly and annual means of
temperature and precipitation at the values where native vegetation changes."* Two marks of context
for one sentence.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Wladimir Köppen, University of Graz, Austria** (Khullar folio 158). Name the institution; it
   costs four words.
2. ✅ **Four editions: 1901 first published · 1918 and 1931 major revisions · 1936 latest work**
   (Khullar folio 158).
3. ✗ **C12: Majid folio 4.28 gives only 1918, 1931, 1936 — 1901 comes from Khullar.** Omission, not
   contradiction.
4. ✅ **Bibliography, Khullar folio 166:** *Grundriss der Klimakund* **(1931)** and *Das Geographische
   System der Klimate*, *Handbuch der Klimablogie* **(1936)**, *"Ed. W. Koppen and **R. Geiger**,
   Vol. I, Berlin"*. **Three typographic errors in the printed entries, reproduced [sic].**
5. **"Köppen-Geiger" is sourced to a 1936 co-editorship and to nothing else.** ⚠️ No map version,
   resolution or release year exists in this evidence base.
6. ✅ **The premise, in Khullar's words:** *"native vegetation accepted as the best expression of the
   **totality** of climate"*.
7. **Three stacked claims:** inputs are **numbers**; the justification is **plants**; the warrant is
   that plants **integrate everything**. Say them in that order.
8. **The adjective *native* is the scheme's own limitation**, admitted in advance.
9. 🇮🇳 ✅ **`g` = the *Ganges type* of annual march of temperature** — hottest month **before** the
   solstice and the summer rains (Khullar folio 159). The only Köppen letter named for an Indian
   river. **It is a temperature letter, not a rain letter.**
10. **Every scheme here has editions, and every owner error in this topic is an edition error** —
    C10 (Thornthwaite 1931 vs 1948), C11 (Trewartha 1954 vs Horn 1980), C13 (Stamp 1953 vs 1958).

### Concept check

**Question:** C1 uses the term "Köppen-Geiger" twice without citing anything. Using only the local
evidence, state exactly what can be said about Geiger's involvement, and exactly what cannot.

**Model answer:** **What can be said.** ✅ Khullar's chapter bibliography at printed folio 166 lists
Köppen's **1936** work *Das Geographische System der Klimate* as appearing in the *Handbuch der
Klimablogie* **[sic]**, *"**Ed. W. Koppen and R. Geiger**, Vol. I, Berlin"*. Geiger is therefore
evidenced as a **co-editor of the 1936 handbook** in which Köppen's system was published, and that
is the sole local warrant for the compound name. ✅ Khullar folio 158 independently places 1936 as
Köppen's **latest work**, so the pairing belongs to the final edition rather than to the scheme's
origin. **What cannot be said.** That Geiger co-authored the classification — no source states it.
That there exists a "Köppen-Geiger map" of any particular version, resolution or release year — **no
local book and no retrieved official page supplies one**, which is why C1 lines 87 and 99 are demoted
under U3 and why this file states no map version anywhere. That modern high-resolution versions
"track boundary migration decade-by-decade" — that is C1's ✅-marked sentence with no dataset behind
it. The defensible formulation is: *the scheme's final edition appeared in a handbook co-edited by
Geiger, which is why the system is often named for both.*

**Misconception to avoid:** That a familiar compound name is self-authenticating. "Köppen-Geiger" is
repeated so often that it feels like a citation, but in this evidence base it resolves to a single
bibliography line about an editorship — and a candidate who attaches a dataset, a resolution or a
release year to it is inventing provenance for a phrase they have only ever heard.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 2

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Trace* the development of Köppen's classification and
explain the premise on which it rests.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** Wladimir Köppen, of the University of Graz, first published his
classification in **1901**, revised it substantially in **1918** and **1931**, and produced his final
statement in **1936** — the last appearing in the *Handbuch* co-edited with R. Geiger, which is the
sole warrant for the name "Köppen-Geiger". Majid's Indian lineage lists only 1918, 1931 and 1936,
placing Köppen second of ten authorities after Blandford's 1889 attempt. The premise is a single
move. The scheme is **empirical**: its inputs are annual and monthly means of **temperature and
precipitation**, taken from station registers. Its justification is botanical — Köppen accepted
**native vegetation as the best expression of the totality of climate** — so the thresholds were
placed where vegetation changes. The word *native* admits the limit: disturbed cover records land
use, not climate. Numbers in, plants as warrant: that is the whole design. *(146 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Chronological accuracy **3.5** · Premise statement **2.5** · Provenance
citation **2.5** · Economy of words **1.5** *(dimension weights 35 · 25 · 25 · 15)*

## Lesson 3 — The five groups and the three numbers that define them


Progress: 3 / 17 · Stage: Foundation · Visual grammar: **threshold ladder on one axis** · India hook: **the January 18 °C line**

### The intuition, before any term

Four of Köppen's five groups are **intervals on a single thermometer**. That is the entire secret of
the group level, and once it is seen the five groups stop being a list to memorise and become a
ladder to read.

Group **B** is the exception, and it is the exception in a specific way: it is not a rung at all. It
is defined by a **relation** — evaporation against precipitation — and it therefore **cuts across**
the ladder, which is why a hot desert and a cold desert are both B. Lesson 4 is entirely about that
one fact, because it is the most common source of error in the whole topic.

So: four groups on one axis, one group off it. Draw the axis.

### The primary visual — one vertical temperature axis, four groups bracketed

**Caption.** Three numbers — **18, 10 and −3** — define four groups; a fourth number, **22**, belongs
to the third letter and not to the groups at all. The fifth group is not on the axis. The sixth group
is not Köppen's.

### The groups, exactly as the source gives them

✅ **Khullar printed folio 158 (scan PDF p.96)**, reproduced with nothing added:

| Group | Köppen's name as printed | Criterion as printed |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **A** | tropical rainy climate, **no cool season** | coolest month **above 18 °C** |
| ✅ **B** | **dry** climate | **excess of evaporation over precipitation** |
| ✅ **C** | mid-latitude rainy, **mild winters** | coldest month **below 18 °C but above −3 °C**; warmest month **over 10 °C** |
| ✅ **D** | mid-latitude rainy, **severe winters** | coldest month **below −3 °C**; warmest month **above 10 °C** |
| ✅ **E** | **polar**, no warm season | warmest month **below 10 °C** |

**And the certified absence.** ✅ **H is not in Khullar's group list.** The list is five long. This is
not a gap in the book; it is the state of the original scheme, and both owners say so correctly —
C1 line 25 calls H *"added later for mountainous, undifferentiated climates"* and C2 line 14 says
**"H is a later highland addition, not one of Köppen's original five groups."** Keep both. **The
reason this matters is C14**: in Lesson 10 the edition has to show that C2 later imports H back into
Köppen's India, contradicting its own line 14.

### ⇄ C5, applied — a convention, not an error

This is the one place where **C1 is already right and the edition keeps C1's formulation unchanged**.

| Source | The C/D boundary | Status |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Khullar folio 158 | **−3 °C** for the coldest month | The book's figure, printed |
| ✅ C1 line 74 | *"Original Köppen uses **−3 °C** for the C/D boundary; many modern maps use **0 °C**. State the convention."* | **Correct, and kept verbatim as C1's formulation** |
| ⚠️ The 0 °C half | — | **No local book and no retrieved page states it.** Marked ⚠️ wherever it appears in this file |

**Why this is not an error.** −3 °C and 0 °C are not rival truths about the atmosphere; they are
rival **drawing conventions** for the same idea — *is the winter severe enough to freeze the ground
for a season?* The examiner-facing move is C1's own: **state the convention you are using**. A
candidate who writes *"coldest month below −3 °C (original Köppen; many modern maps use 0 °C)"* has
protected the answer against either map.

**The same discipline recurs twice more in this file**, and it is worth naming the pattern now:
**Cwg against Cwa** (C16, Lesson 9) and **Stamp 1953 against 1958** (C13, Lesson 13). In all three
cases the instruction is identical — **name both, name the convention, resolve nothing.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook 3 — the January 18 °C line, one number doing two jobs

✅ **Khullar folio 158** uses **18 °C** as Köppen's **A/C boundary**: above it in the coolest month,
the climate has *no cool season* and is tropical.

✅ **Khullar printed folio 156 (scan PDF p.95)** uses **the January 18 °C isotherm** as **Stamp's
dividing line** between *Temperate or Continental India* and *Tropical India* — the single structural
move of the whole Stamp scheme, taught in Lesson 13.

**The same number, in the same country, doing two different jobs.** In Köppen it is a **class
criterion** applied to the coolest month, whichever month that is. In Stamp it is a **cartographic
spine** drawn for January specifically, splitting the country in two before any region is named.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** The coincidence is **not** evidence that Stamp derived his line
> from Köppen; no local source says so, and the edition does not claim it. **18 °C is a convenient
> tropical threshold that two schemes independently found useful**, and a candidate who asserts a
> derivation is inventing a lineage. What can be said is that the number is the same and the use is
> different — which is itself the examinable observation.

### The complete causal chain for this lesson

```text
   MONTHLY MEANS (12 numbers)
        │
        ├─► pick the COOLEST month ──┬── above 18 °C ─────────────► A
        │                             ├── 18 °C to −3 °C ─────────► C   (if warmest > 10 °C)
        │                             └── below −3 °C ────────────► D   (if warmest > 10 °C)
        │
        ├─► pick the WARMEST month ─── below 10 °C ────────────────► E ──┬── T tundra
        │                                                               └── F icecap
        │
        └─► BUT FIRST: test the ARIDITY RELATION ──── if evaporation
            exceeds precipitation ────────────────────────────────► B
            ▲
            └── THIS TEST RUNS FIRST, AND IT OVERRIDES. That is why
                a place at 30 °C can be B and not A, and a place at
                −5 °C can be B and not D. Lesson 4.
```

**The assumption, stated.** That **one month** can carry a class. The coolest month decides A, C and
D; the warmest decides E. **Eleven other months are discarded at the group level** — which is
efficient, reproducible, and the reason two climatically different places can share a group letter.

**The limit, stated.** The ladder tells you nothing about **why** a place sits where it does. Lesson
17 returns to this; it is the central charge in any *"critically examine"* answer.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | Köppen's groups are **intervals on measured means**; ✅ Leong's zones (p.114) are **latitude bands** — 0–10°, 10–30°, 30–45°, 45–65°, 65–90°. Köppen's A can occur at 25° and Leong's Hot Zone is defined to be 10–30° whatever the thermometer says |
| **Objection** | *"Why should one month decide a class? It throws away eleven-twelfths of the data."* |
| **Reply** | Because the discarded months are **correlated with the kept one**, and because the kept one is the **limiting** month for vegetation — the coldest month sets what can survive the winter and the warmest sets whether there is a growing season at all. Köppen's thresholds are survival thresholds, not average-condition thresholds. ⚠️ This reasoning is standard and is not stated in these words by any local source |
| **Unresolved residual** | **Khullar's folio 158 gives no aridity formula for B** — only the words *"excess of evaporation over precipitation"*. **No local source supplies a computable aridity index**, and none is invented in Lesson 4 |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Listing H as one of Köppen's groups.** ✅ Khullar folio 158 has **five**. C1 L25 and C2 L14
  both say "later addition" and are right; C2 contradicts itself later (C14, Lesson 10).
- ❌ **Using 22 °C as a group boundary.** It splits the **third letter** `a`/`c` (Lesson 5).
- ❌ **Quoting the C/D boundary without the convention.** Say **−3 °C (original; many modern maps use
  0 °C)** — C1's own formulation (C5).
- ❌ **Testing the thermal ladder before the aridity relation.** B overrides. A hot place that is dry
  is **B**, not A.
- ❌ **Reading "no cool season" as "no winter".** It is a **statement about the coolest month's
  mean**, which is a threshold, not a season.
- ❌ **Confusing the 10 °C floor with the 18 °C ceiling.** 10 °C separates **D from E**; 18 °C
  separates **A from C**. Different months, different jobs.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The five groups are the **vocabulary** of the *salient features of world's physical
geography* clause. Every type taught in Topics 15 and 17–25 is a sub-case of one of these five
letters, which is why this lesson is foundational rather than merely definitional.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** This lesson supports
`Geography/basic/25_Arctic-or-Polar-Climate`, which owns **2021 GS-I Q15** — *"Melting Arctic ice and
Antarctic glaciers and weather patterns"*, *Explain*, 15 marks
(`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2018-2023.md` line 184) — and
`Geography/basic/15_Hot-Wet-Equatorial-Climate`, which owns **2023 Prelims Q63**, recorded in the
ledger as *"Tropical rainforest soil nutrient levels decomposition rate"* and *"Routed; key
unavailable locally"* (`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` line 666). **Neither is answered here.**
Topic 14's contribution is the **E-group and A-group criteria** that let a candidate place those
questions before reading the options.

**Answer use.** Never write a group letter without its number. *"A, coolest month above 18 °C"* is
one clause and it converts a recitation into a definition. In a 15-mark answer, the three numbers —
**18, 10, −3** — plus the aridity relation are the entire group-level content, and they should take
no more than three lines, leaving the marks for application.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **A** — tropical rainy, no cool season, **coolest month above 18 °C** (Khullar folio 158).
2. ✅ **B** — dry, **excess of evaporation over precipitation**. **Not a temperature interval.**
3. ✅ **C** — mild winters, **coldest month below 18 °C but above −3 °C**, warmest **over 10 °C**.
4. ✅ **D** — severe winters, **coldest month below −3 °C**, warmest **above 10 °C**.
5. ✅ **E** — polar, no warm season, **warmest month below 10 °C**; split by capitals **T** tundra and
   **F** icecap (folio 159).
6. ✅ **H is NOT in Khullar's group list.** Five groups, not six. C1 L25 and C2 L14 both call it a
   later addition — **keep that formulation**, and watch C2 contradict it at C14.
7. ⇄ **C5: the C/D boundary is −3 °C in Khullar; ⚠️ many modern maps use 0 °C.** **State the
   convention.** This is C1 line 74's formulation and it is kept unchanged.
8. **22 °C is not a group boundary** — it is the `a`/`c` split in the third letter (Lesson 5).
9. **Order of operations: aridity first, thermal ladder second.** B overrides.
10. **One month decides the group** — coolest for A/C/D, warmest for E. Eleven months are discarded,
    deliberately.
11. 🇮🇳 **18 °C does two jobs in India:** Köppen's **A/C class criterion** (folio 158) and **Stamp's
    January dividing line** between Temperate/Continental and Tropical India (folio 156). **Same
    number, different job — and no derivation between them is claimed.**
12. **The ladder is silent on causation.** It records where a place sits; it never says why. That
    silence is the standing charge in every evaluative answer (Lesson 17).

### Concept check

**Question:** A station's twelve monthly means run from 16 °C in January to 34 °C in May, and its
rainfall is 9 cm for the year. A candidate assigns it to group C because the coldest month is below
18 °C and above −3 °C. Identify the error in the procedure, not merely in the answer.

**Model answer:** The error is one of **order of operations**, and it is more instructive than the
wrong letter. The candidate has run the **thermal ladder first**. ✅ Khullar folio 158 defines **B**
not as an interval on that ladder but as a **relation** — *"excess of evaporation over
precipitation"* — and that relation is tested **before** the thermal criteria, because it overrides
them. A station with 9 cm of annual rainfall and a May mean of 34 °C is in deep moisture deficit; it
is **B**, and its thermal values then supply only the subordinate letters, not the group. The
candidate's thermal reading is internally correct — 16 °C does lie below 18 °C and above −3 °C — which
is exactly why the procedural error is dangerous: a right calculation applied in the wrong sequence
produces a confident wrong class. The general rule, worth more than the instance, is that **Köppen
has one group defined by a relation and four defined by intervals, and the relation is evaluated
first.** India demonstrates it: ✅ Khullar folio 160 puts **BWhw** across most of western Rajasthan
with **less than 12 cm**, where summer means are among the country's highest.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the five groups form a single ordered sequence from hot to cold with
B sitting between A and C. B is not on the thermal axis at all; it cuts across every height on it,
which is why both a Thar summer and a mid-latitude cold desert carry the same group letter.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 3

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Enumerate and defend* the numeric criteria of Köppen's
five major groups.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** Köppen's groups rest on three numbers and one relation. **A**: coolest month
above **18 °C** — no cool season. **C**: coldest month below 18 °C but above **−3 °C**, warmest above
**10 °C** — mild winters. **D**: coldest below −3 °C, warmest above 10 °C — severe winters. **E**:
warmest month below **10 °C** — no warm season, subdivided **T** tundra and **F** icecap. **B** is
not an interval but a relation: **evaporation exceeding precipitation**, and it is tested first,
overriding the thermal ladder. Each number is a **survival threshold**, not an average: 10 °C where
a growing season ceases, 18 °C where winter cold ceases to constrain, −3 °C where ground freezes for
a season. Conventions must be named — the C/D boundary is −3 °C in
Köppen and 0 °C on many modern maps. **H is a later addition, not one of the five.** *(146 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Threshold accuracy **4.0** · Group completeness **2.5** · Convention naming
**2.0** · Trap avoidance **1.5** *(dimension weights 40 · 25 · 20 · 15)*
