Progress: 4 / 17 · Stage: Foundation · Visual grammar: **balance beam** · India hook: **Jaisalmer, Barmer, Ganganagar, Jodhpur**

### The intuition, before any term

Every other Köppen group answers the question *how warm is it?* Group B answers a different question
entirely: **does the atmosphere take away more water than it delivers?**

That is a **balance**, not a level. A balance has two pans, and either pan can be loaded in several
ways. Rainfall can fall; temperature can rise and drive evaporation up; rain can arrive in one month
instead of six and run off before it is useful. **Any of those tips the beam, and the class changes
without the other pan moving at all.**

This is the single most consequential idea in the topic, and it is why Lesson 16 is possible. A
temperature-defined boundary moves only when temperature moves. **An aridity-defined boundary moves
when *either* input moves** — which is exactly what makes C1's unsourced Thar projection so tempting
and so unsafe.

### The primary visual — the beam, with S and W as positions along it

```text
   GROUP B IS A RELATION, NOT A LEVEL — ✅ Khullar printed folio 158
   "B — DRY CLIMATE: EXCESS OF EVAPORATION OVER PRECIPITATION"

                        the beam tips when the LEFT pan outweighs the right
                                        ╷
          EVAPORATIVE DEMAND            ╷            WATER DELIVERED
          (what the air can take)       ╷            (what the sky gives)
                 ║                      ╷                     ║
          ╔══════╩══════╗        ╔══════╧══════╗       ╔══════╩══════╗
          ║  temperature ║       ║   FULCRUM   ║       ║   rainfall  ║
          ║  sunshine    ║◄──────╢             ╟──────►║   total     ║
          ║  wind        ║       ║  the class  ║       ║   timing    ║
          ║  low humidity║       ║   boundary  ║       ║   intensity ║
          ╚═════════════╝        ╚═════════════╝       ╚═════════════╝
                 │                                             │
                 └──────────── IF LEFT > RIGHT ────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
            ╔═══════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
            ║   GROUP  B   —  and only THEN how far past the     ║
            ║   fulcrum you are decides the SECOND letter:       ║
            ╚═══════════════════════════════════════════════════╝
                                      │
          ┌───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┐
          ▼                                                       ▼
   ╔═════════════════╗                                  ╔═════════════════╗
   ║   S  = STEPPE   ║  just past the fulcrum           ║   W  = DESERT   ║
   ║   semi-arid     ║  ◄─────────────────────────────► ║   arid          ║
   ║ ✅ folio 159     ║      the SAME relation,          ║ ✅ folio 159     ║
   ║ 🇮🇳 BShw 12-25cm ║      further along               ║ 🇮🇳 BWhw < 12 cm ║
   ╚═════════════════╝                                  ╚═════════════════╝

   ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   AND INDEPENDENTLY OF ALL THAT, A THIRD LETTER FOR HOW HOT THE DRYNESS IS:

        h  (heiss = HOT)  ◄─── ✗ KHULLAR'S PRINTED DEFINITION IS INVERTED
        k  (kalt = COLD)  ◄─── ✗ k IS NAMED NOWHERE IN KHULLAR'S LIST (C6)

   WHY THE BEAM AND NOT A TABLE: a table would give B a row beside A and C,
   implying it is one more rung on the ladder. It is not. It is a test that
   runs ACROSS the ladder, and only a two-pan image shows that.
```

**Caption.** One relation, two positions along it, and a temperature letter bolted on afterwards that
describes the dryness but does not define it. A candidate who draws this beam will never again write
that B is "the second-coldest climate".

### ✗ C1, applied — Khullar's `h` threshold is printed upside down

This is the edition's flagship correction, and it is corrected **from inside the book** rather than
from outside authority.

**What the source says.** ✅ **Khullar printed folio 159 (scan PDF p.96)**, in the small-letter list,
prints:

> ✗ **"h (heiss) — average annual temperature *under* 18 °C."**

**Why it cannot stand.** The letter pair is **h = *heiss* = hot** and **k = *kalt* = cold**. The hot
member cannot be the one below the threshold. But the edition does not rest on the German, because it
does not have to — **Khullar contradicts himself two folios later**:

```text
   THE INTERNAL CONTRADICTION — BOTH SIDES READ FROM THE SAME CHAPTER

   ✅ FOLIO 159 SAYS:          "h (heiss) = average annual temperature UNDER 18 °C"
                                                      │
                                                      │ apply it…
                                                      ▼
   ✅ FOLIO 160 ASSIGNS:       BWhw ──► "most of WESTERN RAJASTHAN",  < 12 cm
                               BShw ──► the Thar margin and the rain-shadow
                                        Deccan,                      12-25 cm
                                                      │
                                                      ▼
                    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
                    │  THESE ARE THE HOTTEST TRACTS IN INDIA.      │
                    │  Under the folio-159 rule they would need a  │
                    │  MEAN ANNUAL TEMPERATURE BELOW 18 °C.        │
                    │  The book's own map therefore refutes the    │
                    │  book's own definition.                      │
                    └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

   ⚠️ THE CORRECTED CONVENTION, marked ⚠️ because NO LOCAL SOURCE STATES IT
      POSITIVELY:   h  ≥ 18 °C mean annual temperature   (hot / low-latitude)
                    k  <  18 °C mean annual temperature   (cold / mid-latitude)
```

**How to carry this into an answer.** Do not write *"Khullar is wrong"*. Write the structure: **the
printed definition inverts the pair, and the book's own Indian assignments prove it, because BWhw is
placed in western Rajasthan.** That sentence demonstrates source-handling, which is worth more than
the correction itself.

**And note what the edition does not do.** It does not reproduce the sentence unqualified, and it
does not quietly substitute the right rule without showing the wrong one. ✅ **Both appear, in that
order, every time.**

### ✗ C6, previewed — `k` is missing

✅ **Khullar folio 159** gives Köppen's small letters as *"a, c, f, h, m, g, s and w"*. **`k` is not
in the list and is not defined anywhere on the folio.** C1 line 34 asserts *"h/k for B"* with a ✅.

So the position is: **the pair is real, the pair is standard, and only one member of it is in the
local evidence — printed backwards.** The full treatment is Lesson 5; here it matters because
**BWk** and **BSk** are the codes that make C1 line 79's excellent sentence true, and a learner
holding only Khullar's list cannot write either of them.

> ⚠️ **BWk** (cold desert) and **BSk** (cold steppe) are standard knowledge in this file. **No local
> folio prints either code.**

### The S/W split, and what it is not

✅ **Khullar folio 159:** the capitals **S** and **W** subdivide the dry group as **steppe** and
**desert**.

| | ✅ **S — steppe, semi-arid** | ✅ **W — desert, arid** |
|---|---|---|
| Position on the beam | Just past the fulcrum | Far past it |
| India's instance (folio 160) | **BShw**, **12–25 cm** — rain-shadow of the Western Ghats, much of Rajasthan with contiguous Haryana and Gujarat | **BWhw**, **less than 12 cm** — most of western Rajasthan |
| What it is **not** | A vegetation class. Steppe grassland is the *expected* cover, not the *criterion* | A landform class. **Dunes, playas and deflation hollows are Topic 07** |

**The route-out is not optional here.** ↪ Desert **landforms** belong to
`Geography/basic/07_Arid-Desert-Landforms`; the Thar as a **region** and its **degradation** belong to
`Geography/advanced/07_Thar-Desertification` and `Geography/advanced/18_Thar-Desert-and-GIB`;
desertification as a **process** belongs to the Environment-and-Ecology owner. **Topic 14 owns the
letter and the threshold, and stops.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook 4 — Jaisalmer, Barmer, Ganganagar, Jodhpur, and 60 per cent variability

✅ **Majid *GoI* printed folio 4.28 (scan PDF p.73)**, reading values off **Fig. 4.20**, records:

```text
   RAINFALL AND ITS VARIABILITY — ✅ Majid *GoI* folio 4.28, Fig. 4.20

   ANNUAL RAINFALL                 VARIABILITY        WHERE
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   less than 20 cm  ────────────►  AROUND 60 %   ──►  BARMER · GANGANAGAR ·
                                                      JAISALMER · JODHPUR
   100 - 200 cm     ────────────►  10 - 20 %     ──►  western slopes of the
                                                      Western Ghats, Lesser
                                                      Himalayas, Shiwaliks, Tarai
   over 200 cm      ────────────►  LESS THAN 10% ──►  MAWSYNRAM and CHERRAPUNJI,
                                                      Meghalaya Plateau
   ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   ✅ THE SOURCE'S OWN GENERALISATION, quoted: "there is an INVERSE RELATIONSHIP
      between the average annual rainfall and variability in rainfall."
```

**Why this is the right hook for the dry group.** It converts aridity from a *level* into a
*reliability* problem, which is what aridity actually is for anyone living in it. A place receiving
20 cm with 60 per cent variability is not receiving 20 cm; it is receiving something between roughly
8 and 32 cm, and it does not know which until the season ends. **The beam tips differently every
year.**

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** Variability is a **rainfall** statistic. It does **not** by
> itself set a Köppen class, because the class needs the **evaporation side of the beam** too —
> which needs temperature. A candidate who argues from variability straight to a code has used one
> pan to weigh both.

### The complete causal chain for this lesson

```text
   SUBTROPICAL SUBSIDENCE / RAIN-SHADOW POSITION / DISTANCE FROM MOISTURE SOURCE
            │
            ▼
   LOW AND UNRELIABLE DELIVERY ──────────┐
            │                             │  ⚠️ and simultaneously
            │                             ▼
            │                     HIGH EVAPORATIVE DEMAND
            │                     (high temperature, clear
            │                      skies, dry air, wind)
            │                             │
            └──────────────┬──────────────┘
                           ▼
              ✅ EVAPORATION EXCEEDS PRECIPITATION  ─── folio 158
                           │
                           ▼
                      GROUP  B  ASSIGNED
                           │
            ┌──────────────┴───────────────┐
            ▼                              ▼
     how far past?                  how hot is it?
     ✅ S / W, folio 159             ⚠️ h / k  ✗ h printed inverted (C1)
            │                              │
            └──────────────┬───────────────┘
                           ▼
              🇮🇳 BShw 12-25 cm · BWhw < 12 cm  ─── folio 160
                           │
                           ▼
     USE: dryland cropping calendars, pastoral range, irrigation design,
          groundwater draft assumptions ── ALL ROUTED OUT (Economy, Env-Ecology)
```

**Assumption, stated.** That evaporation can be **inferred** rather than measured. ✅ Khullar gives
no formula at all — only the words. **The edition supplies no index**, which is a real limit: it
means that in this evidence base **B can be reasoned about but not computed.** Thornthwaite's answer
to exactly this problem is Lesson 11, and it is the strongest argument for his scheme.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen** (folio 158) tests evaporation against precipitation **qualitatively**, with no printed formula. ✅ **Thornthwaite 1931** (folio 161) makes the same test **computable** — a *P/E ratio* of monthly precipitation divided by evaporation, summed over twelve months. **Same question; one answers it with a word, the other with an index** |
| **Objection** | *"If Köppen's B has no formula in the source, is the group even operational?"* |
| **Reply** | It is operational in **practice** and under-specified in **this evidence base**. Khullar prints the **outputs** — India's BShw at 12–25 cm and BWhw below 12 cm, folio 160 — so the boundary is reproducible from the book as a **map**, even though the rule that drew it is not printed. **The honest statement is: the class is given, the computation is not.** ⚠️ The standard aridity formulations are not reproduced here because no local source carries them |
| **Unresolved residual** | **Two.** No computable aridity index exists in this evidence base; and **`k` is absent from Khullar's letter list** while **`h` is printed inverted**, so the one letter pair that distinguishes a Thar desert from a mid-latitude cold desert is, locally, half missing and half wrong |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **"B is the second-coldest climate."** ✅ C1 line 79 destroys this and is kept: B *"can be hot
  (BWh/BSh) or cold (BWk/BSk)"*.
- ❌ **Reproducing "h (heiss) = under 18 °C" from the book.** ✗ It is inverted, and ✅ Khullar's own
  BWhw in western Rajasthan proves it (C1).
- ❌ **Writing BWk or BSk as though a local source printed them.** ⚠️ Neither code appears in any
  folio read for this edition.
- ❌ **Confusing S with vegetation.** S is a **position past the aridity fulcrum**; steppe grass is
  the expected consequence, not the test.
- ❌ **Arguing from rainfall variability alone to a Köppen class.** Variability loads one pan only.
- ❌ **Importing desertification.** It is a **land-degradation process**, routed out — and the 2020
  GS-I demand that uses it is routed to Environment and Ecology (see below).
- ❌ **Treating 12 cm and 25 cm as Köppen thresholds.** ✅ They are **Khullar's Indian figures for the
  regions** (folio 160), not criteria of the scheme.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The dry group is where the *salient features of world's physical geography* clause and
the *distribution of key natural resources* clause meet: aridity is the single climatic fact that
most constrains water, cropping and settlement across the subcontinent's north-west.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** This lesson sits closest to the most
Topic-14-adjacent Mains demand in the entire window: **2020 GS-I Q5** — *"Desertification as a
process without climatic boundaries"*, *Justify with examples*, **10 marks, 150 words** — routed by
`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2018-2023.md` line 149 to
**`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/23_Desertification-UNCCD-and-Land-Degradation`**, with the ledger
note *"Routed to owning topic"*. **This edition does not answer it.** What it does is explain the
boundary, because the boundary is instructive: the demand's own premise — that desertification has
*no climatic boundaries* — is only arguable because **aridity is a relation and not a place**.
Topic 14 supplies that premise and nothing else; the process, the convention and the policy are the
other owner's. **The directive *Justify with examples* is used in Lesson 1 as a directive exemplar;
the question itself is reproduced nowhere.**

**Answer use.** In any dry-climate answer, open with the relation and not the rainfall figure: *"B is
assigned where evaporation exceeds precipitation, so a temperature rise can create a dry class
without any fall in rainfall."* That one clause earns the later paragraph on threshold mobility
(Lesson 16) and pre-empts the commonest examiner objection.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **B is a relation, not a level:** *"excess of evaporation over precipitation"* (Khullar folio
   158). **It is tested before the thermal ladder and it overrides.**
2. ✅ **C1 line 79, kept verbatim:** B *"can be hot (BWh/BSh) or cold (BWk/BSk)"*. It is **not** the
   second-coldest group.
3. ✅ **S = steppe, W = desert** (folio 159) — positions along the same relation, not different
   relations.
4. ✗ **C1: Khullar folio 159 prints "h (heiss) = average annual temperature UNDER 18 °C."** Inverted.
5. ✗ **The proof is internal:** ✅ folio 160 puts **BWhw** in *"most of western Rajasthan"* and
   **BShw** on the Thar margin — India's hottest tracts — which the printed rule forbids.
6. ⚠️ **Corrected convention: h ≥ 18 °C mean annual, k < 18 °C.** Marked ⚠️ because **no local source
   states it positively**.
7. ✗ **C6 preview: `k` appears nowhere in Khullar's letter list** *"a, c, f, h, m, g, s and w"*.
   ⚠️ **BWk** and **BSk** are standard knowledge with no local folio.
8. 🇮🇳 ✅ **India's dry pair, folio 160: BShw 12–25 cm** (rain-shadow of the Western Ghats, much of
   Rajasthan with contiguous Haryana and Gujarat); **BWhw less than 12 cm** (most of western
   Rajasthan).
9. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Majid folio 4.28:** **Barmer, Ganganagar, Jaisalmer, Jodhpur** below **20 cm** with
   variability **around 60 %**; above **200 cm** (Mawsynram, Cherrapunji) variability **under 10 %**;
   **100–200 cm** belt at **10–20 %**. ✅ *"Inverse relationship between the average annual rainfall
   and variability in rainfall."*
10. **Variability is one pan only.** It cannot assign a class without the evaporation side.
11. **Khullar prints no aridity formula.** The class is given; the computation is not. **Thornthwaite
    1931 (Lesson 11) is the scheme that answers exactly this complaint.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why a sustained rise in mean temperature can convert a Köppen A or C region
into a B region even if its rainfall total does not change, and state precisely what you may and may
not assert about India on that basis.

**Model answer:** **Why it can happen.** ✅ Khullar folio 158 defines **B** by *"excess of evaporation
over precipitation"* — a **relation between two quantities**, not a threshold on one. Warming loads
the **evaporative-demand** pan: higher temperature raises the atmosphere's capacity to take water,
and the beam can therefore tip while the delivery pan is untouched. A region previously classed by
its thermal values alone becomes B, because **the aridity test is evaluated first and overrides the
thermal ladder.** No other Köppen group has this property; a C region cannot become D unless
temperature itself crosses −3 °C. **What may be asserted about India.** That the **mechanism**
applies, and that the **margins** are where a relation-defined class is most sensitive — because near
the fulcrum a small change in either pan changes the sign. That ✅ Majid folio 4.28's inverse
relationship makes low-rainfall tracts additionally unreliable, with variability *"around 60 per
cent"* below 20 cm. **What may not be asserted.** Any direction, extent, rate or date of movement for
any named Indian boundary. C1 line 105's ✅-marked claim that the Thar's BWh belt *"is projected to
expand eastward and southward"* carries **no dataset, baseline, scenario or period**, and contradicts
C1's own lines 106 and 183–186. It is refused here, and Lesson 16 draws direction with magnitude
visibly withheld.

**Misconception to avoid:** That because the mechanism is sound the consequence is measurable from
it. A relation that *can* tip tells you nothing about whether it *has* tipped, by how much, or where.
Mechanism licenses a direction of reasoning; only a named dataset with a baseline and a scenario
licenses a quantity.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 4

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Distinguish* the basis of Köppen's dry group from that
of his thermal groups, and evaluate how the Indian evidence tests it.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** Köppen's A, C, D and E are **intervals on measured monthly means** — 18 °C,
10 °C, −3 °C. **B is a relation**: *"excess of evaporation over precipitation."* It is therefore not
a rung on the thermal ladder but a test that cuts across it, evaluated first and overriding; hence
hot **BWh/BSh** and cold **BWk/BSk** share a group letter. Within B, **S** is steppe and **W** desert
— positions along the same relation. India tests it twice. Khullar places **BWhw** in most of western
Rajasthan below **12 cm** and **BShw**, at **12–25 cm**, in the Ghats' rain-shadow and much of
Rajasthan — and those assignments refute his own printed rule that *h* means a mean annual
temperature **under** 18 °C. Majid adds that below 20 cm variability runs *"around 60 per cent"*,
against under 10 per cent above 200 cm: aridity is unreliability, not merely shortage. *(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Aridity logic **3.5** · S/W and h/k handling **2.5** · Correction handling
**2.5** · India instance **1.5** *(dimension weights 35 · 25 · 25 · 15)*

## Lesson 5 — Reading a code: the second and third letters, and what Khullar leaves out

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 5 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : C1 §3 (lines 27-34) is a four-row letter table and C1 §4 (lines
                   36-50) an eleven-row code table — the most useful pages in either
                   owner for Prelims. BUT line 34 asserts "a/b/c/d for C-D" and "h/k
                   for B" with a ✅, and the local source defines only a and c. That
                   is C6. C2 has no letter grammar at all
📕 Book evidence : Khullar printed folio 159 (scan PDF p.96) — the printed letter list
                   "a, c, f, h, m, g, s and w" with definitions for a, c, f, w, s, m
                   and g; capitals S/W for the dry group and T/F for polar; the
                   inverted h · Khullar printed folio 160 (scan PDF p.97) — the
                   Indian codes Amw, As, Aw, BShw, BWhw, Cwg, Dfc, Et, E that the
                   grammar must be able to read
🔍 Live check    : none. Letter grammar is stable definitional content
📰 CA anchor     : none in the last six months
🧭 Route-outs    : Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements (rainfall TYPES — orographic,
                   convectional, cyclonic — as mechanism) · Geography/basic/13 again
                   for the ENTIRE isoline block, C1 lines 52-67 (U10)
⚠️ Live gaps     : b, d and k are NAMED IN NEITHER the list nor the definitions at
                   folio 159. a and c ALONE DO NOT PARTITION C and D. This is stated
                   as a defect of the LOCAL EVIDENCE, not of Köppen
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 5 / 17 · Stage: Core · Visual grammar: **slot-grammar railroad** · India hook: **Amw, As and Aw in one country**

### The intuition, before any term

A Köppen code is not an abbreviation. **It is a sentence with a fixed word order**, and each slot
answers one question:

> **Slot 1 — how warm, broadly?** · **Slot 2 — when is it dry?** · **Slot 3 — how hot is the summer,
> or how hot is the dryness?**

Once a learner reads codes as sentences rather than as labels, two things happen. Unfamiliar codes
become readable — **Dfc** decodes without ever having been met. And the commonest Prelims trap
evaporates, because **`w` is obviously not "wet"**: slot 2 is the *dry-season* slot, so every letter
in it names **when the dryness falls**.

### The primary visual — the railroad diagram

A railroad diagram is the standard way to draw a grammar. It is chosen here because it does something
no table can: it **shades which letters the local source actually supplies**, making C6 visible at a
glance.

```text
   KÖPPEN CODE GRAMMAR — a three-slot railroad
   ████ = defined at ✅ Khullar folio 159    ░░░░ = ⚠️ standard knowledge, NO local definition

   START
     │
     ├─►████ A ████──┬─►████ f ████  no dry season ──────────────────┐
     │  tropical     ├─►████ m ████  MONSOON, short dry season ──────┤
     │  (cool mo.    ├─►████ w ████  dry season in WINTER ───────────┤──► (3rd letter
     │   > 18 °C)    └─►████ s ████  dry season in SUMMER ───────────┘     rarely used
     │                                                                      in A)
     │
     ├─►████ B ████──┬─►████ S ████  STEPPE  (semi-arid) ──┬─►████ h ████ ✗ INVERTED
     │  dry          └─►████ W ████  DESERT  (arid)        └─►░░░░ k ░░░░ ✗ ABSENT
     │  (evap >                                                   from folio 159
     │   precip)        🇮🇳 and India adds a FOURTH slot: BShw, BWhw — the trailing
     │                     w being the dry-WINTER letter again
     │
     ├─►████ C ████──┬─►████ f ████  no dry season ────────┬─►████ a ████ warmest
     │  warm temp.   ├─►████ w ████  dry WINTER  🇮🇳        │              month > 22 °C
     │  (cold mo.    └─►████ s ████  dry SUMMER             ├─►████ c ████ warmest
     │   18 to −3)                                          │              month < 22 °C
     │                                                      ├─►░░░░ b ░░░░ ABSENT
     ├─►████ D ████──┬─►████ f ████  🇮🇳 Dfc                 └─►░░░░ d ░░░░ ABSENT
     │  cold cont.   └─►████ w ████
     │  (cold mo.                    ✅ AND ONE LETTER FOUND NOWHERE ELSE:
     │   < −3 °C)                    ████ g ████ the GANGES TYPE of annual
     │                               march of temperature — hottest month comes
     │                               BEFORE the solstice and the summer rains.
     │                               🇮🇳 Cwg, folio 160.
     │
     └─►████ E ████──┬─►████ T ████  TUNDRA  (capital, not a small letter)
        polar        └─►████ F ████  ICECAP
        (warm mo.
         < 10 °C)       ✗ C2, Lesson 9: Khullar's Et and E carry the SAME interval

   ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
   ✗ C6 READ OFF THE DIAGRAM: of the C/D third-letter system a-b-c-d, the local
   source supplies ONLY a AND c. Those two do not partition the range: they
   split at 22 °C and leave the severity dimension (b, d) unrepresented. A
   learner holding only this list CANNOT READ Dfb, Dfd, Cfb or Csb.
```

**Caption.** Three slots, one grammar, and two shaded gaps that are gaps in the **local evidence**
rather than in Köppen. The diagram is the correction: you can see C6 without being told it.

### The letters, exactly as the source gives them

✅ **Khullar printed folio 159 (scan PDF p.96)** introduces Köppen's small letters as *"a, c, f, h,
m, g, s and w"* and defines:

| Letter | ✅ Khullar's definition, folio 159 | Slot | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ **a** | hot summer, **warmest month over 22 °C** | 3rd | Pairs with c |
| ✅ **c** | cool summer, **warmest month under 22 °C** | 3rd | Pairs with a |
| ✅ **f** | **no dry season** | 2nd | From German *feucht* |
| ✅ **w** | dry season in **winter** | 2nd | **Not "wet"** |
| ✅ **s** | dry season in **summer** | 2nd | Mediterranean |
| ✅ **m** | **monsoon**, short dry season | 2nd | 🇮🇳 India's Amw |
| ✅ **g** | **Ganges type** of annual march of temperature — the hottest month comes **before the solstice and the summer rainy season** | 3rd | 🇮🇳 The only Indian-named letter |
| ✗ **h** | *"(heiss) average annual temperature under 18 °C"* | 3rd | **Inverted — C1, Lesson 4** |
| ✅ **S / W** | capitals subdividing the **dry** group: steppe / desert | 2nd | Capitals, not small letters |
| ✅ **T / F** | capitals subdividing the **polar** group: tundra / icecap | 2nd | Capitals |
| ⚠️ **b, d, k** | **named in neither the list nor the definitions** | — | **C6** |

### ✗ C6, applied — what a half-grammar costs

C1 line 34 asserts, ✅-marked: *"Temperature letters — **h/k for B**; **a/b/c/d for C-D**."* The
assertion is standard and correct about Köppen. **It is not supported by the local source**, and the
gap has a practical consequence that is worth more than the pedantry:

```text
   WHAT A LEARNER WITH ONLY KHULLAR'S LIST CAN AND CANNOT READ

   CAN READ                              CANNOT READ
   ─────────────────────────────         ───────────────────────────────────
   Amw  = A + m + w        🇮🇳           Cfb  = ? b is undefined
   As   = A + s            🇮🇳           Dfb  = ? b is undefined
   Aw   = A + w            🇮🇳           Dfd  = ? d is undefined
   Cwg  = C + w + g        🇮🇳           Csb  = ? b is undefined
   Dfc  = D + f + c        🇮🇳           BWk  = ? k is undefined
   Et   = E + t            🇮🇳           BSk  = ? k is undefined
   BShw / BWhw             🇮🇳
   ─────────────────────────────         ───────────────────────────────────
   EVERY INDIAN CODE IS READABLE.        EVERY CODE REQUIRING b, d OR k IS NOT.

   ⚠️ The standard systems, stated as standard knowledge with no local folio:
      3rd letter for C and D ....... a (hot summer) · b (warm summer) ·
                                     c (cool short summer) · d (very cold winter)
      3rd letter for B ............. h (hot) · k (cold)
```

**The honest formulation for an answer.** *"Khullar's printed list gives a, c, f, h, m, g, s and w;
the standard C/D third-letter system is a/b/c/d and the dry-group pair is h/k, of which only h is
printed locally — and inverted."* That sentence is both accurate and demonstrably source-aware.

**Why it is not trivial.** ✅ Every one of Khullar's **nine Indian codes** at folio 160 is readable
from his own list. The missing letters only bite **outside India** — which is precisely where Topics
15 and 17–25 operate. **A candidate who learns the grammar only from the Indian application will
stall on a world-type question**, and that is the examination risk C6 actually names.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 5 — three A codes in one country, separated by *when*, not *how much*

✅ **Khullar folio 160** gives India three codes inside group A:

| Code | Decoded, slot by slot | ✅ Where, and the figure |
|---|---|---|
| **Amw** | A (coolest month > 18 °C) + **m** (monsoon, short dry season) + **w** (dry season in winter) | West coast **south of Mumbai**; **over 300 cm** |
| **As** | A + **s** (dry season in **summer**) | Coromandel coast / coastal Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra; **75–100 cm**, mostly in winter from the **retreating monsoon** |
| **Aw** | A + **w** (dry winter) | Most of the peninsular plateau barring the Coromandel and Malabar strips, northern boundary roughly the **Tropic of Cancer**; **about 75 cm** |

**Read what the slot-2 letter is doing.** All three are tropical; all three have the same group
letter; and the differences between them are **entirely about the calendar**. **As** is the
remarkable one: a tropical Indian coast whose dry season is the *high-sun period*, because its rain
arrives in the **retreating** monsoon. The letter `s` is normally a Mediterranean letter, and here it
is on the Coromandel.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** The codes separate **seasonality**, not amount. **Amw at over
> 300 cm and Aw at about 75 cm differ by more than 200 cm and share a group letter**, while Amw and
> As differ by a single slot-2 letter and by the entire timing of their water year. **A code is not a
> rainfall figure**, and a candidate who treats it as one will say that two Aw places are climatically
> alike when they may not be.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen's grammar is positional**: meaning depends on which slot a letter occupies, so `w` in slot 2 is a season and `h` in slot 3 is a temperature. ✅ **Leong's scheme (p.114) has no grammar at all** — its types are **named**, not **built** — which is why Leong's twelve types cannot be extended and Köppen's codes can |
| **Objection** | *"If the grammar is positional, why does India's dry group produce four-character codes like BShw — doesn't that break the three-slot rule?"* |
| **Reply** | It does not break it; it **fills** it. **B** takes a **capital** in slot 2 (S or W, steppe or desert) and the temperature letter in slot 3 (h or k), which leaves the *seasonality* question unanswered — so ✅ Khullar's Indian codes append the **dry-season letter w** as a fourth character: **BShw**, **BWhw**. The reading is *dry · steppe · hot · dry-winter*. **It is the grammar being used fully, not an exception to it** |
| **Unresolved residual** | **b, d and k have no local definition anywhere**, and the edition does not supply numeric definitions for them. They are named as standard knowledge and left ⚠️. Any world-type code requiring them is outside what this evidence base can certify |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Reading `w` as "wet".** ✅ Slot 2 is the **dry-season** slot: `w` = dry **winter**.
- ❌ **Reading Mediterranean `Cs` as winter-dry.** ✅ `s` = dry **summer**.
- ❌ **Reading `g` as a rain letter.** ✅ It is the **annual march of temperature** (Ganges type).
- ❌ **Treating `h` as printed.** ✗ Inverted (C1, Lesson 4).
- ❌ **Assuming a/b/c/d and h/k are in the book.** ✗ Only **a**, **c** and a broken **h** are (C6).
- ❌ **Confusing the capitals.** **S/W** subdivide **B**; **T/F** subdivide **E**. Both are capitals
  in slot 2, and they belong to different groups.
- ❌ **Inferring rainfall from a code.** Amw > 300 cm and Aw ≈ 75 cm share a group letter.
- ❌ **Importing isoline definitions to "explain" the codes.** ✗ U10 — C1 lines 52–67 are routed out
  entirely to Topic 13, which owns **2024 Prelims Q4**, **2025 Q26** and **2025 Q27**.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The letter grammar is the **transferable** part of Topic 14. It is what Topics 15 and
17–25 consume, and it is the only part of this topic that appears, unnamed, in a routed Prelims
question.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** This lesson's content is the engine behind
**2024 Prelims Q13**, routed to `Geography/basic/22_Cool-Temperate-Western-Margin-British-Type`
(`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2024-2025.md` line 39): a climate described by *"low annual and daily
temperature range and year-round precipitation"* is a slot-2 **`f`** statement and a slot-1 **`C`**
statement, written out in words. **The ledger records *"Key available locally (official Set-A answer
key present); answer not recorded here"* and that neutrality is preserved — no key, option letter or
elimination chain appears here.** The point for Topic 14 is that the grammar lets a candidate
*translate* the stem before approaching the options at all.

**Answer use.** Decode out loud in the answer. Writing *"Cwg — warm temperate, dry winter, Ganges-type
annual march of temperature"* instead of *"Cwg — north Indian plains"* converts a recalled label into
demonstrated understanding, and it costs six words.

### Revision points

1. **A code is a three-slot sentence:** broad warmth · when it is dry · how hot the summer or the
   dryness is.
2. ✅ **Khullar folio 159's printed list: "a, c, f, h, m, g, s and w."** Memorise the list *and* the
   fact that it is incomplete.
3. ✅ **a = warmest month over 22 °C; c = warmest month under 22 °C.**
4. ✅ **f = no dry season · w = dry winter · s = dry summer · m = monsoon, short dry season.**
5. ✅ **g = the Ganges type of annual march of temperature** — hottest month **before** the solstice
   and the summer rains. **A temperature letter, not a rain letter.**
6. ✅ **Capitals S/W subdivide B** (steppe/desert); ✅ **capitals T/F subdivide E** (tundra/icecap).
7. ✗ **C6: b, d and k are named in neither the list nor the definitions.** ⚠️ The standard systems
   are a/b/c/d for C–D and h/k for B.
8. **a and c alone do not partition C and D** — they split at 22 °C and leave severity unexpressed.
   **Dfb, Dfd, Cfb, Csb, BWk and BSk are unreadable from Khullar's list.**
9. **Every one of India's nine codes is readable from Khullar's list.** The gap bites outside India —
   exactly where Topics 15 and 17–25 operate.
10. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Amw** — west coast south of Mumbai, **over 300 cm**.
11. 🇮🇳 ✅ **As** — Coromandel and coastal Tamil Nadu with adjoining Andhra, **75–100 cm**, mostly
    winter rain from the **retreating monsoon**. `s` on an Indian coast.
12. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Aw** — most of the peninsular plateau barring the Coromandel and Malabar strips, northern
    boundary roughly the **Tropic of Cancer**, **about 75 cm**.
13. **A code is not a rainfall figure.** Amw and Aw share a group letter across a 200 cm gap.

### Concept check

**Question:** Decode **BShw** and **Cwg** slot by slot using only the letter definitions printed at
Khullar folio 159, and state explicitly at which point the printed definitions fail you.

**Model answer:** **BShw.** Slot 1 **B** — ✅ dry: *"excess of evaporation over precipitation"*
(folio 158). Slot 2 **S** — ✅ the capital subdividing the dry group as **steppe**, semi-arid rather
than desert (folio 159). Slot 3 **h** — **here the printed definition fails.** ✗ Folio 159 prints
*"h (heiss) — average annual temperature under 18 °C"*, which is inverted; *heiss* is **hot**, and
✅ folio 160 assigns **BShw** to the Thar margin and the Ghats' rain-shadow, so the letter must mean
**hot**, ⚠️ at or above 18 °C mean annual. Slot 4 **w** — ✅ dry season in **winter**. Reading:
*semi-arid steppe, hot, with a dry winter*; ✅ **12–25 cm**. **Cwg.** Slot 1 **C** — ✅ coldest month
below 18 °C but above −3 °C, warmest above 10 °C. Slot 2 **w** — ✅ dry **winter**. Slot 3 **g** — ✅
the **Ganges type of annual march of temperature**, the hottest month arriving **before** the
solstice and the summer rains. Reading: *warm temperate, dry winter, with an early thermal maximum*;
✅ most of the Ganga Plain, summer to **40 °C**. **The second failure** is latent: nothing in the
printed list would let me read **Dfc**'s `c`… which in fact it would, at *"warmest month under
22 °C"* — but **b, d and k are absent entirely**, so any code using them is undecodable from this
source.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a code can be decoded by analogy with English. `w` is not *wet*,
`s` is not *snow*, `m` is not *maritime*, and `h` is not *humid*. Every letter in slot 2 names a
**dry season**, and every letter in slot 3 names a **temperature condition** — which is why the slot
matters more than the letter.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 5

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Decode* the structure of a Köppen code and assess the
completeness of the letter system as printed in your source.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** A Köppen code is positional. Slot 1 is the **group** — A, B, C, D or E. Slot
2 names **when the dryness falls**: **f** no dry season, **w** dry winter, **s** dry summer, **m**
monsoon with a short dry season; for **B** it becomes a capital, **S** steppe or **W** desert, and
for **E** a capital **T** tundra or **F** icecap. Slot 3 is **temperature**: **a** warmest month over
22 °C, **c** under 22 °C, and for India uniquely **g**, the *Ganges type* annual march in which the
hottest month precedes the solstice and the rains. The printed system is **incomplete**: Khullar
lists *"a, c, f, h, m, g, s and w"*, defining neither **b**, **d** nor **k**, and printing **h**
inverted. Every Indian code remains readable — Amw, As, Aw, BShw, BWhw, Cwg, Dfc, Et — but no code
requiring b, d or k is. *(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Slot-by-slot reading **4.0** · Grounded/ungrounded split **2.5** · `g`
explanation **2.0** · Worked example **1.5** *(dimension weights 40 · 25 · 20 · 15)*

## Lesson 6 — The data underneath the map: climatological normals, 1991–2020

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 6 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : NEITHER OWNER MENTIONS A CLIMATOLOGICAL NORMAL. C1's §9.3 "date/
                   data control" clause at lines 241-244 demands "dataset, base
                   period, resolution and issuing agency" for any revised map — and
                   C1 then breaks its own rule four times (U3-U7). This lesson is
                   routed IN by the Topic 13 freeze, line 1278: Topic 13 owns the
                   NORMAL, Topic 14 owns the CLASS BUILT FROM IT
📕 Book evidence : none. No local book discusses normals, reference periods or station
                   networks. THIS LESSON IS BUILT ENTIRELY ON A PRIMARY OFFICIAL
                   DOCUMENT, and that is stated rather than disguised
🔍 Live check    : IMD, *Climatological Tables of Observatories in India 1991-2020*,
                   NINTH EDITION (2022), 844 pp, SHA-256 09D721EF…A544204, retrieved
                   2026-10-04 (HTTP 200) — FRONT MATTER PDF 1-9 RE-READ FROM THE
                   DOCUMENT AT GENERATION TIME · dsp.imdpune.gov.in/home_normals.php
                   HTTP 200, 433 station options RE-COUNTED at generation time ·
                   imdpune.gov.in/library/publicationnew.html HTTP 200, the 416 /
                   1981-2010 description RE-EXTRACTED verbatim
📰 CA anchor     : the publication itself. It is a STANDARD, not an event, and is
                   dated to its edition and its retrieval, not to a bulletin
🧭 Route-outs    : Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements (instruments, station networks,
                   AWS, Doppler, nowcasting — the OBSERVATION half)
⚠️ Live gaps     : THREE OFFICIAL SURFACES GIVE THREE STATION COUNTS — 405, 416, 433.
                   All three are printed side by side with their URLs. NO ARITHMETIC
                   IS PERFORMED BETWEEN THEM · NO STATION-LEVEL NORMAL IS QUOTED
                   ANYWHERE, because no station page was re-read for a value
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 6 / 17 · Stage: Core · Visual grammar: **provenance chain** · India hook: **405 observatories, 1991–2020**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 3 said that **A** means *coolest month above 18 °C*. Ask the obvious question: **which
coolest month?** Last year's? The coldest on record? The average of how many?

**The answer is an institutional decision, and it is the hidden premise of every map in this topic.**
A Köppen class is a threshold test applied to a **normal** — an arithmetic mean over a declared run
of years, published by a named agency, updated on a stated cycle. Change the run of years and the
class can change while the sky does not.

Neither owner mentions this once. The Topic 13 freeze routes it here explicitly: **Topic 13 owns the
normal as a measurement; Topic 14 owns the class built from it.** So this lesson is where the maps
acquire a floor.

### The primary visual — the provenance chain, observatory to map

```text
   WHERE A CLIMATE CLASS ACTUALLY COMES FROM
   📄 IMD, Climatological Tables of Observatories in India 1991-2020,
      Ninth Edition (2022), 844 pp — front matter re-read at generation time

   ① THE OBSERVATORY
      405 of them. 📄 Preface, PDF p.5: "The present publication includes
      climatological normals for the period 1991–2020 for 405 Meteorological
      observatories (INCLUDING WMO CENTENNIAL STATIONS) of India
      Meteorological Department."
            │
            ▼
   ② THE READING — taken at fixed hours, every day
      📄 PDF p.9: "Observation at 0300 UTC (0830 IST)" and
                  "Observation at 1200 UTC (1730 IST)"
      Per month and annually: station pressure (hPa) · dry and wet bulb (°C) ·
      daily max and min (°C) · highest and lowest in the month · highest and
      lowest EVER with date and year · relative humidity (%) · vapour pressure
      (hPa) · rainfall (mm) · mean wind speed (kmph)
            │
            ▼
   ③ THE ARITHMETIC — thirty years, averaged
      📄 Preface, PDF p.5: "The WMO defines climatological normals as
      ARITHMETIC AVERAGES of the climatological data computed for consecutive
      period of THIRTY YEARS."
            │
            ▼
   ④ THE PUBLISHED NORMAL — and the cycle it sits in
      📄 Preface: "Climatological normals are UPDATED EVERY DECADE to keep up
      with any changes in climate that might have taken place during the period."
      📄 THE SERIES: first normals 1904, 171 stations · fourth publication
      1931-60, 235 stations · present publication 1991-2020, 405 observatories,
      NINTH EDITION (2022), issued July 2022
            │
            ▼
   ⑤ THE THRESHOLD TEST ─── Lesson 3's three numbers applied to ④, not to ②
            │
            ▼
   ⑥ THE CLASS ─────────── Amw · BShw · Cwg · Dfc · Et   (Lessons 7-9)
            │
            ▼
   ⑦ THE MAP ──────────── Khullar FIG. 5.28 / 5.30 / 5.31 (Lessons 9-14)
            │
            ▼
   ⑧ THE USE ──────────── crop zoning, forest typing, irrigation design,
                          hazard planning  (Lesson 15 — and mostly ROUTED OUT)

   ◄──────────── LESSON 16 IS THIS CHAIN READ BACKWARDS ────────────►
   If ④ moves, ⑥ moves, and every decision taken at ⑧ on the old ⑥ is built
   on an invalidated baseline — WITHOUT ANYONE HAVING CHANGED THE SCHEME.
```

**Caption.** Eight stages, and a classification enters only at stage five. Seven-eighths of a climate
map is measurement, arithmetic and institutional decision — which is why *"state the dataset, base
period and issuing agency"* is not pedantry but the whole epistemology of the topic.

### The two caveats the source states about itself

These are worth more than any figure, because they are the **document's own limits in its own
words** — 📄 **Acknowledgements, PDF p.7**, signed Krishnanand Hosalikar, Head, Climate Research and
Services, Pune, July 2022:

> 📄 *"The frequencies of weather phenomena included in the Tables have been compiled from daily
> weather reports, which, are based on **visual observations and have an element of subjectivity**."*
>
> 📄 *"Climatological Normals for some elements are derived from all available records **which may be
> less than 30 years**."*

**Read the second one twice.** The WMO definition on page 5 says *thirty years*; page 7 says *some
elements may rest on less*. **The publication is internally honest about the gap between its standard
and its data**, and that honesty is the model for how a Mains answer should treat its own evidence.

The same page also records that *"**all available data from the starting of the observatory to the
end of 2020** have been considered while preparing extremes of maximum temperature, minimum
temperature and rainfall in 24 hours"* — so **extremes** and **normals** in this publication rest on
**different spans**, which is exactly the kind of distinction a careless answer collapses.

### ⇄ The disclosure that defines this lesson — three official surfaces, three counts

This is the hardest discipline in the edition, and it is executed exactly as frozen: **all three
printed side by side, with their URLs and the access date, and nothing computed between them.**

| Official IMD surface | What it says about the station count | Reference period it attaches | Accessed |
|---|---|---|---|
| 📄 **The publication itself** — *Climatological Tables of Observatories in India 1991-2020*, Ninth Edition (2022), Preface PDF p.5 and Acknowledgements PDF p.7 | **405** meteorological observatories, *"including WMO centennial stations"* | **1991–2020** | Downloaded 2026-10-04; **front matter re-read at generation time** |
| 📄 **`https://www.imdpune.gov.in/library/publicationnew.html`** — "IMD Publications in Brief" | *"The present publication is based on data of the **416** stations from all over India for the period **1981-2010**."* — printed **under the heading "Climatological Normals: (1991 - 2020)"**, while the same page lists *(1981 - 2010)* among *"previous editions"* | **1981–2010**, under a **1991–2020** heading | 2026-10-04, HTTP 200, 17,052 B; **re-extracted verbatim at generation time** |
| 📄 **`https://dsp.imdpune.gov.in/home_normals.php`** — IMD Data Service Portal, *"Station Climatological Normals — Climatological Tables (1991-2020)"* | **433** five-digit station options in the selector | **1991–2020** | 2026-10-04, HTTP 200, 34,468 B; **433 re-counted programmatically at generation time, 433 unique** |

**The rule this edition follows, stated so that it can be checked.**

1. The **publication figure is the controlling datum**: **405 observatories, 1991–2020, ninth
   edition, July 2022**. A publication with a preface, a signatory and a date outranks a portal
   widget and a page of marketing copy.
2. The other two surfaces are **named with their numbers, their URLs and the access date**.
3. **No difference is computed, subtracted, ratioed, trended or explained.** 433 minus 405 is not
   written anywhere in this file, and no sentence anywhere suggests what the gap means.

**Why the refusal is the right answer and not a dodge.** Each surface could legitimately be counting
a different thing — a published table, a downloadable series, a selector option — and **nothing in
the retrieved evidence says which**. To subtract them is to assert that they count the same objects.
**That assertion is the fabrication**, not the numbers.

📄 The same publications page also records the previous editions by name: **(1931-1960) · (1951-1980)
· (1961-1990) · (1971-2000) · (1981-2010)** — which independently corroborates the Preface's
"updated every decade" and gives the series its shape.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 6 — 405 observatories, and the history behind them

📄 **Preface, PDF p.5**, signed Mrutyunjay Mohapatra, Director General of Meteorology, New Delhi,
July 2022:

```text
   THE SERIES, AS THE PUBLICATION STATES IT

   1904 ──── FIRST climatological normals ............ 171 stations
     │
     │       📄 "Since then, it has been published after every ten years,
     │           incorporating updates in climatological elements and parameters."
     │
   1931-60 ─ FOURTH publication in the series ........ 235 stations
     │
     │       ⋮  (fifth through eighth editions: the publications page names the
     │           periods 1951-1980 · 1961-1990 · 1971-2000 · 1981-2010)
     │
   1991-2020 PRESENT publication, NINTH EDITION (2022) 405 observatories
                                                        including WMO
                                                        CENTENNIAL STATIONS
```

**Why this is the hook and not a footnote.** It is the only place in the entire topic where an Indian
institution, a date, a signatory and a methodology appear together. **Blandford, in 1889, worked from
"a few selected stations" and was criticised for it on the very folio that introduces him** (Lesson
1). The 1904 normals rested on **171**. The current ones rest on **405**. The topic's opening
criticism and its data layer are the same story, 133 years apart.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** The publication's own Acknowledgements concede that **some
> elements are derived from records shorter than thirty years**, and that **phenomenon frequencies
> rest on visual observations with "an element of subjectivity"**. A denser network is not a perfect
> one. And **no station-level value from this document is quoted anywhere in this file** — the front
> matter was re-read; **no station page was**.

### The complete causal chain, and the assumption it rests on

**Assumption, stated.** That **thirty years of arithmetic mean is a stable description of a place**.
📄 The Preface itself undercuts the assumption in the act of stating it: normals are updated every
decade *"to keep up with any changes in climate that might have taken place during the period"*.
**If nothing changed, no update would be needed.** The reference period is therefore not a window on
a fixed object; it is a **moving description of a moving object**, and that is the whole content of
Lesson 16.

**Limit, stated.** 📄 **No retrieved IMD surface classifies India into climatic zones.** The normals
publication tabulates **stations**; the Hazard Atlas (Lesson 15) maps **hazards**. **There is no
"IMD Köppen map" in this evidence base and none is implied anywhere in this file** (refusal 9).

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Blandford, 1889**: *"a few selected stations"*, judged *"almost an overgeneralisation"* (Majid 4.28). 📄 **IMD, 1904**: 171 stations. 📄 **IMD, 1991–2020**: 405 observatories with a published methodology. **The same task, three densities, 133 years** |
| **Objection** | *"Does the number of stations matter to a classification, or only to a forecast?"* |
| **Reply** | It matters most exactly where this topic is weakest — at **boundaries**. A class boundary is drawn by interpolating between stations, so in a sparse network the line is largely an artefact of where the observatories happen to be. ⚠️ This is standard reasoning and no retrieved source states it; it is marked accordingly. What **is** sourced is that the density has changed by a factor of more than two since 1904 — so **a boundary drawn on 171 stations and a boundary drawn on 405 are not the same kind of object** |
| **Unresolved residual** | **The 405 / 416 / 433 divergence is left open.** No source explains it, and the edition does not. It is reported, attributed and dated, and **no arithmetic is performed** |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Quoting a station normal.** None appears in this file. 📄 The front matter was re-read at
  generation time; **no station page was**, and no value may be traceable to this edition.
- ❌ **Subtracting one station count from another, or explaining the gap.** Refused (refusal 4).
- ❌ **Reading the contents line *"Climatological Tables 1–405"* as a station count.** 📄 **It is a
  page range** (PDF p.4) that happens to coincide with the station count. The two are different
  objects and the coincidence is noted precisely so it is not mistaken for evidence.
- ❌ **Saying IMD classifies India's climate.** It does not, in anything retrieved (refusal 9).
- ❌ **Treating normals and extremes as resting on the same span.** 📄 Extremes use *"all available
  data from the starting of the observatory to the end of 2020"*; normals use 1991–2020.
- ❌ **Predicting the next normals period or its station count.** Refused.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Normals are routed into Topic 14 by the Topic 13 freeze as the **classification
input**. They belong to the *salient features of world's physical geography* clause in the specific
sense that every "salient feature" in this topic is a **statistic with a base period**.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The observational half of this material belongs
to `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements`, which owns **2024 Prelims Q4** (*"Inferences from January
isothermal maps"*), **2025 Prelims Q26** (*"Atmospheric dust distribution across climatic zones"*)
and **2025 Prelims Q27** (*"January isotherms bending over land and ocean"*) at
`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2024-2025.md` lines 30, 152 and 153. **All three ledger rows record *"Key
available locally (official Set-A answer key present); answer not recorded here"*, and that wording
is preserved. No key is disclosed.** Topic 14's contribution is downstream: an isotherm is a Topic 13
object until it is used as a **class boundary**, at which point it becomes Stamp's January 18 °C line
(Lesson 13).

**Answer use.** The single highest-yield sentence in this lesson is a provenance sentence. *"India's
climatic classifications rest on IMD's climatological normals — arithmetic thirty-year means, updated
decadally, the current series covering 1991–2020 across 405 observatories in the ninth edition of
2022."* It dates the data, names the agency, states the method and gives a figure, in one line.

### Revision points

1. **A Köppen class is a threshold test applied to a *normal*, not to a reading.** Neither owner says
   this; the Topic 13 freeze routes it here.
2. 📄 **WMO definition, IMD Preface PDF p.5:** normals are *"arithmetic averages of the climatological
   data computed for consecutive period of **thirty years**"*.
3. 📄 **Normals are *"updated every decade"*** — which concedes that the thing described moves.
4. 📄 **The series:** first normals **1904**, **171** stations · fourth publication **1931-60**,
   **235** stations · present publication **1991–2020**, **405** observatories, **Ninth Edition
   (2022)**, issued **July 2022**.
5. 📄 **Signatories:** Preface by **Mrutyunjay Mohapatra**, Director General of Meteorology;
   Acknowledgements by **Krishnanand Hosalikar**, Head, Climate Research and Services, Pune.
   Compilation by the **National Data Centre**, Pune-411005.
6. 📄 **Observation hours: 0300 UTC (0830 IST) and 1200 UTC (1730 IST).**
7. 📄 **Caveat 1:** phenomenon frequencies come from daily weather reports *"based on **visual
   observations and have an element of subjectivity**"*.
8. 📄 **Caveat 2:** normals for some elements come from records *"**which may be less than 30
   years**"*.
9. 📄 **Extremes use a different span** — *"all available data from the starting of the observatory to
   the end of 2020"* — not 1991–2020.
10. ⇄ **THREE OFFICIAL SURFACES, THREE COUNTS: publication 405 · library page 416 (described against
    1981-2010, under a 1991-2020 heading) · data portal 433 selector options.** All dated
    **2026-10-04**. **The publication figure controls; no arithmetic is performed between them.**
11. ✗ **The contents line "Climatological Tables 1–405" is a PAGE RANGE**, not a station count.
12. 📄 **No retrieved IMD surface classifies India into climatic zones.** The tables list stations;
    the Hazard Atlas maps hazards.

### Concept check

**Question:** An answer states: "IMD's climate normals show that India has 433 climate stations and
that its climate map is based on the 1981–2010 period." Correct it, and state the discipline that the
correction illustrates.

**Model answer:** **Three errors, each with its own source.** First, **433 is not a count of
stations in the publication**; it is the number of **five-digit station options exposed by the IMD
Data Service Portal selector** at `dsp.imdpune.gov.in/home_normals.php`, counted on 2026-10-04. The
**publication** — 📄 *Climatological Tables of Observatories in India 1991-2020*, Ninth Edition
(2022), Preface PDF p.5 — states **405 Meteorological observatories, including WMO centennial
stations**, and it is the controlling figure because it carries an edition, a date and a signatory.
Second, **the period is 1991–2020**, not 1981–2010; the 1981–2010 description survives in the library
page's body text *beneath a heading that already reads "Climatological Normals: (1991 - 2020)"*, and
the same page lists 1981-2010 among **previous** editions. Third, **IMD does not publish a climate
map of India at all** in anything retrieved: the tables list stations and the Hazard Atlas maps
hazards, so no classification can be attributed to the agency. **The discipline** is that every
figure must carry its **surface, its period and its access date** — and that where official surfaces
diverge, all are reported and **none is reconciled by arithmetic**, because no retrieved source says
the three are counting the same objects.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a number found on an official domain is thereby an official figure
for the thing you want it for. A selector widget, a publication preface and a page of descriptive
copy are three different kinds of statement, and the first duty is to say which kind you are quoting.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 6

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Assess* the dependence of climatic classification on
the climatological normal, with reference to Indian data.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** Every Köppen threshold tests a **normal**, not a reading — so a class is
only as stable as its reference period. IMD's ninth edition (2022) publishes normals for **1991–2020**
across **405 observatories**, following WMO's definition of **arithmetic averages over thirty
consecutive years**, with readings at **0830 and 1730 IST**, and states that normals are *"updated
every decade"* — an admission that the described object moves. The series shows the dependence
historically: **171** stations in **1904**, **235** for **1931–60**, 405 now. Density matters most at
boundaries, which are interpolations between stations. The data are also self-limiting: IMD records
that some elements rest on records *"which may be less than 30 years"* and that phenomenon
frequencies carry *"an element of subjectivity"*. Official surfaces even diverge on the count — 405
in the publication, 416 and 433 on two portals, all accessed 2026-10-04 — and the divergence is
reported, not resolved. *(149 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Normal definition **3.0** · Provenance chain **2.5** · Divergence disclosure
**2.5** · Volatility discipline **2.0** *(dimension weights 30 · 25 · 25 · 20)*

| Material | Owner, by exact path |
|---|---|
| Observation instruments, station networks, AWS, Doppler, nowcasting | `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` |
| Isotherms and isohyets as **constructed isolines** | `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` |
| Forecasting institutions, bulletins and warning dissemination | `Geography/advanced/13_India-JetStream-Western-Disturbances`; `Disaster-Management` owners |

Progress: 7 / 17 · Stage: Core · Visual grammar: **west–east transect profile** · India hook: **Malabar against Coromandel**

### The intuition, before any term

India's tropical belt is not organised by latitude. It is organised by **which side of a mountain you
are on, and when the rain arrives**.

That sentence is the whole lesson, and it is why the visual is a **profile** rather than a map. Walk
east from the Arabian Sea at roughly 13° N and you cross, in a few hundred kilometres, a place
receiving **over 300 cm** and a place receiving **about 75 cm**. Neither has moved in latitude.
Köppen records the difference faithfully — and explains none of it, which is Lesson 17's charge.

### The primary visual — the transect, with codes beneath and rainfall above

**Caption.** One line of travel, four Köppen codes, and a rainfall range from over 300 cm to 12 cm.
The scheme records every transition correctly and attributes none of them — which is precisely the
balance an evaluative answer must strike.

### The three A regions, exactly as the source gives them

✅ **Khullar printed folio 160 (scan PDF p.97)**, regions 1 to 3 of nine:

| # | ✅ Code | ✅ Köppen's type as printed | ✅ Extent as printed | ✅ Figure |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Amw** | monsoon with a **short dry winter** | **west coast south of Mumbai** | **over 300 cm** |
| 2 | **As** | monsoon with a dry season in the **high-sun period** | **Coromandel coast / coastal Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra** | **75–100 cm**, mostly in winter from the **retreating monsoon** |
| 3 | **Aw** | **tropical savanna** | most of the **peninsular plateau** barring the Coromandel and Malabar strips; northern boundary roughly the **Tropic of Cancer** | **about 75 cm** |

**Note what the extents are doing.** Region 1 is bounded by a **city** (south of Mumbai); region 3 by
a **line of latitude used as a rough boundary** (the Tropic of Cancer); region 2 by a **coastal
strip**. Three different kinds of boundary object in three consecutive rows — which is a good early
warning that a climatic region map is a **drawing convention**, not a survey.

### ✗ The owner gap, stated — what C2 leaves out of these three rows

| | C2's row (lines 22–24) | ✅ Khullar folio 160 | What is lost |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Amw** | *"West coast and other very wet monsoon pockets"* | **west coast south of Mumbai, over 300 cm** | **The figure, and the latitude limit.** "Other very wet monsoon pockets" is not in the source |
| **As** | *"Coromandel / Tamil Nadu coast; winter rain from NE monsoon"* | Coromandel coast / coastal Tamil Nadu **and adjoining Andhra**, **75–100 cm** | **The figure, and Andhra** |
| **Aw** | *"Most of peninsular plateau/interior"* | most of the plateau **barring the Coromandel and Malabar strips**, northern boundary **≈ Tropic of Cancer**, **about 75 cm** | **The figure, the exclusions and the northern limit** |

**C2 is not wrong here; it is empty.** 870 words cannot carry nine regions with figures, which is
exactly why this edition reconstructs them from the folio rather than integrating the owner.

**And one thing C2 gets right, kept verbatim.** C2 line 57: *"🔑 Trap: West Coast is **Amw**, not
**Aw**; Aw is mainly peninsular interior savanna/dry-winter climate."* ✅ Khullar folio 160 supports
it exactly, and it is one of the few C2 lines this edition can use as written.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 7 — Malabar against Coromandel: opposite coasts, same latitudes

Two Indian coasts at broadly the same latitudes, under two different schemes, in two different
sources — and both sources tell the same story:

| | ✅ **Köppen** (Khullar folio 160) | ✅ **Stamp and Kendrew** (Majid *GoI* folio 4.33) |
|---|---|---|
| **West coast** | **Amw**, **over 300 cm** | **Malabar Coast** — Goa to Cape Comorin (Kanniyakumari); **over 250 cm**; mean annual temperature about **27 °C** with an annual range of **3 °C**; **Kochi** typical |
| **North of it** | — | **The Konkan Coast** — mouth of the Tapi to Goa; **more than 200 cm**, over **90 per cent** from the Arabian stream; mean January about **24 °C**, mean July about **27 °C**; annual range **3–6 °C**, increasing south to north |
| **East coast** | **As**, **75–100 cm**, mostly winter rain | **The Tamil Nadu Coast** — greater parts of Tamil Nadu and the Coromandel Coast; **100–150 cm**; most during the **retreating monsoon (October to December)**; January about **24 °C**, July about **30 °C** |

**The convergence is the evidence.** Two schemes, two books, two different criteria — and both place
the Malabar well above 250 cm and the Coromandel in the low hundreds with its rain in the wrong half
of the year. **A candidate can therefore cite the contrast under either scheme and be correct**, which
is rare in this topic and worth exploiting in an answer.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** The contrast is produced by the **monsoon's direction** — the
> Arabian stream striking a windward wall, the Bay stream arriving on the retreat — and the monsoon
> mechanism is **Topic 16's**, not Topic 14's. **The classification records the outcome; it does not
> explain it.** Note also that the two schemes' figures for the east coast differ (**75–100 cm** in
> Khullar's Köppen against **100–150 cm** in Majid's Stamp-and-Kendrew) because **they are bounding
> different regions**, not because either is wrong — a point worth making explicitly rather than
> averaging them.

### The complete causal chain for the A regions

```text
   ARABIAN SEA MOISTURE STREAM
            │
            ├──► FORCED ASCENT at the Western Ghats wall
            │         │
            │         ▼
            │    HEAVY WINDWARD DELIVERY, CONCENTRATED IN THE SW MONSOON
            │         │
            │         ├──► coolest month stays above 18 °C ──► GROUP A
            │         ├──► a SHORT dry season only ─────────► letter m
            │         └──► that dry season is in WINTER ────► letter w
            │                            │
            │                            ▼
            │                 ✅ Amw, over 300 cm, west coast south of Mumbai
            │
            └──► DESCENT east of the crest ──► Lesson 8 (BShw)

   BAY OF BENGAL STREAM, ON THE RETREAT (Oct-Dec)
            │
            ▼
   DELIVERY TO THE COROMANDEL IN THE LOW-SUN HALF OF THE YEAR
            │
            ├──► coolest month above 18 °C ───────────────► GROUP A
            └──► the DRY season falls in the HIGH-SUN period ► letter s
                                 │
                                 ▼
                      ✅ As, 75-100 cm, mostly winter rain

   PLATEAU INTERIOR, between the two
            │
            ├──► monsoon-concentrated rain, clear dry winter ► GROUP A + w
            └──► about 75 cm ──────────────────────────────► ✅ Aw, savanna

   ⚠️ ASSUMPTION: that one summer's concentration can stand for the year.
   ⚠️ LIMIT: NONE of the three arrows above is explained by Köppen. The scheme
      consumes the outcome. ↪ The monsoon mechanism is Topic 16's.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen** gives the west coast one code (**Amw**) for the whole strip south of Mumbai. ✅ **Stamp** (Majid 4.33) splits the same strip into **two regions at Goa** — Konkan at over 200 cm, Malabar at over 250 cm — on amount and annual range. **One scheme sees one place; the other sees two.** Lesson 13 shows why |
| **Objection** | *"If As is 'dry in the high-sun period', is it not simply a Mediterranean climate on an Indian coast?"* |
| **Reply** | No, and the reason is the group letter. ✅ **As is group A** — coolest month **above 18 °C** — whereas a Mediterranean **Cs** has a coldest month **below 18 °C**. The slot-2 letter is the same; the slot-1 letter is not. **The seasonality rhymes; the thermal regime does not.** This is the cleanest available demonstration that a Köppen code must be read slot by slot and never as a word |
| **Unresolved residual** | ✗ **Majid's Köppen folios 4.29–4.30 are absent from the local extract.** C2 claims Majid as a source for the Köppen-India table (U2) and that claim **cannot be verified locally**. Nothing is quoted from those folios anywhere in this file, and the cross-evidence used above is from folio **4.33**, which is **Stamp-and-Kendrew**, a different scheme |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Calling the west coast Aw.** ✅ It is **Amw** (C2 line 57 is right; Khullar folio 160 confirms).
- ❌ **Reading As as a Mediterranean type.** ✅ It is **group A**; Mediterranean is **Cs**.
- ❌ **Giving Amw no figure.** ✅ **Over 300 cm**, and *south of Mumbai*.
- ❌ **Forgetting Andhra in As.** ✅ The source says *"coastal Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra"*.
- ❌ **Mapping Aw over the whole peninsula.** ✅ It excludes the **Coromandel and Malabar strips**.
- ❌ **Treating the Tropic of Cancer as a sharp Köppen boundary.** ✅ The source says *roughly*.
- ❌ **Averaging Khullar's 75–100 cm against Majid's 100–150 cm for the east coast.** They bound
  **different regions under different schemes**. Report both; average neither.
- ❌ **Explaining the monsoon here.** ↪ Topic 16 owns it.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The A regions carry the *distribution of key natural resources* clause's Indian half
most directly: the evergreen west coast, the savanna interior and the retreating-monsoon east coast
are the climatic premises of three completely different resource and cropping geographies.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** This lesson supports
`Geography/basic/16_Tropical-Monsoon-and-Marine-Climate`, which owns **2026 Prelims Q33** —
*"Andaman and Nicobar climate, monsoon rainfall, and seasonal precipitation"*
(`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2026.md` line 63, whose key is recorded locally as **provisional** and is
**not disclosed here**) — and `Geography/basic/15_Hot-Wet-Equatorial-Climate`, which owns **2021
Prelims Q60** and **2023 Prelims Q63** (`_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2018-2023.md` lines 453 and 666, both
*"Routed; key unavailable locally"*). **None is answered here.** Topic 14's contribution is the
distinction those owners rely on: **Am is not Af**, and a monsoon climate with a short dry season is
not an equatorial climate with none.

**Answer use.** In any Indian regional-climate answer, pair **code + extent + figure** in a single
clause: *"Amw — the west coast south of Mumbai, over 300 cm."* Three facts, nine words, and it
immediately separates a candidate who has read a source from one who has read a summary.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Amw** — monsoon with a **short dry winter**; **west coast south of Mumbai**; **over 300 cm**
   (Khullar folio 160).
2. ✅ **As** — monsoon with a dry season in the **high-sun period**; **Coromandel coast / coastal
   Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra**; **75–100 cm**, mostly winter rain from the **retreating
   monsoon**.
3. ✅ **Aw** — **tropical savanna**; most of the **peninsular plateau** barring the Coromandel and
   Malabar strips; northern boundary **roughly the Tropic of Cancer**; **about 75 cm**.
4. **Three A codes, three seasonal stories.** The group letter is shared; the calendar is not.
5. ✅ **C2 line 57 is right and is kept:** the west coast is **Amw**, not Aw.
6. ✗ **C2 gives no rainfall figure for any of the three.** All figures here are Khullar's.
7. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Cross-evidence, Majid folio 4.33 under Stamp:** **Malabar Coast** Goa to Cape Comorin,
   **over 250 cm**, mean annual **27 °C**, annual range **3 °C**, **Kochi** typical.
8. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Konkan Coast** — Tapi mouth to Goa, **more than 200 cm**, over **90 %** from the Arabian
   stream, January ≈ **24 °C**, July ≈ **27 °C**, annual range **3–6 °C** increasing south to north.
9. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Tamil Nadu Coast under Stamp** — **100–150 cm**, most in the **retreating monsoon,
   October to December**, January ≈ **24 °C**, July ≈ **30 °C**. **Different scheme, different
   bounding, different figure from Köppen's As — report both, average neither.**
10. **As is group A, not Cs.** Same slot-2 letter, different slot-1 letter, different thermal regime.
11. ✗ **U2: Majid's Köppen folios 4.29–4.30 are not in the local extract.** C2's Majid grounding for
    the Köppen table is **not locally verifiable**, and nothing is quoted from those folios.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two stations lie at approximately the same latitude in peninsular India: one receives
over 300 cm and is coded Amw, the other about 75 cm and is coded Aw. Account for the difference, and
state exactly where Köppen's explanation stops.

**Model answer:** **The difference is relief and wind direction, not latitude.** ✅ Khullar folio 160
places **Amw** on the **west coast south of Mumbai** with **over 300 cm**, and **Aw** across most of
the **peninsular plateau** barring the Coromandel and Malabar strips with **about 75 cm**. The
Arabian moisture stream meets the Western Ghats wall and is forced to ascend, delivering its load on
the windward strip; east of the crest the same air descends and the delivery collapses. Both stations
keep a coolest month above **18 °C**, so both remain in group **A**; they diverge in the **second
slot** — **m**, a monsoon regime with a *short* dry season, against **w**, a clear dry winter — and in
amount by more than 200 cm. **Where the explanation stops.** Köppen's apparatus consists of
**thresholds on monthly means**. It can record that one station crosses the *m* criterion and the
other the *w* criterion; it contains **no term for a mountain, no term for wind direction and no term
for seasonal circulation reversal**. The orographic account above is supplied from outside the
scheme, and the monsoon mechanism that drives it belongs to
`Geography/advanced/16_India-Monsoon-Mechanism`. **The classification is a faithful record of an
outcome and a silent witness to its cause** — which is the standing charge in any critical evaluation.

**Misconception to avoid:** That sharing a group letter implies climatic similarity. Amw and Aw are
both A, and they differ by more than 200 cm of annual rainfall and by the entire length of their dry
season. The group letter answers one question — is the coolest month above 18 °C — and nothing else.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 7

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Account for* the distribution of Köppen's A-group
regions in India.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** India's three A regions share a coolest month above **18 °C** and differ
entirely in **when** water arrives. **Amw** — the west coast **south of Mumbai**, **over 300 cm** —
is the windward face of the Western Ghats, where the Arabian stream is forced to ascend; its dry
season is short and falls in winter. **Aw** — most of the **peninsular plateau** barring the
Coromandel and Malabar strips, north to roughly the **Tropic of Cancer**, **about 75 cm** — is the
leeward interior, monsoon-fed with a clear dry winter: tropical savanna. **As** — the **Coromandel
and coastal Tamil Nadu with adjoining Andhra**, **75–100 cm** — is the anomaly, drawing most of its
rain in **winter from the retreating monsoon**, so its dry season falls in the high-sun period.
Relief and wind direction organise all three; latitude organises none. Köppen records the pattern
faithfully and explains none of it. *(148 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Causal chain **3.5** · Code–region fit **3.0** · Figure accuracy **2.0** ·
Route-out discipline **1.5** *(dimension weights 35 · 30 · 20 · 15)*

Progress: 8 / 17 · Stage: Core · Visual grammar: **nested isohyet rings** · India hook: **BShw within a short distance of Amw**

### The intuition, before any term

The dry regions of India are not a block. They are a **gradient with a centre**, and the centre is in
the far west. Rainfall falls away towards it; variability rises towards it; and the class changes
twice along the way — from humid, to steppe, to desert.

That is why this lesson's visual is a set of **rings** rather than a transect or a table. Rings show
a gradient **without pretending the line is sharp**, which matters more here than anywhere else in
the topic: the BSh/BW boundary runs through a belt where the rainfall difference between one year and
the next is **larger than the difference between the two classes**.

### The primary visual — nested rings over the north-west

```text

   ✅ Codes, extents and figures: Khullar printed folio 160 (scan PDF p.97)
   ✅ Variability: Majid *GoI* printed folio 4.28, Fig. 4.20 (scan PDF p.73)

                        ╭───────────────────────────────────────────╮
                     ╭──┤  OUTSIDE THE RINGS — humid classes        ├──╮
                  ╭──┤  │  Aw ≈ 75 cm · Cwg Ganga plain             │  ├──╮
               ╭──┤  │  ╰───────────────────────────────────────────╯  │  ├──╮
            ╭──┤  │  │                                                 │  │  ├──╮
         ╭──┤  │  │  │   ░░░░░░░░░░ 25 cm RING ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░     │  │  │  ├──╮
```
