**Caption.** Two rings, three classes, and a variability gradient running the opposite way to the
rainfall gradient. The soft edges are the content: this is the one place in India where a sharp
classification line is least defensible, and it is also the place every climate-shift claim wants to
be made about.

### The two B regions, exactly as the source gives them

✅ **Khullar printed folio 160 (scan PDF p.97)**, regions 4 and 5 of nine:

| # | ✅ Code | ✅ Type as printed | ✅ Extent as printed | ✅ Figure |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | **BShw** | **semi-arid steppe** | **rain-shadow of the Western Ghats** and much of **Rajasthan** with contiguous **Haryana and Gujarat** | **12–25 cm** |
| 5 | **BWhw** | **hot desert** | **most of western Rajasthan** | **less than 12 cm** |

**Decode the codes fully** — the grammar from Lesson 5, applied:

```text
   B   S   h   w                          B   W   h   w
   │   │   │   └─ ✅ dry season in WINTER  │   │   │   └─ ✅ dry season in WINTER
   │   │   └───── ✗ "hot" — ✗ Khullar's   │   │   └───── ✗ as before
   │   │          printed definition is    │   │
   │   │          INVERTED (C1, L4)        │   │
   │   └───────── ✅ S = STEPPE            │   └───────── ✅ W = DESERT
   └───────────── ✅ B = evaporation       └───────────── ✅ B = same relation
                  exceeds precipitation
```

### ✗ C1, reinforced — the Thar is the proof

Lesson 4 established that ✅ Khullar folio 159's *"h (heiss) — average annual temperature **under**
18 °C"* is inverted. **This lesson is where the proof lives**, and it should be stated in exactly
this form in an answer:

> ✅ Khullar's own folio 160 places **BWhw** across *"most of western Rajasthan"* and **BShw** on the
> Thar margin and the Ghats' rain-shadow. ✗ Under his own printed rule, every one of those places
> would need a **mean annual temperature below 18 °C**. They are among the hottest tracts in India.
> **The assignment refutes the definition, inside one chapter, two folios apart.**

⚠️ The corrected convention — **h ≥ 18 °C mean annual, k < 18 °C** — is marked ⚠️ throughout, because
**no local source states it positively**.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 8 — BShw within a short distance of Amw, across one ridge

✅ **Khullar folio 160** puts **BShw**, at **12–25 cm**, in the *"rain-shadow of the Western Ghats"*.
✅ The same folio puts **Amw**, at **over 300 cm**, on the west coast south of Mumbai.

**Those two regions are separated by a single ridge line**, and the rainfall ratio across it exceeds
**twelve to one**. There is no comparable gradient anywhere else in the Indian classification, and it
is the sharpest available demonstration of the central Indian climatic fact: **relief and wind
direction, not latitude, organise this country's climate map.**

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** The map line is a **transition belt**, not a boundary. The
> scheme draws it sharp because it must — a classification has to assign every point to exactly one
> class — but the ground has a gradient tens of kilometres wide. **The sharpness is an artefact of
> classification, not a feature of the atmosphere**, and an answer that says so has made the most
> important criticism of threshold mapping in one sentence.

### The complete causal chain for the B regions

```text
   ① SUBTROPICAL SUBSIDENCE over the north-west
      ⚠️ plus weak monsoon penetration into the far west
            │
   ② RAIN-SHADOW POSITION east of the Western Ghats crest
            │
   ③ DISTANCE FROM THE MOISTURE SOURCE, increasing westward
            │
            ▼
   LOW DELIVERY ◄──────────────┬──────────────► HIGH EVAPORATIVE DEMAND
   ✅ 12-25 cm → under 12 cm   │                 ⚠️ high temperature, clear
   ✅ variability ≈ 60 %        │                    skies, dry air, wind
   (Majid 4.28)                 │
                                ▼
            ✅ EVAPORATION EXCEEDS PRECIPITATION   (folio 158)
                                │
                     ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                     ▼                     ▼
              ✅ S (steppe)          ✅ W (desert)
                 BShw 12-25 cm          BWhw < 12 cm
                     │                     │
                     └──────────┬──────────┘
                                ▼
       USE: dryland cropping calendars · pastoral range · irrigation design ·
            groundwater draft · salinisation risk  ── ALL ROUTED OUT

   ⚠️ ASSUMPTION: that an annual total can stand for availability. At 60 per
      cent variability it cannot, which is why Thornthwaite's P/E index
      (Lesson 11) sums TWELVE MONTHLY ratios rather than using one annual figure.
   ✗ REFUSAL: no direction, rate, extent or date of movement is asserted for
      either ring (U5). Lesson 16 carries the mechanism with magnitude withheld.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen** gives the north-west **two** classes, BShw and BWhw, separated at about **12 cm**. ✅ **R.L. Singh** (Lesson 14, Khullar folio 165) gives the same tract **one** division — *Arid*: Thar, west Rajasthan, south-west Haryana, Kachchh, **about 25 cm, locally 10 cm**, with a *"very large diurnal and annual range"*. **Two schemes, two resolutions of the same ground** |
| **Objection** | *"If variability is 60 per cent, is a 12 cm class boundary meaningful at all?"* |
| **Reply** | It is meaningful as a **long-run statement** and misleading as a **short-run** one. The class rests on a thirty-year normal (Lesson 6), so it describes the central tendency correctly; what it cannot do is tell you about any given year, which is what a cultivator needs. **This is a genuine limitation of threshold classification and it should be conceded in an answer, not defended.** ⚠️ The reasoning is standard; ✅ the 60 per cent figure and the inverse relationship are Majid's |
| **Unresolved residual** | ✗ **No computable aridity index exists in this evidence base.** Khullar's B is defined in words only; the Indian classes are given as outputs. The boundary can be **read off the book's map** but not **recomputed from the book's rule** |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Treating 12 cm and 25 cm as Köppen criteria.** ✅ They are **Khullar's Indian figures for the
  regions**, not thresholds of the scheme.
- ❌ **Reproducing the inverted `h`.** ✗ C1 — and the Thar assignment is the proof.
- ❌ **Writing BWk or BSk for India.** ⚠️ Neither code is printed in any folio read here, and India's
  dry regions are **h**, hot.
- ❌ **Asserting that the dry belt is expanding.** ✗ U5 refused: no dataset, baseline, scenario or
  period exists in this evidence base.
- ❌ **Giving C2's extent as the source's.** ✅ The source says *"much of Rajasthan with contiguous
  Haryana and Gujarat"*; C2 says *"parts of Punjab, Gujarat and NW margins"*.
- ❌ **Teaching desertification here.** ↪ Routed out; **2020 GS-I Q5** belongs to Environment and
  Ecology (Lesson 4).
- ❌ **Drawing the BSh/BW boundary as a hard line in an answer map.** It is a belt, and saying so
  earns marks.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The dry belt is where the *distribution of key natural resources* clause bites hardest
in India — water above all — and where the classification's limitations are most consequential for
anyone using it.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The nearest Mains demand is **2020 GS-I Q5**,
*"Desertification as a process without climatic boundaries"*, *Justify with examples*, 10 marks,
routed by `_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2018-2023.md` line 149 to
**`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/23_Desertification-UNCCD-and-Land-Degradation`** with the note
*"Routed to owning topic"*. **It is not answered here.** Topic 14 supplies one thing to it — the
reason the phrase *"without climatic boundaries"* is arguable at all, namely that **aridity is a
relation and not a place**. The **2024 Essay prompt** *"Forests precede civilizations and deserts
follow them"* (`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2024-2025.md` line 100, routed to
`Essay/basic/03_Issue-Based-Prompt-Scoping`) stands in the same relation: **Topic 14 supplies the
aridity-threshold material that makes the second clause arguable, and nothing more.**

**Answer use.** Quantify twice — once for amount and once for reliability. *"BWhw, under 12 cm; and
at under 20 cm, rainfall variability runs around 60 per cent."* The second figure is the one almost
no candidate has, and it converts a description into an argument about risk.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **BShw** — **semi-arid steppe**; **rain-shadow of the Western Ghats** and much of **Rajasthan**
   with contiguous **Haryana and Gujarat**; **12–25 cm** (Khullar folio 160).
2. ✅ **BWhw** — **hot desert**; **most of western Rajasthan**; **less than 12 cm**.
3. **Decode both fully:** B (evaporation > precipitation) + S/W (steppe/desert) + h (hot) + w (dry
   winter).
4. ✗ **C1 reinforced: the Thar assignment refutes Khullar's printed `h` definition**, two folios
   apart, inside one chapter.
5. 🇮🇳 ✅ **BShw sits within a short distance of Amw across the Ghats crest** — **12–25 cm against
   over 300 cm**, a ratio above twelve to one. **Relief, not latitude.**
6. **The map line is a transition belt.** The scheme draws it sharp because it must.
7. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Majid folio 4.28:** **Jaisalmer, Barmer, Ganganagar, Jodhpur** under **20 cm**, variability
   **around 60 %**; **100–200 cm** belt at **10–20 %**; over **200 cm** (Mawsynram, Cherrapunji) under
   **10 %**. ✅ *"Inverse relationship between the average annual rainfall and variability."*
8. **At 60 % variability the year-to-year range exceeds the class interval.** Concede this; do not
   defend it.
9. ⇄ **R.L. Singh gives the same tract ONE division — *Arid*, about 25 cm, locally 10 cm, very large
   diurnal and annual range** (Khullar folio 165). Two schemes, two resolutions.
10. ✗ **U5 refused: no expansion of any Indian dry belt is asserted anywhere in this file**, in any
    direction, at any rate, for any period.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using only figures from the local sources, explain why India's dry belt is better
described as a gradient with a core than as a region with a boundary — and state what this implies
for how the BSh/BW line should be drawn in an answer map.

**Model answer:** **The figures describe a gradient twice over.** ✅ Khullar folio 160 gives
**BShw** at **12–25 cm** across the Ghats' rain-shadow and much of Rajasthan with contiguous Haryana
and Gujarat, and **BWhw** at **less than 12 cm** across most of western Rajasthan — two classes
separated by a single number on a continuously falling rainfall surface, not by any physical
discontinuity. ✅ Majid folio 4.28 adds the second gradient, running the other way: below **20 cm**
variability is *"around 60 per cent"*; at **100–200 cm** it is **10–20 per cent**; above **200 cm**
it is *"less than 10 per cent"* — the source's own *"inverse relationship between the average annual
rainfall and variability in rainfall."* **Put the two together and the implication is sharp**: near
the 12 cm line, the difference between one year and the next is larger than the difference between
the two classes, so a station can satisfy either criterion depending on which years the normal
happens to cover. **For an answer map**, the line should be drawn as a **belt with a stated width**
and labelled as a transition, with the core — ✅ Jaisalmer, Barmer, Ganganagar, Jodhpur — marked
rather than the edge. A candidate who draws a hard line is reproducing a cartographic necessity as if
it were an observation.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the sharpness of a class boundary reflects sharpness on the ground.
Every classification must assign each point to exactly one class, so every boundary is drawn hard
regardless of how gradual the underlying change is. The hardness is a property of the method, and
saying so is a criticism the examiner is looking for.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 8

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Explain with reference to data* the character of
India's dry climatic regions.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** India's dry belt is a gradient with a core. **BShw**, semi-arid steppe at
**12–25 cm**, occupies the Western Ghats' rain-shadow and much of Rajasthan with contiguous Haryana
and Gujarat; **BWhw**, hot desert at **less than 12 cm**, covers most of western Rajasthan. Both are
**B** because evaporation exceeds precipitation — a relation, so the class survives whatever the
temperature. The data show the character better than the codes. Below **20 cm**, rainfall variability
runs **around 60 per cent** at Jaisalmer, Barmer, Ganganagar and Jodhpur; at 100–200 cm it is
10–20 per cent; above 200 cm, under 10 per cent — an **inverse relationship** between amount and
reliability. Aridity is therefore unreliability, not merely shortage: near the 12 cm line the
year-to-year range exceeds the class interval, so the boundary is a belt. **No direction or rate of
movement is asserted** without a named dataset, baseline and scenario. *(149 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Gradient reasoning **3.0** · Variability evidence **3.0** · Threshold handling
**2.5** · Map clarity **1.5** *(dimension weights 30 · 30 · 25 · 15)*

Progress: 9 / 17 · Stage: Core · Visual grammar: **altitudinal column** · India hook: **Dfc in Sikkim and Arunachal**

### The intuition, before any term

India's A and B regions spread **horizontally**. Its C, D and E regions stack **vertically**.

That is not a metaphor. ✅ Khullar's regions 6 to 9 run from the Ganga Plain, to the eastern
Himalayan front, to the Uttarakhand mountain belt, to the high Jammu & Kashmir and Himachal
Pradesh — which is an **ascent**, not a journey north. A column is therefore the honest visual, and
it is the one that makes the final count legible: **nine regions, five groups, no H.**

This lesson carries three corrections and is the hinge of the whole edition. Get it right and
Lesson 10's A/B/C/H correction closes itself.

**Caption.** Four bands, one ascent, and the count that both owners destroy. Note the brace: the
absence of H is as examinable as the presence of Dfc, and it is the same error seen from the other
side.

### The four regions, exactly as the source gives them

✅ **Khullar printed folio 160 (scan PDF p.97)**, regions 6 to 9 of nine:

| # | ✅ Code | ✅ Type as printed | ✅ Extent as printed | ✅ Figure |
|---:|---|---|---|---|
| 6 | **Cwg** | monsoon with **dry winter** | most of the **Ganga Plain**, eastern **Rajasthan**, **Assam** and the **Malwa Plateau** | summer to **40 °C**, winter **27 °C** |
| 7 | **Dfc** | **cold humid winters** with a **shorter summer** | **Sikkim**, **Arunachal Pradesh** and parts of **Assam** | winter about **10 °C** |
| 8 | **Et** | **tundra** | mountain areas of **Uttarakhand** | **0–10 °C** |
| 9 | **E** | **polar** | **higher Jammu & Kashmir** and **Himachal Pradesh** | warmest month **0–10 °C** |

✅ The body text at the same folio cites **Fig. 5.28** for the Köppen map — while the map printed at
folio 159 is numbered **FIG. 5.27**, which is **also** the number on the Stamp map at folio 157.
**This is why no figure number is ever cited alone in this file.**

### ✗ C15 and C14, applied — nine rows, not seven, and no H

```text
   SIDE BY SIDE: WHAT C2 PRINTS, AND WHAT THE SOURCE PRINTS

   C2's TABLE (7 rows)                  ✅ KHULLAR folio 160 (9 regions)
   ─────────────────────────────        ──────────────────────────────────────
   Am / source variant Amw        ───►  1 Amw   over 300 cm, W coast S of Mumbai
   As                             ───►  2 As    75-100 cm, Coromandel + Andhra
   Aw                             ───►  3 Aw    about 75 cm, peninsular plateau
   BShw                           ───►  4 BShw  12-25 cm
   BWhw                           ───►  5 BWhw  under 12 cm
   Cwg                            ───►  6 Cwg   summer 40 °C, winter 27 °C
                                        7 Dfc   ✗✗ ABSENT FROM C2 ENTIRELY ✗✗
   "E / H and local cold types;   ───►  8 Et    0-10 °C, Uttarakhand mountains
    code varies by map convention"      9 E     warmest month 0-10 °C, higher
                                                J&K and HP
   ─────────────────────────────        ──────────────────────────────────────
   TWO REGIONS LOST. ONE GROUP (D) LOST ENTIRELY. AND AN H IMPORTED THAT
   KÖPPEN'S GROUP LIST AT FOLIO 158 DOES NOT CONTAIN.
```

**✗ U9, applied in passing.** C2 line 16 states, ✅-marked, that *"D/E-type conditions are essentially
high-altitude Himalayan, **not lowland Indian climates**"*. As **geography** that is true — and as a
**Köppen statement** it is the error. ✅ Khullar's Köppen-India **does** award India a **D** region
(**Dfc**) and **two E** regions (**Et**, **E**). The correct formulation is: **India's D and E regions
are montane, but they are genuine Köppen regions, not absences.**

### ✗ C2, applied — Et and E cannot be told apart by the printed criterion

This is the subtlest defect in the chapter and the one most worth teaching, because it shows a
candidate how to audit a source rather than consume it.

| ✅ Region 8 | ✅ Region 9 |
|---|---|
| **Et** — tundra; mountain areas of **Uttarakhand**; **0–10 °C** | **E** — polar; **higher Jammu & Kashmir and Himachal Pradesh**; **warmest month 0–10 °C** |

**The two intervals are identical.** And ✅ Khullar's own polar rule at folio 159 — **T = tundra,
F = icecap** — settles what should follow: **a polar region whose warmest month reaches 0–10 °C is
ET, not EF.** So as printed, **regions 8 and 9 are the same class under two labels**, distinguished
only by the states named.

**What the edition does.** It teaches the nine-region list **exactly as printed** — because that is
what the source says and a candidate must recognise it — and then states plainly that **the Et/E
separation is not supported by the printed thresholds.**

> **The safe formulation for an answer:** write *"E-group conditions (**ET**, and above the snow line,
> **EF**)"* rather than reproducing the two identical ranges. That sentence is defensible under any
> map convention and it demonstrates that the candidate has noticed what the book did not.

### ⇄ C16, applied — Cwg against Cwa

| Source | Code for the northern plains | Status |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Khullar folio 160** | **Cwg** | **The source code.** Printed |
| ✅ **C2** lines 27, 42, 54, 61 | **Cwg** | Agrees with the source |
| ✗ **C1** line 142 | **Cwa** | **Disagrees with C2 and with the source** |
| ⚠️ Modern maps | **Cwa** | ⚠️ **No local source states this.** Marked accordingly |

**The resolution is not to choose.** ✅ **Cwg** is what the book prints, and the `g` is the single
most valuable letter in the topic, because ✅ folio 159 defines it as the **Ganges type of annual
march of temperature — the hottest month comes *before* the solstice and the summer rainy season.**

**And that definition is itself a physical observation worth stating in an answer.** North India's
temperature peak arrives in **May**, ahead of the June solstice, because the monsoon's arrival then
caps it. A classification letter has encoded a monsoon fact — which is the one place where Köppen's
otherwise causally silent grammar brushes against causation.

⚠️ **Cwa** is the modern-map equivalent, where the third slot takes **`a`** (warmest month over
22 °C) instead of the regional **`g`**. **Name both and name the convention**, exactly as Lesson 3
did for −3 °C against 0 °C.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 9 — Dfc in Sikkim and Arunachal

✅ **Khullar folio 160, region 7: Dfc — cold humid winters with a shorter summer; Sikkim, Arunachal
Pradesh and parts of Assam; winter about 10 °C.**

**This is the single most examinable row in the topic and it is absent from both owners.** It is also
the most counter-intuitive: a **D** region — Köppen's *mid-latitude rainy with severe winters*, the
group of the Siberian and Canadian interiors — inside tropical India, at latitudes under 29° N.

Decode it and the strangeness resolves: **D** (coldest month below −3 °C) + **f** (no dry season) +
**c** (warmest month under 22 °C). Altitude supplies the winter cold; the eastern Himalayan monsoon
supplies the year-round moisture that makes it **f** rather than **w**; and the short, cool summer
supplies the **c**.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** **Dfc is a montane region**, and reading it as evidence that
> India has continental climates **in the lowlands** is precisely the error C2 commits from the other
> direction (U9). The correct statement is narrow and must stay narrow: **India has a genuine Köppen
> D region; it is in the eastern Himalaya; and it exists because of height, not because of
> continentality.**

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen** gives the Himalayan belt **three** regions — Dfc, Et and E — using groups D and E. ✅ **Trewartha** (Lesson 10, folio 163) gives the same belt **one** code: **H**, undifferentiated highlands, covering J&K, HP, Uttarakhand, Sikkim and Arunachal together. **Köppen resolves the mountains; Trewartha declines to.** That is the honest contrast, and it is the opposite of what C2's A/B/C/H sentence implies |
| **Objection** | *"If Khullar's Et and E are indistinguishable, should the nine-region list be taught at all?"* |
| **Reply** | Yes — because it is **what the source prints**, and a candidate who meets it in an examination hall must recognise it. But it should be taught **with the defect attached**, and the safe answer-form — *"E-group conditions (ET and, above the snow line, EF)"* — should be used in writing. **Teaching a flawed printed list honestly is not the same as reproducing it uncritically** |
| **Unresolved residual** | **Two.** The **Et/E separation** is unsupported by the printed thresholds and no local source resolves it. And ⚠️ **the Cwa equivalence and the 0 °C C/D convention both rest on standard knowledge with no local folio** — they are named as conventions and never asserted as findings |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Saying India has Köppen A, B, C and H.** ✗ **That is Trewartha's result** (C14). Köppen-India
  is **A B C D E**, **nine regions**, **no H**.
- ❌ **Omitting Dfc.** ✅ It is region 7, and both owners lose it (C15).
- ❌ **Saying India has no D or E Köppen regions.** ✗ U9 — it has one D and two E.
- ❌ **Writing Cwa as the source code.** ✅ Khullar prints **Cwg**; **name both and name the
  convention** (C16).
- ❌ **Treating `g` as a rainfall letter.** ✅ It is the **annual march of temperature**.
- ❌ **Reproducing Et and E with their identical ranges unqualified.** ✗ C2.
- ❌ **Citing "Fig. 5.27" for the Köppen map.** ✗ It is printed on both the Stamp map and the Köppen
  map, and the body text cites **Fig. 5.28**. **Cite by folio.**
- ❌ **Explaining western disturbances here.** ↪ Topic 13 advanced owns them.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** These four regions complete the Indian application of the *salient features* clause,
and they carry the country's most consequential agricultural climate — ✅ **Cwg across most of the
Ganga Plain**, whose dry winter is the premise of the whole rabi system (Lesson 12's hook).

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The Himalayan material here supports
`Geography/advanced/25_India-Cold-Desert-and-Poles` and
`Geography/basic/25_Arctic-or-Polar-Climate`, the latter owning **2021 GS-I Q15** — *"Melting Arctic
ice and Antarctic glaciers and weather patterns"*, *Explain*, 15 marks,
`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2018-2023.md` line 184. **It is not answered here.** The winter
rainfall that makes Cwg's `w` a *dry* rather than an *absent* winter comes from western disturbances,
owned by `Geography/advanced/13_India-JetStream-Western-Disturbances`. **Topic 14 names the class;
Topic 13 names the system that waters it.**

**Answer use.** The highest-value sentence a candidate can write about Indian climate classification
is this one: *"Köppen's India is nine regions across A, B, C, D and E — including Dfc in Sikkim and
Arunachal — and contains no H, because H is not one of Köppen's groups."* It is correct, it is
specific, and it pre-empts the single commonest error in the topic.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Cwg** — monsoon with dry winter; most of the **Ganga Plain**, eastern **Rajasthan**, **Assam**
   and the **Malwa Plateau**; summer to **40 °C**, winter **27 °C** (Khullar folio 160).
2. ✅ **Dfc** — cold humid winters with a shorter summer; **Sikkim, Arunachal Pradesh and parts of
   Assam**; winter about **10 °C**. **Absent from both owners.**
3. ✅ **Et** — tundra; mountain areas of **Uttarakhand**; **0–10 °C**.
4. ✅ **E** — polar; **higher Jammu & Kashmir and Himachal Pradesh**; warmest month **0–10 °C**.
5. ✅ **THE COUNT: nine regions, five groups — A, B, C, D and E — and NO H.**
6. ✗ **C14: "India has Köppen A, B, C and H" is Trewartha's result, not Köppen's.** It imports H and
   deletes D, Et and E in one sentence.
7. ✗ **C15: C2's table has seven rows and no Dfc**, collapsing row 7 into *"E / H and local cold
   types; code varies by map convention"*.
8. ✗ **U9: India's D and E regions are montane but genuine**, not absences.
9. ✗ **C2: Et (0–10 °C) and E (warmest month 0–10 °C) carry the SAME interval** and are not separable
   by the printed criterion. ✅ By Khullar's own **T/F** rule, a polar region whose warmest month
   reaches 0–10 °C is **ET**, not **EF**.
10. **Safe answer-form:** write *"E-group conditions (**ET** and, above the snow line, **EF**)"*.
11. ⇄ **C16: Khullar prints Cwg; C1 says Cwa; C2 says Cwg.** ✅ Teach **Cwg**, explain **g**, present
    **Cwa** as the ⚠️ modern-map equivalent, name the convention.
12. 🇮🇳 ✅ **g = the Ganges type of annual march** — the hottest month arrives **before** the solstice
    and the summer rains. **A classification letter encoding a monsoon fact.**
13. ✅ **The body text cites Fig. 5.28 for the Köppen map while the map at folio 159 is numbered FIG.
    5.27 — the same number as the Stamp map at folio 157.** **Never cite a Khullar figure number
    alone.**
14. ⇄ **Köppen gives the Himalaya three regions; Trewartha gives it one (H).** Köppen resolves the
    mountains; Trewartha declines to.

### Concept check

**Question:** State precisely what is wrong with the claim "India has Köppen A, B, C and H", and give
the correct statement with its evidence.

**Model answer:** **The claim transplants one scheme's result onto another.** ✅ **A, B, C and H is
Trewartha's** Indian result, printed at Khullar folio 163: *"Out of the above climatic types four
types viz. A, B, C and H are found in India. These four types have further been divided into seven
sub-types."* ✅ **Köppen's** Indian result, at folio 160, is **nine regions** — Amw, As, Aw, BShw,
BWhw, **Cwg**, **Dfc**, **Et** and **E** — drawn from **five groups: A, B, C, D and E.** The claim
therefore commits three errors at once. **First, it imports H into Köppen**, although ✅ Khullar's
Köppen group list at folio 158 is **A/B/C/D/E only, with H absent**, and both owners elsewhere say
correctly that H is a later addition. **Second, it deletes India's only Köppen D region** —
**Dfc**, cold humid winters with a shorter summer, in **Sikkim, Arunachal Pradesh and parts of
Assam**, winter about **10 °C**. **Third, it deletes both E regions** — **Et**, tundra in the
mountain areas of Uttarakhand at 0–10 °C, and **E**, polar in higher Jammu & Kashmir and Himachal
Pradesh. The correct statement is: **Köppen-India is A, B, C, D and E across nine regions with no H;
Trewartha-India is A, B, C and H across seven sub-types.** The two results belong to two schemes and
must never be merged.

**Misconception to avoid:** That H can be quietly added to Köppen because every modern highland map
uses it. H is a **later addition** to the scheme and is **not in Khullar's group list**; importing it
into a statement about Köppen's Indian regions does not add a region, it **replaces three** — the D
region and both E regions vanish to make room for it.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 9

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Critically enumerate* Köppen's climatic regions of
India.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** Köppen's India is **nine regions** across **five groups**. **Amw** — west
coast south of Mumbai, over 300 cm. **As** — Coromandel and coastal Tamil Nadu with adjoining Andhra,
75–100 cm, mostly winter rain. **Aw** — peninsular plateau to roughly the Tropic of Cancer, about
75 cm. **BShw** — Ghats' rain-shadow and much of Rajasthan, 12–25 cm. **BWhw** — most of western
Rajasthan, under 12 cm. **Cwg** — most of the Ganga Plain, eastern Rajasthan, Assam, Malwa; summer
40 °C, winter 27 °C; *g* is the **Ganges type** annual march, the hottest month preceding the
solstice. **Dfc** — Sikkim, Arunachal, parts of Assam; winter about 10 °C. **Et** — Uttarakhand
mountains, 0–10 °C. **E** — higher J&K and Himachal. **Critically:** there is **no H** — A/B/C/H is
Trewartha's result — and **Et and E carry the same printed interval**, so the separation is
unsupported. Modern maps render Cwg as **Cwa**. *(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Nine-region completeness **3.5** · Dfc and Et/E handling **3.0** · Cwg/Cwa
convention **2.0** · Defect disclosure **1.5** *(dimension weights 35 · 30 · 20 · 15)*

```text
                   microthermal · E boreal · F polar, PLUS H. The word "subtropical"
```

### The intuition, before any term

Trewartha did not invent a rival logic. He **tidied Köppen's**. ✅ Khullar folio 162 says it in one
phrase: Trewartha *"modified and simplified"* Köppen.

That makes this lesson a **translation exercise**, and translation errors are the examinable content.
Two schemes using the same letters for different things is the perfect setting for a statement
question, and it is exactly where C2 went wrong: it read Trewartha's Indian answer and filed it under
Köppen.

So the visual is a **crosswalk matrix**. A misattribution is a **mapping** error, and only a mapping
shows it.

### The primary visual — the crosswalk

```text

      No local source prints a conversion table, and none is asserted here.

   ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
   THE DECISIVE ROW — WHAT EACH SCHEME SAYS ABOUT INDIA

   ✅ KÖPPEN-INDIA  (folio 160) ──►  A  B  C  D  E   ·  NINE REGIONS  ·  NO H
                                     ╰─────────────╯
                                     Amw As Aw BShw BWhw Cwg Dfc Et E

   ✅ TREWARTHA-INDIA (folio 163) ─►  A  B  C  H      ·  SEVEN SUB-TYPES
                                     ╰──────────╯
                                     Am Aw Bs Bsh Bwh Caw H
                  ╭──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╮
                  │  ✗ C14: C2 LINE 49 TAKES THE SECOND ROW AND LABELS IT    │
                  │     KÖPPEN — ✅-MARKED. That single transposition        │
                  │     imports H, deletes D, deletes both E regions, and    │
                  │     turns nine regions into seven.                       │
                  ╰──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────╯

   NOTE WHICH WAY ROUND THE "SIMPLIFICATION" RUNS IN THE MOUNTAINS:
   KÖPPEN resolves the Himalaya into THREE regions — Dfc, Et, E.
   TREWARTHA declines to resolve it at all — ONE code, H, "UNDIFFERENTIATED".
   C2's sentence implies the opposite of the truth.
```

**Caption.** Seven Trewartha groups against five Köppen groups, and two completely different Indian
answers. The boxed note is C14, and it is the single most likely source of a wrong answer in the
entire topic.

### Trewartha's scheme, exactly as the source gives it

✅ **Khullar printed folio 162 (scan PDF p.98)**: **Prof. Glenn T. Trewartha**, **University of
Wisconsin**, author of ***An Introduction to Climate***, *"modified and simplified"* Köppen's
classification into **six major world climates plus H**:

| ✅ Group | ✅ Name as printed |
|---|---|
| **A** | tropical humid |
| **B** | dry |
| **C** | humid mesothermal |
| **D** | humid microthermal |
| **E** | **boreal** |
| **F** | **polar** |
| **H** | **undifferentiated climate of highlands** |

**Read the shift in E and F.** In Köppen, **E is polar** and there is no F group. In Trewartha, **E
is boreal** and **F is polar**. **The same letter names a different thing in the two schemes** — which
is the structural reason the two Indian results cannot be merged, and the reason a candidate must
always say *whose* E they mean.

### ✗ C11, applied — C2's group list is Trewartha-Horn 1980, not Trewartha 1954

C2 line 35 lists the groups as *"tropical, dry, **subtropical**, temperate, boreal, polar and
highland"*. **"Subtropical" is not in Khullar's folio-162 list.** Where does it come from?

✅ **Khullar's chapter bibliography at printed folio 167 (scan PDF p.100)** holds the answer, and it
lists **three distinct Trewartha entries**:

> ✅ **Trewartha, Glenn T. (1954): *An Introduction to climate*, McGraw Hill, New York**
> ✅ **— (1961): *The Earth's Problem Climates***
> ✅ **Trewartha, Glenn T. and Horn, Lyle H. (1980): *An Introduction to Climate*, McGraw Hill**

**So the bibliography itself separates the 1954 Trewartha from the 1980 Trewartha-and-Horn**, and ✅
Majid folio 4.28's lineage dates Trewartha **1954**. The *subtropical* group belongs to the **later
revision**.

**Required treatment, applied here.** Teach **Khullar's six-plus-H as the examinable list**. Name
**Trewartha and Horn (1980)** as the origin of C2's labels. And mark the 1980 content ⚠️, because
**the local evidence for it is a bibliography line, not a table** — no group definitions, no
thresholds, no Indian application. **No 1980 table is reproduced anywhere in this file.**

### Trewartha applied to India — the four types and seven sub-types

✅ **Khullar printed folio 163 (scan PDF p.99)**, quoted exactly:

> ✅ *"Out of the above climatic types **four types viz. A, B, C and H** are found in India. These
> four types have further been divided into **seven sub-types**."*

And the seven, with their figures:

| ✅ Sub-type | ✅ Description as printed | ✅ Temperature | ✅ Rainfall | ✅ Where |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Am** | — | average annual **27 °C** | **over 250 cm** | **western coastal plain and Tripura** |
| **Aw** | — | **27 °C** | **about 100 cm**, all in summer | major part of the **peninsular plateau and Mizoram** |
| **Bs** | **semi-arid steppe** | above **27 °C** | **below 100 cm** | **rain-shadow of the Western Ghats** |
| ✗ **Bsh** | *"tropical and subtropical **desert**"* | above **27 °C** | **50–100 cm** | — |
| ✗ **Bwh** | *"**middle latitude** desert"* | — | down to **20 cm** | **west Rajasthan and Kachchh** |
| **Caw** | — | winter temperature **falls below 18 °C** | mostly in **summer**, decreasing **east to west**; **western disturbances** giving light winter rain | **most of the northern plain** |
| **H** | **undifferentiated highlands** | **invariably low**; snowfall in winter | monsoon rain in summer **plus western disturbances in winter** | most of the Himalayan region including **J&K, Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, Sikkim and Arunachal Pradesh** |

**The count checks out.** **Am, Aw, Bs, Bsh, Bwh, Caw, H** is **exactly seven**, matching the printed
claim. ✅ Map **FIG. 5.30**.

### ✗ C3 and C4, applied — two inverted letters in the sub-type list

```text
   ✗ C3 — "Bsh — TROPICAL AND SUBTROPICAL DESERT", 50-100 cm
   ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   THE LETTER SAYS:   S = STEPPE.  A desert sub-type is Bw.
   THE NUMBER SAYS:   50-100 cm is STEPPE-CONSISTENT, not desert-consistent.
                      Khullar's own Bs, two rows above, is "below 100 cm".
   ►► THE WORD IS THE SLIP; THE NUMBER IS SOUND.
   ►► CORRECTED READING: Bsh = HOT SEMI-ARID STEPPE.

   ✗ C4 — "Bwh — MIDDLE LATITUDE DESERT", west Rajasthan and Kachchh, to 20 cm
   ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   THE LETTER SAYS:   h = HOT / LOW LATITUDE.  A middle-latitude desert is BWk.
   THE CROSS-REFERENCE SAYS: ✅ Khullar's OWN KÖPPEN section at folio 160 calls
                      the SAME TRACT — "most of western Rajasthan" — BWhw,
                      a HOT desert.
   ►► CORRECTED READING: Bwh = TROPICAL / HOT DESERT.
   ►► AND NOTE: this is the THIRD time in one chapter that the letter h is
      mishandled. C1 at folio 159 (inverted definition), C4 here. The pattern
      is worth naming in an answer: THE SOURCE IS UNRELIABLE ON h SPECIFICALLY.
```

**How to use this in an answer.** Do not list the errors as errors. Use them to demonstrate
**internal cross-checking**: *"Khullar describes Bsh as a desert at 50–100 cm, but S denotes steppe
and the figure is steppe-consistent; and his Bwh is called a middle-latitude desert although his own
Köppen section codes the same tract BWhw, hot."* That is a paragraph of demonstrated source-handling.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 10 — Caw across the northern plain

✅ **Khullar folio 163:** **Caw** covers **most of the northern plain**; its **winter temperature
falls below 18 °C**; its rainfall is **mostly in summer, decreasing from east to west**; and
**western disturbances give light winter rain**.

Set that beside ✅ **Köppen's Cwg** from Lesson 9 — *most of the Ganga Plain, eastern Rajasthan,
Assam and the Malwa Plateau; summer to 40 °C, winter 27 °C* — and the comparison teaches itself:

```text
   THE SAME GROUND, TWO SCHEMES, TWO CODES

   ✅ KÖPPEN   Cwg  ──► C  + w (dry winter) + g (GANGES-TYPE annual march:
      folio 160                              hottest month BEFORE the solstice)
                     ──► "most of the Ganga Plain, eastern Rajasthan,
                          Assam and the Malwa Plateau"
                     ──► summer to 40 °C · winter 27 °C

   ✅ TREWARTHA Caw ──► C  + a + w
      folio 163      ──► "most of the northern plain"
                     ──► winter FALLS BELOW 18 °C · summer-concentrated rain
                          DECREASING EAST TO WEST · western disturbances
                          giving LIGHT WINTER RAIN

   ►► SAME COUNTRY, SAME PLAIN, DIFFERENT SCHEMES, DIFFERENT THIRD LETTERS.
      They are NOT interchangeable labels, and writing "Cwg/Caw" as if they
      were one code is a scheme-merge of exactly the kind C14 punishes.
```

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** Trewartha's **Caw** and Köppen's **Cwg** cover the same ground
> under different schemes. **They are not synonyms**, and the only honest way to use both in an
> answer is to name the scheme each time. Note too that Trewartha's description adds a fact Köppen's
> does not — the **east-to-west decline in summer rainfall** and the **western-disturbance winter
> rain** — which is a reminder that a scheme's *prose* often carries more geography than its *code*.

### The complete causal chain, and what Trewartha's simplification costs

```text
   KÖPPEN'S SCHEME (1901-1936)
            │
            ▼
   ✅ "MODIFIED AND SIMPLIFIED" by TREWARTHA (1954, Univ. of Wisconsin)
            │
            ├──► SPLIT Köppen's polar end:  E becomes BOREAL, F becomes POLAR
            │          │
            │          └──► GAIN: the boreal forest belt gets its own group
            │               COST:  the letter E now means two different things
            │                      in the two schemes
            │
            └──► ADD H, "UNDIFFERENTIATED CLIMATE OF HIGHLANDS"
                       │
                       ├──► GAIN: one honest admission that a mountain cannot be
                       │          classified from a lowland station register
                       └──► COST: ✅ KÖPPEN'S INDIA RESOLVES THE HIMALAYA INTO
                                  THREE REGIONS (Dfc, Et, E). TREWARTHA'S
                                  RESOLVES IT INTO NONE.
            │
            ▼
   ✅ INDIA: four types, seven sub-types, FIG. 5.30
            │
            ▼
   ✗ AND THEN C2 READS THIS RESULT AND FILES IT UNDER KÖPPEN (C14)

   ⚠️ ASSUMPTION: that simplification is improvement. In the mountains it is
      demonstrably a loss of resolution, and an evaluative answer should say so.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen-India**: nine regions, five groups, **no H**, Himalaya resolved into **three**. ✅ **Trewartha-India**: four types, seven sub-types, **H present**, Himalaya resolved into **one**. **Trewartha is simpler in the lowlands and coarser in the mountains** |
| **Objection** | *"If Trewartha simplified Köppen, is Trewartha not simply the better scheme to quote?"* |
| **Reply** | Not for India, and the reason is specific. ✅ Trewartha's **H** is explicitly *"undifferentiated"* — it declines to distinguish Sikkim from higher Himachal — whereas ✅ Köppen's folio-160 list separates **Dfc**, **Et** and **E**. For a country where the mountain belt is a quarter of the territory and carries three distinct climatic regimes, *"undifferentiated"* is a real cost. **The honest position is that Trewartha is cleaner at the group level and poorer at the Indian margin** |
| **Unresolved residual** | **Three.** ✗ Majid's Trewartha folios **4.34–4.35 are absent from the local extract** and nothing is quoted from them. ⚠️ **Trewartha-Horn (1980) is known only from a bibliography line** and no table from it is reproduced. ✅ And **Khullar's two letter errors (C3, C4) are corrected but not explained** — no source says why they occurred |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Attributing A/B/C/H to Köppen.** ✗ **C14** — it is Trewartha's, Khullar folio 163.
- ❌ **Listing "subtropical" among Trewartha's 1954 groups.** ✗ **C11** — it is **Trewartha-Horn
  (1980)**.
- ❌ **Assuming E means the same thing in both schemes.** ✅ Köppen's E is **polar**; Trewartha's E is
  **boreal** and his **F** is polar.
- ❌ **Calling Bsh a desert.** ✗ **C3** — S is steppe, and the **50–100 cm** figure is
  steppe-consistent.
- ❌ **Calling Bwh a middle-latitude desert.** ✗ **C4** — h is hot, and Khullar's own Köppen section
  codes the same tract **BWhw**.
- ❌ **Writing "Cwg/Caw" as one code.** Two schemes, two codes, name both.
- ❌ **Saying Trewartha resolves India's mountains better than Köppen.** ✅ The opposite: his H is
  *undifferentiated*; Köppen gives three regions.
- ❌ **Quoting Majid for Trewartha-India.** ✗ Folios 4.34–4.35 are not in the local file.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** The alternative schemes are what turn a *describe* question into an *evaluate* question.
Trewartha is the cheapest comparison available — same letters, same empirical logic, different
answer — which is why it is the first alternative taught.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** This lesson supports
`Geography/basic/21_Warm-Temperate-Eastern-Margin-China-Type`, whose type Trewartha's **C** group
contains, and `Geography/basic/25_Arctic-or-Polar-Climate`, which owns **2021 GS-I Q15**
(`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2018-2023.md` line 184). **Neither is answered here.** The
contribution is the warning that **polar in one scheme is E and in the other F** — a displacement of
exactly the kind that a statement question is built on, and the same species of error as the Beaufort
nomenclature shifts that Topic 13 documents.

**Answer use.** Whenever two schemes are compared, **report the scheme results as a pair of counts**
before any prose: *"Köppen-India: nine regions, five groups, no H. Trewartha-India: four types, seven
sub-types, H present."* Two lines, and the comparison is already made.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Prof. Glenn T. Trewartha, University of Wisconsin**, *An Introduction to Climate*; *"modified
   and simplified"* Köppen (Khullar folio 162).
2. ✅ **Six world groups plus H:** A tropical humid · B dry · C humid mesothermal · D humid
   microthermal · **E boreal** · **F polar** · **H undifferentiated highlands**.
3. **E means different things in the two schemes.** Köppen's E is polar; Trewartha's **F** is polar.
4. ✗ **C11: C2's "subtropical" group belongs to TREWARTHA AND HORN (1980)**, cited separately in
   Khullar's bibliography (folio 167) from **Trewartha (1954)** and **Trewartha (1961),
   *The Earth's Problem Climates***. ⚠️ The 1980 content is a bibliography line only.
5. ✅ **Trewartha-India: "four types viz. A, B, C and H … divided into seven sub-types"** (folio 163).
6. ✅ **The seven: Am · Aw · Bs · Bsh · Bwh · Caw · H.** Count verified.
7. ✅ **Am** — 27 °C, **over 250 cm**, western coastal plain **and Tripura**.
8. ✅ **Aw** — 27 °C, **about 100 cm** all in summer, peninsular plateau **and Mizoram**.
9. ✅ **Bs** — semi-arid steppe, above 27 °C, **below 100 cm**, Ghats' rain-shadow.
10. ✗ **C3: Bsh is printed as "tropical and subtropical desert" at 50–100 cm.** S = steppe, and the
    figure is steppe-consistent. **Corrected: hot semi-arid steppe.**
11. ✗ **C4: Bwh is printed as a "middle latitude desert" in west Rajasthan and Kachchh, to 20 cm.**
    h = hot, and ✅ Khullar's own Köppen section codes the same tract **BWhw**. **Corrected: tropical
    / hot desert.**
12. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Caw** — most of the northern plain; winter **below 18 °C**; summer-concentrated rain
    **decreasing east to west**; **western disturbances** giving light winter rain. **Same ground as
    Köppen's Cwg, different scheme, different code.**
13. ✗ **C14 CLOSED: Köppen-India = A B C D E, nine regions, no H. Trewartha-India = A B C H, seven
    sub-types.** ✅ Köppen resolves the Himalaya into **three** regions; ✅ Trewartha's **H** is
    *"undifferentiated"*.

### Concept check

**Question:** Trewartha is routinely described as a simplification of Köppen. Using the Indian
results from both schemes, assess whether "simplification" is an accurate description.

**Model answer:** **It is accurate at the group level and misleading at the Indian margin.** ✅ At the
group level the claim holds: Khullar folio 162 records that Trewartha *"modified and simplified"*
Köppen into **six groups plus H**, splitting the cold end so that **E becomes boreal** and **F
polar**, which gives the boreal forest belt its own group — a genuine tidying. ✅ **In the Indian
lowlands it also holds**: four types and seven sub-types (folio 163) against Köppen's nine regions
(folio 160), with the two schemes agreeing closely on the ground — Trewartha's **Caw** across *"most
of the northern plain"* corresponds to Köppen's **Cwg**, and Trewartha's **Bwh** in *"west Rajasthan
and Kachchh"* to Köppen's **BWhw**. **In the mountains the claim inverts.** ✅ Trewartha assigns the
whole Himalayan belt — J&K, Himachal, Uttarakhand, Sikkim and Arunachal — to a single code, **H,
"undifferentiated climate of highlands"**, whereas ✅ Köppen's folio-160 list distinguishes **Dfc**
(Sikkim, Arunachal, parts of Assam, winter about 10 °C), **Et** (Uttarakhand mountains) and **E**
(higher J&K and Himachal). For a quarter of India's territory, Trewartha is not simpler — he is
**coarser**, and he says so in the group's own name. The accurate verdict is that simplification
bought clarity at the poles and cost resolution in the Himalaya.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a later scheme supersedes an earlier one. Trewartha is a
**modification with different priorities**, not a correction; and his Indian result — A, B, C, H —
is routinely transplanted onto Köppen, which turns nine regions into seven and deletes India's only
D region in the process.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 10

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** *Compare and contrast* Köppen's and Trewartha's
classifications as applied to India.

**Model (≤ 250 words):** **Shared logic, different answers.** Trewartha *"modified and simplified"*
Köppen, retaining the empirical method — thresholds on temperature and precipitation — while
rearranging the groups. Köppen has **five**: A, B, C, D, E. Trewartha has **six plus H**: A tropical
humid, B dry, C humid mesothermal, D humid microthermal, **E boreal**, **F polar**, and **H
undifferentiated highlands**. The displacement matters: **E is polar in Köppen and boreal in
Trewartha**, so the same letter names different things.

**India divides them sharply.** Köppen's India is **nine regions across A, B, C, D and E, with no
H** — Amw over 300 cm on the west coast south of Mumbai; As at 75–100 cm on the Coromandel; Aw about
75 cm on the plateau; BShw 12–25 cm; BWhw under 12 cm; Cwg on the Ganga Plain; **Dfc** in Sikkim and
Arunachal; Et in Uttarakhand; E in higher Jammu & Kashmir. Trewartha's India is **four types — A, B,
C and H — divided into seven sub-types**: Am (over 250 cm), Aw (about 100 cm), Bs, Bsh, Bwh (to
20 cm) and Caw across most of the northern plain, plus H.

**Contrast, not ranking.** Trewartha is cleaner at the group level; Köppen is finer in the mountains,
where Trewartha's single *undifferentiated* H replaces three Köppen regions. Khullar's sub-type
descriptions also invert two letters — Bsh called a desert though S is steppe, Bwh called
middle-latitude though h is hot. **Merging the two results is the common error: A/B/C/H is
Trewartha's, never Köppen's.** *(248 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Six-group accuracy **3.75** · Four-type India result **3.75** ·
Misattribution correction **3.75** · Sub-type figures **2.25** · Balance **1.5**
*(dimension weights 25 · 25 · 25 · 15 · 10)*

## Lesson 11 — Thornthwaite 1931: precipitation-effectiveness and temperature-efficiency

Progress: 11 / 17 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **double-entry index grid** · India hook: **AA′r against EA′d**

### The intuition, before any term

Lesson 4 ended on a complaint: ✅ Köppen defines **B** as *"excess of evaporation over
precipitation"* and **prints no formula**. The class can be read off a map and not recomputed from a
rule.

**Thornthwaite's 1931 scheme is the answer to exactly that complaint.** It takes the same question —
how much water is actually available? — and makes it **arithmetic**: divide precipitation by
evaporation, do it twelve times, add the results. The output is an index, and the index is a class.

That is a different kind of scheme from Köppen's, and the difference is worth naming: **Köppen
classifies by crossing thresholds; Thornthwaite classifies by computing an index and then banding
it.** One asks *which side of the line are you on*; the other asks *what is your score*.

### The primary visual — the double-entry grid

A grid is chosen because **5 × 4 × 6 = 120 is literally a grid**, and drawing it proves the
arithmetic rather than asserting it.

```text
   THORNTHWAITE 1931 — THE DOUBLE-ENTRY INDEX GRID
   ✅ All definitions, bands and counts: Khullar printed folio 161 (scan PDF p.98)
   ✅ Indian type 12: Khullar printed folio 162

   STEP 1 — THE TWO INDICES
   ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
   ✅ P/E RATIO  =  monthly PRECIPITATION  ÷  monthly EVAPORATION
   ✅ P/E INDEX  =  the SUM of the TWELVE monthly P/E ratios
                    ▲
                    └── NOTE THE DESIGN: twelve ratios summed, NOT one annual
                        ratio. A regime that delivers all its water in two
                        months scores differently from one that spreads it.
                        This is the scheme's answer to the variability problem
                        that defeated Köppen's B at 60 per cent (Lesson 8).

   STEP 2 — THE GRID

                 A′        B′         C′          D′       E′        F′
             tropical  mesothermal microthermal  taiga   tundra    frost
              128+      64-127      32-63       16-31    1-15        0
   ┌────────┬─────────┬───────────┬────────────┬────────┬─────────┬────────┐

   ✅ w = deficient in WINTER       ✅ d = deficient in ALL seasons

   STEP 4 — THE ARITHMETIC, SHOWN RATHER THAN ASSERTED
   ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
     5 humidity provinces  ×  4 seasonal subtypes  ×  6 T/E provinces  =  120
     ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
     ✅ "120 COMBINATIONS" — and the product is EXACTLY what the book claims.
     ✅ "32 WORLD TYPES recognised"   ◄── A SOURCE COUNT. NOT DERIVABLE.
     ✅ "12 TYPES FOUND IN INDIA"     ◄── A SOURCE COUNT. NOT DERIVABLE.
     ►► C7: SHOW the 5×4×6. ATTRIBUTE the 32 and the 12. NEVER present the
        latter two as computable — nothing in the source lets you derive which
        120 combinations are realised on Earth.
```

**Caption.** Two indices, three dimensions, one product that checks out and two counts that must be
attributed. The grid is also the correction: the empty cells and the two bare codes at D′ and E′ are
C9, visible without commentary.

### The apparatus, exactly as the source gives it

✅ **Khullar printed folio 161 (scan PDF p.98):**

| ✅ **Humidity provinces** (from the P/E index) | ✅ Band |
|---|---|
| **A** — wet / **rainforest** | **128+** |
| **B** — humid / **forest** | **64–127** |
| **C** — subhumid / **grassland** | **32–63** |
| **D** — semiarid / **steppe** | **16–31** |
| **E** — arid / **desert** | **under 16** |

| ✅ **Seasonal subtypes** | ✅ Meaning |
|---|---|
| **r** | adequate in **all** seasons |
| **s** | deficient in **summer** |
| **w** | deficient in **winter** |
| **d** | deficient in **all** seasons |

| ✅ **Temperature-efficiency (T/E) provinces** | ✅ Band |
|---|---|
| **A′** — **tropical** | **128+** |
| **B′** — **mesothermal** | **64–127** |
| **C′** — **microthermal** | **32–63** |
| **D′** — **taiga** | **16–31** |
| **E′** — **tundra** | **1–15** |
| **F′** — **frost** | **0** |

**Notice the elegance, and say so in an answer.** The humidity provinces run **A to E** and the
temperature provinces run **A′ to F′** — **the same alphabet, primed**. A full Thornthwaite code is
therefore *humidity letter + seasonal letter + primed temperature letter*, and it carries **two
independent dimensions in four characters**. Köppen's code carries one dimension and a seasonal
qualifier. **Thornthwaite's notation is strictly more expressive**, which is both its strength and
the reason it is harder to use.

### ✗ C7, applied — what may be derived and what must be attributed

```text
   DERIVABLE                              ATTRIBUTABLE ONLY
   ──────────────────────────────────     ────────────────────────────────────
   5 × 4 × 6 = 120 combinations           ✅ "32 world types recognised"
   ✅ matches the printed claim exactly    ✅ "12 types found in India"
   ►► SHOW THE PRODUCT                     ►► CITE THE FOLIO. DO NOT JUSTIFY.
                                           Nothing in the source states WHICH
                                           combinations are realised, or why
                                           32 of 120 and not some other number.
```

**Why this distinction is worth a mark.** A candidate who writes *"120 combinations arise as 5 × 4 ×
6"* has demonstrated the structure of the scheme. A candidate who writes *"of these, 32 occur
globally and 12 in India"* **and attributes it** has demonstrated source discipline. A candidate who
tries to *explain* why 32 has invented a justification the book does not contain.

### India under Thornthwaite 1931 — the twelve types, and what the evidence base does and does not hold

✅ **Khullar folios 161–162** state that **twelve** of the thirty-two world types occur in India and
enumerate them. **The following are the certified readings**, and the edition reproduces **only**
these:

| ✅ # | ✅ Code | ✅ Where |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | **AA′r** | **west coast**, **Tripura**, **Mizoram** |
| 2 | **BA′w** | **eastern slopes of the Western Ghats**, **West Bengal** |
| 3 | **BB′w** | **Assam**, **Meghalaya**, **Mizoram**, **Nagaland** |
| 4 | **CA′w** | **large parts of peninsular India** |
| ✗ 5 | printed **CA′w′** | **Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra Pradesh** — described as *"rainfall occurs in winter"* |
| ✗ 10 | bare **D′** | *(no humidity prefix printed)* |
| ✗ 11 | bare **E′** | *(no humidity prefix printed)* |
| 12 | **EA′d** | **western Rajasthan** — *"extremely hot and dry"*, T/E **over 128**, P/E **below 16** |

> ⚠️ **A stated limit of this edition.** ✅ The source's enumeration runs to **twelve** and the
> **count is certified**. **Items 6, 7, 8 and 9 were not captured as certified readings in this
> evidence base and are therefore not reproduced anywhere in this file.** They are **not** claimed to
> be absent from the book; they are **not held here**, and inventing them would be the exact failure
> this edition exists to avoid.

### ✗ C8, applied — the enumeration is complete, and a hypothesis is withdrawn

✅ **Khullar printed folio 162** carries **India type 12, EA′d** — *extremely hot and dry*, T/E over
128, P/E below 16, western Rajasthan — **closing the list begun at folio 161**.

**This matters as a method point.** An early reading of folio 161 *alone* suggested that only eleven
types were listed and that the book's "twelve" claim was unsupported. **Reading the next folio
disproved it.** The hypothesis is therefore **withdrawn and recorded as withdrawn**, and **it appears
nowhere in this file as a defect.** A source is not shown to be wrong by reading half of it.

### ✗ C9, applied — a code that contradicts its own description

✅ **Folio 161, item 5** is printed **CA′w′** and described as *"rainfall occurs in winter"* for
**Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra Pradesh**.

```text
   THE CONTRADICTION, BY THE BOOK'S OWN RULES

   ✅ FOLIO 161 DEFINES:   w = deficient in WINTER
                           s = deficient in SUMMER
                                    │
   ✅ THE DESCRIPTION SAYS: "rainfall occurs in WINTER"
                                    │
                                    ▼
          A region whose rain falls in winter is deficient in SUMMER
                                    │
                                    ▼
                    IT SHOULD READ  C A′ s  — NOT  C A′ w′
                                    │
   ✗ AND THE PRIME ON THE SEASONAL LETTER (w′) IS UNEXPLAINED: the prime
     is the TEMPERATURE-PROVINCE marker everywhere else in the scheme.
```

**Required treatment, applied.** Print the code as it appears. State the rule. State the
contradiction. **And recommend that an answer describe the regime in words rather than reproduce the
code** — *"a winter-rainfall regime in Tamil Nadu and adjoining Andhra"* is both accurate and
unfalsifiable.

**Also note, and do not repair:** ✗ items **10** and **11** carry bare **D′** and **E′** with **no
humidity prefix**, which are incomplete codes under the scheme's own grammar. **Reported, not
repaired.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook 11 — AA′r against EA′d, the two Indian extremes

✅ **Type 1, AA′r** — west coast, Tripura, Mizoram. Decode it: **A** humidity (P/E index **128+**,
rainforest) + **A′** temperature (T/E **128+**, tropical) + **r** (adequate in **all** seasons).
**The wettest, hottest, most reliably watered class the scheme has.**

✅ **Type 12, EA′d** — western Rajasthan. Decode it: **E** humidity (P/E **below 16**, desert) + **A′**
temperature (T/E **over 128**, tropical) + **d** (deficient in **all** seasons). *"Extremely hot and
dry."*

```text
   ONE COUNTRY, THE FULL RANGE OF THE HUMIDITY AXIS

   P/E index ◄──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────►
   128+        64-127        32-63         16-31         under 16
     A            B             C             D              E
   ████                                                     ████
   🇮🇳 AA′r                                               🇮🇳 EA′d
   west coast                                            western Rajasthan
   Tripura, Mizoram                                      "extremely hot and dry"
   r = adequate ALL seasons                              d = deficient ALL seasons
   ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   SAME TEMPERATURE PROVINCE (A′, tropical, T/E 128+) AT BOTH ENDS.
   ►► The two extremes differ on the MOISTURE axis ALONE. That is the single
      clearest demonstration available that India's climatic range is a WATER
      range, not a temperature range — and it is a Thornthwaite insight that
      Köppen's notation cannot express as compactly.
```

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** ✅ The **P/E index requires evaporation data**, and India's
> station network reports evaporation far less uniformly than it reports rainfall and temperature.
> **The scheme is data-hungry**, which is precisely why Köppen's cruder thresholds remain the
> operational standard. ⚠️ This is reasoning, not a sourced claim — **no local source states that
> Indian evaporation data are uneven**, and the point is marked accordingly.

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen** tests thresholds on **temperature and precipitation** and is computable from any station register. ✅ **Thornthwaite 1931** computes **two indices**, one of which needs **evaporation**, and bands them. **Köppen is cheaper; Thornthwaite is more physiologically meaningful.** ✅ Both place western Rajasthan at the dry extreme and the west coast at the wet one — **the schemes agree about India and differ about method** |
| **Objection** | *"If Thornthwaite measures what plants actually get, why did Köppen's scheme win?"* |
| **Reply** | **Data cost.** Köppen's inputs are the two most universally recorded quantities in meteorology; Thornthwaite's P/E ratio needs **evaporation**, measured at fewer stations and less consistently. A scheme that cannot be computed everywhere cannot be mapped everywhere. ⚠️ This reasoning is not stated by any local source. **Köppen survives because it is operational, not because it is more accurate** — which is C1 §9.2's own conclusion and the thesis of Lesson 17 |
| **Unresolved residual** | **Three.** ✗ **Thornthwaite exists only in Khullar in this evidence base** — no Majid folio, no Leong page, no retrieved document. ✗ **Indian types 6–9 are not held here and are not reproduced.** ✗ **Item 5's code contradicts its own description, and items 10 and 11 are incomplete** — all three reported, none repaired |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Describing Thornthwaite 1931 as "moisture-index based, not temperature-based".** ✗ **C10** —
  the 1931 scheme uses **both** a P/E index **and** a T/E index. The claim is true only of **1948**.
- ❌ **Presenting 32 or 12 as derivable.** ✗ **C7** — only **5 × 4 × 6 = 120** is.
- ❌ **Reproducing item 5's code CA′w′ without flagging it.** ✗ **C9**.
- ❌ **Writing the primed letters as the humidity letters.** The **prime marks the temperature
  province**: A is humidity, **A′** is temperature.
- ❌ **Reporting that Khullar lists only eleven Indian types.** ✗ **C8** — folio 162 supplies the
  twelfth. That hypothesis was disproved and must not reappear.
- ❌ **Supplying contents for Indian types 6–9.** Not held here; **not invented**.
- ❌ **Treating the P/E index as an annual ratio.** ✅ It is the **sum of twelve monthly ratios**.
- ❌ **Teaching evapotranspiration measurement or irrigation scheduling.** ↪ Routed out to Economy
  agriculture owners.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Thornthwaite is where climatic classification meets the *distribution of key natural
resources* clause most directly, because a **water-availability index** is the climatic variable that
agriculture and irrigation planning actually consume.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The water-balance material here is adjacent to
**2020 GS-I Q5** — *"Desertification as a process without climatic boundaries"*, *Justify with
examples*, 10 marks, routed to
**`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/23_Desertification-UNCCD-and-Land-Degradation`**
(`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2018-2023.md` line 149). **It is not answered here.** The
contribution is precise: **an aridity index is a classification object, while land degradation is a
process**, and the demand's premise — *without climatic boundaries* — is arguable only because the
two are distinct.

**Answer use.** If an answer needs one sentence of Thornthwaite, make it the **apparatus**, not the
label: *"Thornthwaite's 1931 scheme sums twelve monthly precipitation-to-evaporation ratios into a
P/E index, bands it into five humidity provinces, cross-classifies it against six
temperature-efficiency provinces and four seasonal subtypes, yielding 120 combinations."* That
single sentence outperforms a paragraph of description.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Dating, folio 160:** presented **1931**, revised **1933** and **1948**; the first two
   similar; **"the 1948 scheme was markedly different."**
2. ✅ **P/E ratio = monthly precipitation ÷ evaporation.**
3. ✅ **P/E index = the SUM of the twelve monthly P/E ratios** — not an annual ratio.
4. ✅ **Humidity provinces:** A wet/rainforest **128+** · B humid/forest **64–127** · C
   subhumid/grassland **32–63** · D semiarid/steppe **16–31** · E arid/desert **under 16**.
5. ✅ **Seasonal subtypes:** **r** adequate all seasons · **s** deficient in summer · **w** deficient
   in winter · **d** deficient in all seasons.
6. ✅ **T/E provinces:** A′ tropical **128+** · B′ mesothermal **64–127** · C′ microthermal **32–63** ·
   D′ taiga **16–31** · E′ tundra **1–15** · F′ frost **0**.
7. ✅ **120 combinations — and 5 × 4 × 6 = 120 checks out.** **Show the product.**
8. ✅ **32 world types; 12 Indian types.** ✗ **C7: source counts. Attribute; never justify.**
9. 🇮🇳 ✅ **1 AA′r** — west coast, Tripura, Mizoram. **The wet extreme.**
10. 🇮🇳 ✅ **2 BA′w** — eastern slopes of the Western Ghats, West Bengal. ✅ **3 BB′w** — Assam,
    Meghalaya, Mizoram, Nagaland. ✅ **4 CA′w** — large parts of peninsular India.
11. 🇮🇳 ✅ **12 EA′d** — western Rajasthan, *"extremely hot and dry"*, T/E **over 128**, P/E **below
    16**. **The dry extreme — same temperature province as AA′r.**
12. ✗ **C9: item 5 is printed CA′w′ but described as winter-rain for Tamil Nadu and adjoining
    Andhra.** By the book's own rules it should be **CA′s**. **Describe the regime in words.**
13. ✗ **Items 10 and 11 carry bare D′ and E′ with no humidity prefix.** Incomplete codes; reported,
    not repaired.
14. ✗ **C8: folio 162 supplies type 12 and completes the enumeration.** The "only eleven" hypothesis
    is **withdrawn** and appears nowhere.
15. ✗ **Thornthwaite exists only in Khullar here**, and **Indian types 6–9 are not held in this
    evidence base and are not reproduced.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Derive the number of climatic combinations in Thornthwaite's 1931 scheme and state
which of its other numeric claims can and cannot be derived in the same way.

**Model answer:** **The derivation.** ✅ Khullar folio 161 gives three independent dimensions: **five
humidity provinces** from the P/E index — A wet 128+, B humid 64–127, C subhumid 32–63, D semiarid
16–31, E arid under 16; **four seasonal subtypes** — r adequate in all seasons, s deficient in
summer, w deficient in winter, d deficient in all seasons; and **six temperature-efficiency
provinces** — A′ tropical 128+, B′ mesothermal 64–127, C′ microthermal 32–63, D′ taiga 16–31, E′
tundra 1–15, F′ frost 0. Since the three are independent, the combination count is the product:
**5 × 4 × 6 = 120**, which matches the folio's printed claim of **120 combinations** exactly. **What
cannot be derived.** The same folio states that **32 world types are recognised** and that **12 are
found in India**. Neither follows from the apparatus. Nothing in the scheme determines which of the
120 logically possible combinations are **actually realised** on the Earth's surface — that is an
**empirical finding** about the planet, not a consequence of the classification — and nothing
determines how many of those fall within one country's borders. **Both must therefore be attributed
to the source and never justified.** The distinction is the point: a scheme's **structure** is
derivable; its **occupancy** is observed.

**Misconception to avoid:** That because a classification is arithmetical, all its numbers are
arithmetical. The 120 is a property of the notation; the 32 and the 12 are properties of the world,
reported by the author. Treating the latter two as computable invites an invented explanation for
figures that have none in the source.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 11

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** *Derive and apply* Thornthwaite's 1931 classification to
India.

**Model (≤ 250 words):** **The apparatus.** Thornthwaite presented his classification in **1931**,
revising it in **1933** and **1948**. The 1931 scheme — the one the source tabulates — rests on two
indices. The **P/E ratio** is monthly precipitation divided by evaporation; the **P/E index** is the
**sum of the twelve monthly ratios**, so a concentrated regime scores differently from a spread one.
The index bands into five **humidity provinces**: A wet/rainforest **128+**, B humid/forest
**64–127**, C subhumid/grassland **32–63**, D semiarid/steppe **16–31**, E arid/desert **under 16**.
Four **seasonal subtypes** qualify them — **r** adequate in all seasons, **s** deficient in summer,
**w** deficient in winter, **d** deficient in all. A parallel **temperature-efficiency** index gives
six provinces: **A′** tropical 128+, **B′** mesothermal 64–127, **C′** microthermal 32–63, **D′**
taiga 16–31, **E′** tundra 1–15, **F′** frost 0.

**The derivation.** Three independent dimensions give **5 × 4 × 6 = 120 combinations**, exactly as
claimed. Of these, **32 world types** are recognised and **12 occur in India** — source counts, not
derivable.

**The application.** India spans the moisture axis end to end at one temperature province: **AA′r**
on the west coast, Tripura and Mizoram, adequate in all seasons; **BA′w** on the Ghats' eastern
slopes and West Bengal; **BB′w** in Assam, Meghalaya, Mizoram and Nagaland; **CA′w** across much of
peninsular India; and **EA′d** in western Rajasthan, *"extremely hot and dry"*. **Notation defects
exist**: item 5's **CA′w′** is described as winter-rain, which the rules make **CA′s**, and items 10
and 11 carry bare **D′** and **E′**. *(249 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Index definitions **3.75** · 5 × 4 × 6 derivation **3.75** · Indian types
enumerated **3.75** · Notation defects flagged **2.25** · Economy **1.5**
*(dimension weights 25 · 25 · 25 · 15 · 10)*

## Lesson 12 — Thornthwaite 1948: the water-balance turn, and which version to cite

```text

                   based" — IS MISLEADING, because the 1931 scheme the book actually
                   tabulates uses BOTH a precipitation-effectiveness index AND a

⚠️ Live gaps     : THE 1948 APPARATUS IS NOT TABULATED ANYWHERE IN THIS EVIDENCE

                   he does not print its moisture-index bands. The moisture-index
```
