
Progress: 12 / 17 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **before-and-after panel** · India hook: **the rabi crop as a water-balance argument**

### The intuition, before any term

Both owners describe a Thornthwaite that their own source does not tabulate. **That is not a small
slip; it is the reason this lesson exists as a separate lesson.**

The two schemes ask different questions. **1931 asks: how much rain arrives relative to how much
evaporates?** **1948 asks: how much water does the vegetation actually move, and is the supply enough
to meet that demand?** The second is a question about **plants as pumps**, not about rainfall as a
quantity, and it produces a different kind of class.

Merging them produces the specific error in C2 line 59 — and that trap is worth dissecting, because
it is **true of one version and false of the other.**

### The primary visual — two panels, with the owners' sentences placed under the panel they describe

A version conflict needs **two panels**, not one table. The design point is that each owner sentence
is placed beneath the apparatus it is actually about.

```text
   THORNTHWAITE, 1931 AND 1948 — TWO PANELS, ONE CONFLICT
   ✅ Dating and characterisation: Khullar printed folio 160 (scan PDF p.97)

   ╔══════════════════════════════════╗   ╔══════════════════════════════════╗
   ║   PANEL A — 1931                 ║   ║   PANEL B — 1948                 ║
   ║   ✅ TABULATED IN THE SOURCE      ║   ║   ✅ CHARACTERISED IN PROSE ONLY  ║
   ╠══════════════════════════════════╣   ╠══════════════════════════════════╣
   ║ THE QUESTION                     ║   ║ THE QUESTION                     ║
   ║ How much rain arrives relative   ║   ║ How much water does VEGETATION   ║
   ║ to how much evaporates?          ║   ║ actually MOVE, and is supply     ║
   ║                                  ║   ║ enough to meet that demand?      ║
   ╟──────────────────────────────────╢   ╟──────────────────────────────────╢
   ║ THE APPARATUS  (✅ folio 161)     ║   ║ THE APPARATUS                    ║
   ║ · P/E ratio  = P ÷ evaporation   ║   ║ ✅ folio 160, in the book's own   ║
   ║ · P/E index  = Σ of 12 ratios    ║   ║   terms:                         ║
   ║ · 5 humidity provinces A-E       ║   ║   · the PLANT as a METEOROLOGICAL ║
   ║ · 4 seasonal subtypes r s w d    ║   ║     INSTRUMENT                   ║
   ║ · 6 T/E provinces A′-F′          ║   ║   · VEGETATION as the PHYSICAL   ║
   ║ · 5 × 4 × 6 = 120 combinations   ║   ║     MECHANISM TRANSPORTING WATER ║
   ║ · ✅ 32 world types, 12 Indian    ║   ║   · EVAPOTRANSPIRATION           ║
   ║                                  ║   ║ ⚠️ NO BANDS, NO INDEX VALUES AND ║
   ║ ►► USES BOTH A MOISTURE INDEX    ║   ║    NO TABLE ARE PRINTED ANYWHERE ║
   ║    AND A TEMPERATURE INDEX       ║   ║    IN THIS EVIDENCE BASE         ║
   ╟──────────────────────────────────╢   ╟──────────────────────────────────╢
   ║ THE SOURCE'S VERDICT ON THE PAIR ║   ║ THE SOURCE'S VERDICT             ║
   ║ ✅ 1931 and 1933 are "similar"    ║   ║ ✅ "the 1948 scheme was MARKEDLY  ║
   ║                                  ║   ║    DIFFERENT"                    ║
   ╟──────────────────────────────────╢   ╟──────────────────────────────────╢
   ║ WHICH OWNER SENTENCES BELONG HERE║   ║ WHICH OWNER SENTENCES BELONG HERE║
   ║                                  ║   ║ ✗ C2 L36 "moisture index using   ║
   ║ ✗ C2 L59's trap —                ║   ║   precipitation and POTENTIAL    ║
   ║   "Thornthwaite is only          ║   ║   EVAPOTRANSPIRATION" ✅-marked   ║
   ║   temperature-based → it is      ║   ║ ✗ C2 L45, L55 — same             ║
   ║   moisture-index based" —        ║   ║ ✗ C1 L160 — same                 ║
   ║   IS FALSE OF THIS PANEL.        ║   ║ ►► ALL FOUR DESCRIBE PANEL B AND ║
   ║   1931 USES BOTH INDICES.        ║   ║    CITE A BOOK THAT PRINTS PANEL A║
   ╚══════════════════════════════════╝   ╚══════════════════════════════════╝

   ⚠️ AND THE CITATION GAP, REPORTED AND NOT REPAIRED:
   ✅ Khullar's BODY (folio 160) dates the first scheme to 1931.
   ✅ Khullar's BIBLIOGRAPHY (folio 167) lists Thornthwaite 1933 and 1948 —
      AND HAS NO 1931 ENTRY AT ALL.
   ✅ Majid *GoI* folio 4.28 independently dates him "1931, 1933, 1948".
```

**Caption.** Two apparatus, two questions, and four owner sentences filed under the panel they
actually describe. The asymmetry is the finding: one panel is tabulated and one is only characterised,
and the owners describe the one that is not.

### ✗ C10, applied — the version separation, stated plainly

| | ✅ **1931** | ✅ **1948** |
|---|---|---|
| **Status in the source** | **Tabulated in full**, folios 161–162 | **Characterised in prose**, folio 160 |
| **Status in the bibliography** | ✗ **No entry at all** (folio 167) | ✅ *"Thornthwaite, C.W. (1948): 'An Appraisal Towards Rational Classification of Climate', Geographical Review, Vol. 38, pp. 55–94"* |
| **What it uses** | ✅ **Both** a precipitation-effectiveness index **and** a temperature-efficiency index | ✅ Evapotranspiration; the plant as instrument; vegetation as the water-transporting mechanism |
| **Which owner sentences describe it** | **None** | ✗ **C2 lines 36, 45, 55, 59 and C1 line 160 — all ✅-marked** |
| **Is C2's "moisture-index not temperature" trap true of it?** | ✗ **No.** 1931 uses **both** | ✅ **Yes** |

**The rewritten trap.** C2 line 59 reads: *"❌ Thornthwaite is only temperature-based → It is
moisture-index / water-balance based."* **As written it is misleading.** The corrected form:

> **❌ "Thornthwaite is only temperature-based" → Wrong for both versions, but for different reasons.
> The *1931* scheme uses a precipitation-effectiveness index *and* a temperature-efficiency index, so
> it is neither purely moisture-based nor purely temperature-based. The *1948* revision is
> water-balance based, resting on evapotranspiration. Name the version before you name the basis.**

### What the 1948 revision actually asserts, in the book's own terms

✅ **Khullar folio 160**, introducing Thornthwaite, characterises the 1948 turn through three ideas:

| ✅ Idea as printed | What it means, unpacked |
|---|---|
| **The plant as a meteorological instrument** | A plant integrates temperature, radiation, humidity, wind and soil water into one observable response. It is a **measuring device that has already solved the equation** — the same move Köppen made with *native vegetation*, pushed one stage further |
| **Vegetation as the physical mechanism transporting water** | Vegetation does not merely *indicate* climate; it **performs** part of it, lifting soil water into the atmosphere. Climate becomes partly a **biological** process |
| **Evapotranspiration** | The combined quantity — evaporation from surfaces plus transpiration through plants — which is the **demand** side of a water balance |

**Why this is "markedly different", in the book's own phrase.** 1931 measures **what arrives against
what evaporates**. 1948 measures **what the system needs against what it gets**, with the vegetation
inside the system rather than outside it as an indicator. ⚠️ **The moisture-index formulation the
owners describe belongs here** — and it is marked ⚠️, not ✅, because **no local source prints its
bands or its values.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook 12 — the rabi crop as a water-balance argument

✅ **Khullar folio 163**, describing Trewartha's **Caw** across most of the northern plain, records
that its **winter temperature falls below 18 °C**, its rainfall is **mostly in summer**, and
**western disturbances give light winter rain**. ✅ **Khullar folio 160** gives the same plain as
**Cwg**, with **summer to 40 °C and winter 27 °C**.

**Put those facts into a water-balance frame and the argument writes itself.** The northern plain's
winter is **dry, cool and lightly supplemented**. In a precipitation-only scheme, a dry winter is
simply a deficit. In a **water-balance** scheme it is something more interesting: **demand falls with
temperature at the same time as supply falls**, so the deficit is **smaller than the rainfall figure
alone suggests** — and what remains can be met from stored soil moisture and light western-disturbance
rain. That is the climatic premise on which a second, cool-season crop becomes possible at all.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** This is an **agricultural inference**, and the crop owner is
> **Economy**, not Geography. Topic 14 states the **climate premise** — dry cool winter, light winter
> supplementation, reduced evaporative demand — and stops. ⚠️ **No local source states the rabi
> argument in these terms**; C1 line 142 gestures at it with a ⚠️ and the edition keeps the same
> tier. **No area, yield, cropping-intensity or irrigation figure appears here.**

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen** — thresholds on two measured quantities; cheapest; causally silent. ✅ **Thornthwaite 1931** — two computed indices; more physiologically meaningful; data-hungry. ✅ **Thornthwaite 1948** — a water balance with the plant inside it; most physiologically meaningful; **most data-hungry of all**, and not tabulated in this evidence base |
| **Objection** | *"If 1948 is better, why do the owners and the examination still work with Köppen's codes?"* |
| **Reply** | Because **operability beats fidelity in a mapping scheme**. Köppen's inputs exist for every station on Earth. Thornthwaite's 1948 inputs require evapotranspiration estimates, which require more data and more assumptions. ✅ C1 §9.2's own conclusion states the position exactly — Köppen survives because it is the most **operational** scheme, not the most accurate — and that sentence is the one piece of C1's analysis that survives this audit intact |
| **Unresolved residual** | **Two, both reported and neither repaired.** ✗ **The citation gap:** the body dates the first scheme to **1931**; the bibliography has **no 1931 entry**, listing only **1933** and **1948**. ✗ **The 1948 apparatus is not tabulated anywhere in this evidence base**, so every statement of its index bands would be unsourced — and none is made |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Describing Thornthwaite without naming a version.** ✗ **C10** — the owners' exact error.
- ❌ **Repeating C2 line 59's trap as written.** It is **false of 1931**, which uses both indices.
- ❌ **Marking the moisture-index / potential-evapotranspiration formulation ✅.** ⚠️ **No local source
  prints the 1948 bands.**
- ❌ **Assuming the bibliography confirms the body.** ✗ There is **no 1931 Thornthwaite entry** in
  Khullar's references.
- ❌ **Saying 1948 "improved" 1931.** ✅ The source says **"markedly different"**, which is a statement
  about kind, not quality.
- ❌ **Teaching the rabi system.** ↪ Economy owns the crop; Topic 14 owns the climate premise.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** This lesson is the clearest demonstration in the topic that a **classification has
editions and the editions change the answer** — which is the methodological content the *salient
features* clause is examined through at Mains level.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The water-balance frame is adjacent to the
Economy agriculture owners and to
`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/23_Desertification-UNCCD-and-Land-Degradation`, which owns **2020 GS-I
Q5** (`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2018-2023.md` line 149). **Not answered here.** Topic 14's
contribution is the distinction between a **moisture deficit as a class criterion** and a **water
deficit as an agronomic fact** — two different objects that the same words describe.

**Answer use.** One clause protects the whole paragraph: *"Thornthwaite's 1931 scheme — the one
tabulated in Khullar — uses a P/E index with a temperature-efficiency index; the 1948 revision, which
the standard description actually reports, is water-balance based on evapotranspiration."* A
candidate who writes that sentence cannot be caught by either version.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **Folio 160:** presented **1931**, revised **1933** and **1948**; the first two **similar**;
   **"the 1948 scheme was markedly different."**
2. ✅ **1931 is TABULATED** in the source (folios 161–162). ✅ **1948 is CHARACTERISED IN PROSE ONLY**
   (folio 160).
3. ✅ **The 1948 turn, in the book's terms:** the **plant as a meteorological instrument**;
   **vegetation as the physical mechanism transporting water**; **evapotranspiration**.
4. ✗ **C10: C2 lines 36, 45, 55 and C1 line 160 all describe 1948 and all are ✅-marked** against a
   book that prints 1931.
5. ✗ **C2 line 59's trap is misleading.** The **1931** scheme uses **both** a
   precipitation-effectiveness index **and** a temperature-efficiency index. **Name the version
   before the basis.**
6. ⚠️ **The moisture-index / potential-evapotranspiration formulation is ⚠️, not ✅** — no local
   source prints its bands.
7. ✅ **Bibliography, folio 167:** *"Thornthwaite, C.W. (1933): 'The Climates of the Earth',
   Geographical Review, Vol. 23, No. 3, pp. 433–440"* and *"Thornthwaite, C.W. (1948): 'An Appraisal
   Towards Rational Classification of Climate', Geographical Review, Vol. 38, pp. 55–94"*.
8. ✗ **The citation gap: the body dates the first scheme to 1931 and the bibliography has no 1931
   entry.** ✅ Majid folio 4.28 independently dates him *"1931, 1933, 1948"*. **Reported, not
   repaired.**
9. 🇮🇳 ⚠️ **The rabi premise:** the northern plain's winter is **dry and cool**, so **demand falls with
   supply**; ✅ **western disturbances give light winter rain** (folio 163). **The climate premise is
   Topic 14's; the crop is Economy's.**
10. ✅ **C1 §9.2's conclusion survives the audit intact:** Köppen endures because it is the most
    **operational** scheme, not the most accurate.

### Concept check

**Question:** C2 marks "Thornthwaite uses a moisture index based on precipitation and potential
evapotranspiration" as ✅ "from source book". Evaluate that mark.

**Model answer:** **The description is accurate; the mark is not.** ✅ Khullar folio 160 dates
Thornthwaite's classification to **1931**, with revisions in **1933** and **1948**, states that the
first two are **similar**, and says explicitly that *"the **1948** scheme was **markedly
different**"* — characterising it through the **plant as a meteorological instrument**, **vegetation
as the physical mechanism transporting water**, and **evapotranspiration**. The scheme C2 describes
is therefore the **1948** one. But the scheme the source **tabulates**, across folios 161 and 162, is
the **1931** one: a **P/E ratio** of monthly precipitation to evaporation, a **P/E index** summing
twelve monthly ratios, **five humidity provinces** from 128+ down to under 16, **four seasonal
subtypes** r/s/w/d, **six temperature-efficiency provinces** A′ to F′, **120 combinations**, **32**
world types and **12** Indian types. **So C2's sentence describes a version its cited book does not
print.** A ✅ claims a named folio, and no folio carries the 1948 index bands — the 1948 apparatus is
present only as prose and as a bibliography entry for the 1948 *Geographical Review* paper. The mark
should be **⚠️**, and the sentence should name its version. **The consequence is live**, because C2's
own trap at line 59 — *"Thornthwaite is moisture-index based, not temperature-based"* — is **false of
the 1931 scheme**, which uses both a precipitation-effectiveness and a temperature-efficiency index.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a scheme has one correct description. Thornthwaite revised twice
across seventeen years, and his 1948 revision was, in his own source's word, *markedly different*
from the 1931 original. A description without a version is not a half-answer; it is an answer to an
unasked question.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 12

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Differentiate* Thornthwaite's 1931 and 1948 schemes and
state which should be cited in an Indian context.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** Thornthwaite presented his classification in **1931** and revised it in
**1933** and **1948**; the first two are similar, and the source calls the 1948 **"markedly
different"**. **1931** is a double-index scheme: a **P/E ratio** of monthly precipitation to
evaporation, summed over twelve months into a **P/E index**, banded into five humidity provinces, and
cross-classified against six **temperature-efficiency** provinces and four seasonal subtypes —
**120 combinations**, 32 world types, 12 in India. **1948** is a **water-balance** scheme: the plant
treated as a meteorological instrument, vegetation as the mechanism transporting water, and
**evapotranspiration** as the demand term. **For India, cite 1931 for anything tabulated** — AA′r on
the west coast, EA′d in western Rajasthan — because that is the scheme the standard texts print; cite
**1948** when the argument is about **water availability to crops**. Always name the version: the
common claim that Thornthwaite is "moisture-based, not temperature-based" is **false of 1931**.
*(150 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Version separation **4.0** · Citation placement **2.5** · Which-to-cite
guidance **2.0** · Caveat **1.5** *(dimension weights 40 · 25 · 20 · 15)*

| The rabi system, the rice–wheat core and cropping intensity | Economy agriculture owners; `Geography/advanced/20_India-Wheat-Granary` |

Progress: 13 / 17 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **split-India isotherm map** · India hook: **Konkan and Malabar as two Stamp regions**

### The intuition, before any term

Köppen, Trewartha and Thornthwaite all ask the same kind of question: *what class does this place
fall into?* **Stamp asks a different kind: where should the country be cut in two first?**

✅ **Khullar printed folio 156 (scan PDF p.95)** gives the answer: **the January 18 °C isotherm**.
Everything north and above it is ***Temperate or Continental India***; everything south of it is
***Tropical India***. **Only then** are regions named — five in the first half, six in the second.

**That is a regionalisation, not a classification**, and the distinction is worth stating clearly
because it changes what the map is *for*. A classification assigns; a regionalisation **partitions a
particular country**. Stamp's eleven regions do not exist outside India and cannot be applied
anywhere else. Köppen's **Aw** can be found on three continents.

### The primary visual — the split map

```text
   in total. The scheme's first move is a CUT, not a class. That is what makes
   it a REGIONALISATION of one country rather than a CLASSIFICATION of climates.
```

**Caption.** One isotherm, two halves, eleven regions — and a legend that uses the same words for
region 4 and region 8, which is why the halves must be named every time a region is cited.

### ✗ C17, applied — the duplicated legend label

✅ **Khullar's FIG. 5.27 at printed folio 157** labels **region 4** and **region 8** identically as
***"Moderate Rainfall"*** — one in the Temperate/Continental half, one in the Tropical half.

**Required treatment, applied throughout this edition:** cite by **half and number**, never by label
alone.

> ✅ *"Moderate Rainfall (Temperate/Continental India, region 4)"* and ✅ *"Moderate Rainfall
> (Tropical India, region 8)"* — **and say that the printed legend does not disambiguate them.**

**And the limit that goes with it.** ✅ Majid folio 4.33 supplies figures for the **Tropical** member
— *"east of the Western Ghats … about **75 cm** … January about **18 °C**, July about **32 °C**"* —
and **no local folio supplies the Temperate member's figures.** ⚠️ **They are not invented here, and
any answer reproducing Stamp's eleven regions should give region 4 by name and extent only.**

### ⇄ C13, applied — Stamp, or Stamp and Kendrew, and in which year?

**Three local statements, none of which agrees with the others, and the edition resolves none.**

| Source | What it says | Exact location |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Majid *GoI*** | *"**L.D. Stamp and W.G. Kendrew, 1953**"* — a **joint** entry in the ten-authority list | printed folio **4.28** (scan PDF p.73) |
| ✅ **Khullar, chapter body and map** | The eleven-region map is captioned *"Climatic Regions of India (**After Stamp**)"* — **Stamp alone** | printed folios **156–157** |
| ✅ **Khullar, bibliography** | *"**Stamp, L.D. (1958)**: Asia: Regional and Economic Geography"* and *"**Kendrew, W.G. (1961)**: Climates of Continents"* — **two separate works, two separate dates** | printed folios **166–167** |
| ✅ **Khullar, R.L. Singh passage** | *"Following **Kendrew and Stamp**, Dr. R.L. Singh presented his climatic divisions…"* — **pairs them, in the reverse order** | printed folio **164** |

```text
   THE CONFLICT, LAID OUT — AND LEFT OPEN

   ATTRIBUTION      Stamp ALONE (Khullar map)  ⇄  Stamp AND Kendrew (Majid list,
                                                   and Khullar's own folio 164)
   DATE             1953 (Majid)               ⇄  1958 and 1961 (Khullar biblio)
   ORDER OF NAMES   "Stamp and Kendrew"        ⇄  "Kendrew and Stamp"
                    (Majid 4.28)                   (Khullar 164)

   ►► REQUIRED TREATMENT, APPLIED: present the scheme as "STAMP (AND KENDREW)",
      give BOTH datings WITH THEIR FOLIO CITATIONS, and STATE THAT THE LOCAL
      SOURCES DISAGREE.
   ►► DO NOT RESOLVE THE DATE. Nothing in the evidence base adjudicates between
      a 1953 joint scheme and two separate works of 1958 and 1961, and a
      fabricated consensus would be worse than the disagreement.
```

**Why refusing is the right answer.** Majid's 1953 is a **date for a scheme**; Khullar's 1958 and
1961 are **publication dates for two books**. Those are different kinds of object, and **nothing in
the evidence base says they refer to the same event.** Subtracting or averaging them would assert a
relationship no source states — the same discipline that governs the IMD station counts in Lesson 6.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 13 — Konkan and Malabar as two different Stamp regions

To a Köppen reader, the entire Indian west coast south of Mumbai is **one** region: ✅ **Amw, over
300 cm** (folio 160). **Stamp splits the same coast in two, near Goa:**

| ✅ **9 — The Konkan Coast** | ✅ **10 — The Malabar Coast** |
|---|---|
| **Mouth of the Tapi to Goa** | **Goa to Cape Comorin (Kanniyakumari)** |
| **More than 200 cm**, over **90 per cent** from the **Arabian stream** | **Over 250 cm** |
| Mean January ≈ **24 °C**; mean July ≈ **27 °C** | Mean annual ≈ **27 °C** |
| Annual range **3–6 °C**, **increasing from south to north** | Annual range **3 °C** |
| — | **Kochi** typical |

**The split is made on two criteria Köppen's code cannot express**: the **amount** (over 200 cm
against over 250 cm) and the **annual range** (3–6 °C, widening northward, against a flat 3 °C).
**Stamp is reading the coast as a gradient of maritime influence**, and the northward widening of the
range is the evidence.

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** Both coasts are *"very wet west coast"* to a Köppen reader —
> **one code, one region.** Stamp separates them on **amount and range**, which are real differences
> that Köppen's three-slot grammar has no place to record. **The lesson is not that one scheme is
> right; it is that a classification can only express what its notation has room for**, and that is
> the strongest single argument available for reading more than one map of the same country.

### The complete causal chain for Stamp's scheme

```text
   ① A QUESTION ABOUT A COUNTRY, NOT ABOUT CLIMATES
      "How should INDIA be divided?" — not "what class is this place?"
            │
            ▼
   ② ONE STRUCTURING DECISION: the JANUARY 18 °C ISOTHERM  ✅ folio 156
            │
            ├──────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
            ▼                      ▼                      │
   A. TEMPERATE / CONTINENTAL    B. TROPICAL INDIA        │
      INDIA — 5 regions            — 6 regions            │
            │                      │                      │
            └──────────┬───────────┘                      │
                       ▼                                  │
   ③ WITHIN EACH HALF, REGIONS NAMED BY RAINFALL AMOUNT    │
      AND REGIME — "Very Heavy", "Heavy", "Moderate",      │
      "Arid Lowland", "Transitional"                       │
                       │                                  │
                       ▼                                  │
   ④ ELEVEN NAMED REGIONS, ✅ FIG. 5.27 ◄─────────────────┘
                       │
                       ▼
   ⑤ ✅ R.L. SINGH (1971) MODIFIES THIS SCHEME — Lesson 14
      "Following Kendrew and Stamp…" (folio 164)

   ⚠️ ASSUMPTION: that ONE ISOTHERM, in ONE MONTH, can carry the primary
      division of a subcontinent. It is a defensible choice — January is the
      month in which India's north-south thermal contrast is greatest — but
      it is a CHOICE, and the five regions above the line are grouped by a
      shared winter rather than by a shared climate.
   ✗ LIMIT: the scheme is NOT TRANSFERABLE. "The Konkan Coast" is not a
      climatic class; it is a place. Stamp's regions cannot be applied to any
      other country, which is exactly what Köppen's codes can do.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Köppen-India**: nine regions, **transferable codes**, boundaries set by **thresholds**. ✅ **Stamp-India**: eleven regions, **named places**, boundary set by **one isotherm and then by rainfall amount**. ✅ Köppen gives the west coast **one** region; Stamp gives it **two**; ✅ Köppen gives the Himalaya **three** (Dfc, Et, E); Stamp gives it **one** (*The Himalayan Region*). **Each scheme resolves finely exactly where the other does not** |
| **Objection** | *"If Stamp's regions are just places with rainfall labels, what does the scheme add that Köppen does not?"* |
| **Reply** | **Two things, both real.** It adds the **primary cut** — a single explicit structuring decision that a reader can accept or reject, whereas Köppen's map has no hierarchy at all. And it adds **variables Köppen's notation cannot carry**: the **annual range** that separates Konkan from Malabar, and the **share of rainfall from a named stream** — ✅ *"over 90 per cent"* from the Arabian stream in the Konkan, over 90 per cent in the SW monsoon in the Very Heavy Rainfall region, over 90 per cent from the Bay of Bengal stream in the Transitional Zone. **No Köppen code records which ocean the water came from** |
| **Unresolved residual** | **Three.** ⇄ **C13: the dating and the attribution are not resolved** — 1953 joint against 1958 and 1961 separate. ✗ **C17: no local folio supplies figures for region 4**, the Temperate/Continental *Moderate Rainfall*. ✗ And **the figures used here for Stamp's regions come from Majid under the heading "Stamp and Kendrew", while the eleven-region map and legend come from Khullar under "After Stamp"** — the edition uses both and says so each time |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Citing "Moderate Rainfall" without its half.** ✗ **C17** — it is printed twice.
- ❌ **Citing "Fig. 5.27".** ✗ It is printed on **both** the Stamp map (folio 157) and the Köppen map
  (folio 159). **Cite by folio.**
- ❌ **Picking a date for Stamp.** ⇄ **C13** — give **1953 (Majid 4.28)** and **1958 / 1961 (Khullar
  bibliography)** and state the disagreement.
- ❌ **Calling it a classification.** It is a **regionalisation of one country**; its regions are not
  transferable.
- ❌ **Teaching what an isotherm is.** ↪ **U10** — Topic 13 owns it, with **2024 Prelims Q4**, **2025
  Q26** and **2025 Q27**. Topic 14 may **draw** the January 18 °C line and name what it separates,
  and no more.
- ❌ **Treating Konkan and Malabar as one region because Köppen does.** ✅ Stamp separates them at
  **Goa**, on amount and range.
- ❌ **Inventing figures for region 4.** Not held; not supplied.
- ❌ **Writing "Cherrapunji" as the only world record station.** ✅ The source names **Mawsynram and
  Cherrapunji** together.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Stamp's scheme is the clearest Indian instance of the *salient features* clause being
answered by a **regionalisation** rather than a classification — and it is the scheme R.L. Singh
modifies, so it is a prerequisite for Lesson 14.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The **isotherm** this lesson draws is a Topic 13
object in every other role: `Geography/basic/13_Weather-Elements` owns **2024 Prelims Q4**
(*"Inferences from January isothermal maps"*, `_PYQ-ROUTING-PRELIMS-2024-2025.md` line 30) and **2025
Prelims Q27** (*"January isotherms bending over land and ocean"*, line 153), both recorded as *"Key
available locally (official Set-A answer key present); answer not recorded here"*. **That neutrality
is preserved and no key is disclosed.** The single object that legitimately crosses the boundary into
Topic 14 is the **January 18 °C isotherm in its role as Stamp's divider** — a classification object,
not a weather element.

**Answer use.** For any *"climatic regions of India"* demand, the Stamp spine is the fastest
high-scoring structure available: **name the divider, name the two halves, give the counts, then name
regions with figures.** *"Stamp divides India at the January 18 °C isotherm into Temperate or
Continental India (five regions) and Tropical India (six regions)."* One sentence, and the structure
of a fifteen-mark answer is already visible.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **The divider: the JANUARY 18 °C ISOTHERM** (Khullar folio 156), separating ***Temperate or
   Continental India*** from ***Tropical India***.
2. ✅ **Eleven regions: five above the line, six below** — FIG. 5.27 at folio 157, captioned
   *"Climatic Regions of India (After Stamp)"*.
3. ✅ **Temperate/Continental India:** 1 **The Himalayan Region** · 2 **The North Western Region** ·
   3 **The Arid Lowland** · 4 **Moderate Rainfall** · 5 **The Transitional Zone**.
4. ✅ **Tropical India:** 6 **Very Heavy Rainfall** · 7 **Heavy Rainfall** · 8 **Moderate Rainfall** ·
   9 **The Konkan Coast** · 10 **Malabar Coast** · 11 **Tamil Nadu**.
5. ✗ **C17: regions 4 and 8 carry the identical legend label.** **Cite by half and number.** ⚠️ **No
   local folio gives region 4's figures** — not invented.
6. ✅ **Region 5, Transitional Zone (Majid 4.33):** eastern UP, western and north-western Bihar,
   north-western Jharkhand; **100–150 cm**; **over 90 %** in the season of general rains from the
   **Bay of Bengal** stream; January ≈ **15 °C**; mean maximum July **over 40 °C**.
7. ✅ **Region 6, Very Heavy Rainfall:** Assam, Meghalaya, Nagaland, Manipur, Tripura, Mizoram;
   **over 200 cm**; *"the heaviest rainfall in the world … at the stations of **Mawsynram and
   Cherrapunji**"*; over **90 %** in the SW monsoon.
8. ✅ **Region 7, Heavy Rainfall:** West Bengal, Orissa, Jharkhand, eastern Andhra; **100–200 cm
   decreasing east to west**; January **over 18 °C**; ≈ **30 °C** in June–July.
9. ✅ **Region 8, Moderate Rainfall (Tropical):** east of the Western Ghats including Gujarat,
   south-western MP, Maharashtra, Karnataka, greater parts of Andhra; **rain-shadow**; **about
   75 cm**; January ≈ **18 °C**; July ≈ **32 °C**.
10. 🇮🇳 ✅ **Region 9, Konkan:** Tapi mouth to **Goa**; **more than 200 cm**, over **90 %** from the
    **Arabian** stream; January ≈ 24 °C, July ≈ 27 °C; **annual range 3–6 °C increasing south to
    north**. ✅ **Region 10, Malabar:** **Goa to Cape Comorin**; **over 250 cm**; annual ≈ 27 °C,
    **range 3 °C**; **Kochi** typical. **Köppen sees one region here; Stamp sees two.**
11. ✅ **Region 11, Tamil Nadu:** greater parts of Tamil Nadu and the Coromandel; **100–150 cm**; most
    in the **retreating monsoon, October to December**; January ≈ 24 °C, July ≈ 30 °C.
12. ⇄ **C13: Majid 4.28 dates "L.D. Stamp and W.G. Kendrew, 1953"; Khullar's bibliography gives
    Stamp 1958 and Kendrew 1961 separately; Khullar's map says "After Stamp" alone and his folio 164
    says "Kendrew and Stamp".** **Present as "Stamp (and Kendrew)" with both datings. Do not
    resolve.**

### Concept check

**Question:** Stamp's scheme and Köppen's scheme both divide India, yet they are not the same kind of
object. Identify the structural difference and show, with one Indian example from each, what follows
from it.

**Model answer:** **The structural difference is between a classification and a regionalisation.**
✅ Köppen assigns each place to a **transferable class** defined by thresholds — **Aw** means *coolest
month above 18 °C, dry winter*, and it exists on three continents. ✅ Stamp instead makes a **single
structuring cut through one country** at the **January 18 °C isotherm** (Khullar folio 156), yielding
***Temperate or Continental India*** with five regions and ***Tropical India*** with six, and then
names those eleven regions by **place and rainfall amount** — *The Konkan Coast*, *Region of Very
Heavy Rainfall*. **Stamp's regions cannot be applied outside India; Köppen's codes can be applied
anywhere.** **What follows, from each side.** From Köppen's side: the **entire west coast south of
Mumbai is one region, Amw, over 300 cm** — the notation has no slot for anything finer. From Stamp's
side: the **same coast is two regions**, the **Konkan** from the Tapi mouth to Goa at *more than
200 cm* with an annual range of **3–6 °C increasing northward**, and the **Malabar** from Goa to Cape
Comorin at *over 250 cm* with a range of **3 °C**. Stamp can record **annual range** and **which
ocean stream delivered the water**; Köppen's three slots cannot. The consequence for an answer is
that **the two maps are not rivals to be scored but instruments with different resolutions**, and
citing both is stronger than defending either.

**Misconception to avoid:** That the scheme with more regions is the more detailed scheme. Stamp has
eleven regions to Köppen's nine, yet Köppen resolves the Himalaya into three regions where Stamp has
one, and Stamp resolves the west coast into two where Köppen has one. **Resolution is local, not
global**, and each scheme is finest exactly where its chosen variables vary most.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 13

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** *Map and interpret* Stamp's climatic regions of India.

**Model (≤ 250 words):** **The structuring decision comes first.** Stamp divides India at the
**January 18 °C isotherm** into **Temperate or Continental India** and **Tropical India**, then names
**eleven** regions — five above the line, six below.

**Temperate/Continental India:** 1 the **Himalayan Region**; 2 the **North Western Region**; 3 the
**Arid Lowland**; 4 **Moderate Rainfall**; 5 the **Transitional Zone** — eastern Uttar Pradesh,
western and north-western Bihar, north-western Jharkhand, **100–150 cm**, over **90 per cent** from
the Bay of Bengal stream, January about **15 °C**, July maxima over **40 °C**.

**Tropical India:** 6 **Very Heavy Rainfall** — the north-east, **over 200 cm**, containing
**Mawsynram and Cherrapunji**; 7 **Heavy Rainfall** — Bengal to eastern Andhra, **100–200 cm**
decreasing westward; 8 **Moderate Rainfall** — the Ghats' rain-shadow, **about 75 cm**, January
**18 °C**, July **32 °C**; 9 the **Konkan Coast** — Tapi to Goa, **over 200 cm**, range **3–6 °C**
widening northward; 10 the **Malabar Coast** — Goa to Cape Comorin, **over 250 cm**, range **3 °C**,
Kochi typical; 11 **Tamil Nadu** — **100–150 cm**, most in the **retreating monsoon**.

**Interpretation.** The scheme records what codes cannot: annual range, and the ocean stream
supplying the rain. But it is a **regionalisation of one country**, not transferable; its legend
prints *"Moderate Rainfall"* **twice**, for regions 4 and 8; and its dating is contested — **1953** as
a joint Stamp-and-Kendrew scheme, against **1958** and **1961** as separate works. *(246 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Isotherm split **3.75** · Eleven regions ordered **3.75** · Figure accuracy
**3.0** · Duplicate-legend handling **3.0** · Dating conflict **1.5**
*(dimension weights 25 · 25 · 20 · 20 · 10)*

## Lesson 14 — R.L. Singh 1971: ten divisions, and a map that disagrees with its own book

Progress: 14 / 17 · Stage: Advanced · Visual grammar: **ten-cell choropleth skeleton** · India hook: **Singh's two coasts**

### The intuition, before any term

R.L. Singh did not start a new scheme. ✅ **Khullar printed folio 164** says so in the opening clause:
*"**Following Kendrew and Stamp**, Dr. R.L. Singh presented his climatic divisions of India in
**1971**"*, dividing the country into **ten** climatic divisions on **the temperature of the hottest
and coldest months and the average annual rainfall**.

✅ **Majid *GoI* folio 4.36** says the same thing with a sharper edge: *"Professor R.L. Singh
**modified** the climatic divisions of **Stamp and Kendrew** in 1971… His classification is
**quantitative as well as qualitative**, largely based on the amount of rainfall and temperature. In
fact, **the modifications have been done on the basis of variations in temperature**."*

**So the lineage is explicit and the modification is specific**: Singh kept Stamp's regional logic
and **re-cut it on temperature**. That is why the names change character — Stamp's *"Moderate
Rainfall"* becomes Singh's *"Sub-Humid Continental"*, a **moisture-and-continentality** label rather
than a rainfall label.

### The primary visual — the ten-cell skeleton

```text

   ✅ Divisions, states and figures: Khullar printed folios 164-165
   ✅ Legend as Majid's Fig. 4.24 prints it: Majid *GoI* folio 4.36
   Format: DIVISION · JANUARY °C / JULY or MAY °C · RAINFALL

   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ 10  WEST HIMALAYAN                   J&K · Himachal Pradesh · Uttarakhand│
   │     Jan 0-4 °C  /  July 5-30 °C  ·  150 cm                               │
   │     SW monsoon PLUS western disturbances                                 │
   ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
   │  1  ⇄ "PER HUMID NORTH-EAST" (Khullar 164)                               │
   │     ⇄ "THE HUMID NORTH-EAST" (Majid 4.36 body AND Fig. 4.24 legend)      │
   │     ✅ Majid adds: the whole of NE India EXCEPT TRIPURA, Sikkim and       │
   │        north-western West Bengal · MORE THAN 200 cm · July 25-33 °C ·    │

      West Himalayan climate · Arid · Semi-Arid Sub-Tropical · Humid continental
      climate · Sub-Humid Transitional · Humid North East · HUMID SOUTH-EAST ·
      Semi-Arid Tropical · Humid Sahayadri & West coast · Sub-Humid Littorals
```

**Caption.** Ten divisions, each with a January figure, a hot-month figure and a rainfall band — the
most completely quantified Indian scheme in the evidence base, and the only one where a second local
source corrects the first's map.

### ✗ C18, applied — the one defect a second source settles outright

```text
   THE LEGEND ERROR, AND ITS RESOLUTION

   ✅ KHULLAR, BODY TEXT (folio 164)      ──► "Humid SOUTH-EAST"
                                               Bengal, Odisha, Chhattisgarh,
                                               Jharkhand
                     ✗ CONTRADICTS ITSELF
   ✗ KHULLAR, FIG. 5.31 LEGEND (folio 165) ──► "HUMID SOUTH WEST"
                                                │
                     ⇄ ADJUDICATED BY           │
                                                ▼
   ✅ MAJID *GoI*, Fig. 4.24 LEGEND (folio 4.36) ──► "Humid South-East"
                                                │
   ✅ AND BY THE GEOGRAPHY ITSELF ──────────────┤
      Bengal · Odisha · Chhattisgarh · Jharkhand are EASTERN states.
                                                │
                                                ▼
                 ►► TEACH "HUMID SOUTH-EAST". NAME KHULLAR'S LEGEND
                    AS THE ERROR. CITE MAJID'S MAP AS THE CORROBORATION.
```

**This is the only defect in the entire audit that a second independent local source resolves
outright**, and it is worth pointing out in an answer precisely because of that: *"Khullar's own body
text and his Fig. 5.31 legend disagree; Majid's Fig. 4.24 legend and the geography both support the
body."* That is a complete, closed, citable argument in one sentence — and it is the model for how
source conflicts should be handled when they *can* be settled.

### ⇄ C19, applied — and the one that cannot be settled

| Source | Division 1's name | What else it supplies |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Khullar folio 164** | ***"Per Humid North-East"*** | No figures for this division |
| ✅ **Majid *GoI* folio 4.36** | ***"The Humid North-East"*** in the body; ***"Humid North East"*** on Fig. 4.24 | **The whole of north-east India except Tripura, Sikkim and north-western West Bengal; more than 200 cm; mean July 25–33 °C; mean January 10–25 °C** |

**Required treatment, applied.** ⇄ **Give both readings with their citations. Do not choose.** Unlike
C18, there is **no third source and no internal contradiction** to adjudicate between them — *Per
Humid* and *Humid* are both plausible division names, and nothing in the evidence base says which
Singh wrote.

**And note what follows.** The **figures for division 1 come from Majid alone**; ✅ Khullar's folio
does not supply them. **An answer quoting "more than 200 cm, July 25–33 °C, January 10–25 °C" for the
north-east must cite Majid 4.36, not Khullar.**

### ✗ The bibliography, with its printed error

✅ **Khullar printed folio 167:** *"**Singh, R.L. (ed.) (1971): India: A Regional Geography, National
Geog. Society of Indian**"* — reproduced with its typographic error **[sic]**, exactly as the Köppen
entries were in Lesson 2. **Note that Singh is recorded as an *editor*, not an author**, which is
consistent with a regional-geography compendium rather than a classificatory monograph — and which is
a small but real caution against treating the ten divisions as a single-author scheme in the way
Köppen's or Thornthwaite's are.

### 🇮🇳 India hook 14 — Singh's two coasts, and what a planning regionalisation is for

✅ Singh's legend has **two coastal divisions**, and the pair is instructive:

| ✅ **2 — Humid Sahyadri and West Coast** | ✅ **5 — Sub-Humid Littorals** |
|---|---|
| January **19–28 °C**; July **26–32 °C** | January **20–29 °C**; **May 28–38 °C** |
| **About 200 cm** | **75–150 cm** |
| — | **Mostly November–December**, from the **retreating monsoon** |
| — | Eastern Tamil Nadu, coastal Andhra |

**Compare the three schemes on the same two coasts:**

```text
   THE SAME TWO COASTS, THREE TIMES

   ✅ KÖPPEN     west: Amw (over 300 cm)      east: As (75-100 cm, winter rain)
   ✅ STAMP      west: TWO regions — Konkan   east: Tamil Nadu (100-150 cm,
                 (>200 cm) and Malabar              Oct-Dec retreating monsoon)
                 (>250 cm)
   ✅ R.L. SINGH west: "Humid Sahyadri &      east: "Sub-Humid LITTORALS"
                 West coast" (about 200 cm)         (75-150 cm, Nov-Dec)

   ►► NOTE THE VOCABULARY SHIFT. Köppen gives a CODE. Stamp gives a PLACE NAME.
      Singh gives a MOISTURE-CLASS NAME — "Humid", "Sub-Humid", "Semi-Arid",
      "Arid" — which is a PLANNING vocabulary, not a climatological one.
```

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** Singh's divisions are **planning-oriented regionalisations**.
> They are **not Köppen classes and must not be coded** — writing *"Sub-Humid Littorals = As"* merges
> two schemes exactly as C14 does. And note the asymmetry in the names themselves: *Humid*,
> *Sub-Humid*, *Semi-Arid* and *Arid* form a **moisture ladder**, while *Continental*, *Littoral*,
> *Sub-Tropical* and *Tropical* are **position and thermal qualifiers** — so a single division name
> like *"Semi-Arid Sub-Tropical"* carries two independent dimensions, which is closer to
> Thornthwaite's notation than to Köppen's.

### The complete causal chain, and the basis of the scheme

```text
   ✅ STAMP AND KENDREW'S DIVISIONS  (Lesson 13)
                    │
                    │  ✅ Majid 4.36: "Professor R.L. Singh MODIFIED the climatic
                    │     divisions of Stamp and Kendrew in 1971."
                    ▼
   ✅ THE BASIS OF THE MODIFICATION
        ├── ✅ Khullar 164: "the TEMPERATURE OF THE HOTTEST AND COLDEST MONTHS
        │     and the AVERAGE ANNUAL RAINFALL"
        ├── ✅ Majid 4.36: "QUANTITATIVE AS WELL AS QUALITATIVE, largely based on
        │     the amount of rainfall and temperature"
        └── ✅ Majid 4.36: "the modifications have been done ON THE BASIS OF
              VARIATIONS IN TEMPERATURE"
                    │
                    ▼
   ✅ TEN DIVISIONS, each with a JANUARY figure, a HOT-MONTH figure and a
      RAINFALL BAND  —  FIG. 5.31 (Khullar) / Fig. 4.24 (Majid)
                    │
                    ▼
   USE: regional planning vocabulary — Humid / Sub-Humid / Semi-Arid / Arid
                    │
                    ▼
   ↪ ROUTED OUT: agro-climatic zone delineation for planning (Economy), and the
     economy, demography and administration of each named division (regional owners)

   ⚠️ ASSUMPTION: that two temperature figures and one rainfall band suffice to
      describe a division. It is the most quantified Indian scheme here, and it
      still records NOTHING about seasonality beyond two parenthetical notes
      (divisions 5 and 10).
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **Stamp**: eleven regions, cut at the January 18 °C isotherm, named by **rainfall amount**. ✅ **Singh**: ten divisions, **modified from Stamp on temperature variation**, named on a **moisture-plus-position** vocabulary, each with **two temperature figures and a rainfall band**. ✅ **Köppen**: nine regions, **transferable codes**. **Singh is the most quantified and the least transferable** |
| **Objection** | *"If Singh merely modified Stamp, why does the lane route both in separately?"* |
| **Reply** | Because they answer the examination differently. **Stamp supplies a structure** — one explicit cut and two halves — that organises an answer. **Singh supplies numbers** — every division carries a January range, a hot-month range and a rainfall band, which is the densest citable Indian data in the topic. ✅ And Singh's vocabulary is the one that **survives into planning language**: *arid*, *semi-arid*, *sub-humid*, *humid* are the terms administrative documents still use |
| **Unresolved residual** | ⇄ **C19 stands open**: *"Per Humid North-East"* (Khullar 164) against *"Humid North-East"* (Majid 4.36 body and map). **No third source adjudicates and the edition does not choose.** ⚠️ A second, smaller naming divergence also stands open — Majid's legend prints **"Humid continental climate"** where Khullar's body has **"Subhumid Continental"** for division 6; **both shown, neither chosen** |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Writing "Humid South West" for division 3.** ✗ **C18** — Khullar's **legend** is the error; his
  body, Majid's map and the geography all say **South-East**.
- ❌ **Choosing between "Per Humid" and "Humid" North-East.** ⇄ **C19** — give both with citations.
- ❌ **Citing Khullar for division 1's figures.** ✅ They are **Majid's** (folio 4.36).
- ❌ **Coding Singh's divisions as Köppen classes.** They are **planning regionalisations**.
- ❌ **Calling Singh's scheme original.** ✅ Both sources say it **follows / modifies Stamp and
  Kendrew**.
- ❌ **Treating the divisions as agro-climatic zones.** ↪ Agro-climatic zoning is Economy's; the two
  are different objects (Lesson 15).
- ❌ **Citing "Fig. 5.31" without the folio.** Cite **Khullar printed folio 165** and, for the
  corroborating legend, **Majid *GoI* folio 4.36, Fig. 4.24**.
- ❌ **Treating Singh as the sole author.** ✅ The bibliography records him as **editor** of *India: A
  Regional Geography*.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** Singh's scheme is the bridge between the *salient features of world's physical
geography* clause and the **planning vocabulary** of Indian administration — which is where a GS-I
climate answer most naturally reaches a GS-III consequence without leaving its own owner.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The regional half of this material is owned by
`Geography/advanced/29_Regional-Development-and-Five-Year-Plans` and the regional owners; the
agro-climatic half by the Economy agriculture owners. **Neither is taught here.** Topic 14's
contribution is the vocabulary itself — the ladder **arid / semi-arid / sub-humid / humid** — and the
warning that it is a **regionalisation and not a classification**, so it cannot be converted into
codes.

**Answer use.** Singh is the scheme to cite when an answer needs **numbers for a named Indian
region**. *"R.L. Singh's Arid division — Thar, west Rajasthan, south-west Haryana and Kachchh — carries
about 25 cm, locally 10 cm, with a very large diurnal and annual range."* One division, three facts,
and the answer stops sounding generic.

### Revision points

1. ✅ **1971; ten divisions; following Kendrew and Stamp** (Khullar folio 164); ✅ *"modified the
   climatic divisions of Stamp and Kendrew"* (Majid 4.36).
2. ✅ **The basis:** *"the temperature of the hottest and coldest months and the average annual
   rainfall"* (Khullar); *"quantitative as well as qualitative"* and *"the modifications have been
   done **on the basis of variations in temperature**"* (Majid).
3. ⇄ **C19: division 1 is "Per Humid North-East" in Khullar and "Humid North-East" in Majid.** Both
   given; **neither chosen**.
4. ✅ **Division 1's figures are Majid's alone:** the whole of NE India **except Tripura**, Sikkim and
   north-western West Bengal; **more than 200 cm**; July **25–33 °C**; January **10–25 °C**.
5. ✅ **2 Humid Sahyadri and West Coast** — January **19–28 °C**, July **26–32 °C**, **about 200 cm**.
6. ✗ **C18: division 3 is "Humid South-East"** — Bengal, Odisha, Chhattisgarh, Jharkhand; **12–27 °C
   / 26–34 °C**; **100–200 cm**. **Khullar's FIG. 5.31 legend prints "HUMID SOUTH WEST" and is the
   error**; ✅ Majid's Fig. 4.24 legend and the geography both confirm South-East.
7. ✅ **4 Subhumid Transition** — eastern UP, Bihar, north Jharkhand; **9–24 / 24–41 °C**;
   **100–200 cm**.
8. ✅ **5 Subhumid Littorals** — eastern Tamil Nadu, coastal Andhra; January **20–29 °C**, **May
   28–38 °C**; **75–150 cm**, mostly **November–December** from the **retreating monsoon**.
9. ✅ **6 Subhumid Continental** — the Ganga plain; **7–23 / 26–41 °C**; **75–150 cm**. ⚠️ Majid's
   legend prints **"Humid continental climate"**; both shown.
10. ✅ **7 Semi-arid Subtropical** — the **Satluj–Yamuna divide**: Punjab, Haryana, eastern Rajasthan,
    Delhi, Chandigarh; **25–100 cm**; January **6–23 °C**, May **26–41 °C**.
11. ✅ **8 Semi-arid Tropical** — Gujarat, Maharashtra, MP, Chhattisgarh, Karnataka, Telangana;
    January **13–29 °C**, July **26–42 °C**; **50–100 cm**. ✅ **9 Arid** — Thar, west Rajasthan, SW
    Haryana, Kachchh; **about 25 cm, locally 10 cm**; January **5–22 °C**, June **20–40 °C**; **very
    large diurnal and annual range**.
12. ✅ **10 West Himalayan** — J&K, Himachal, Uttarakhand; July **5–30 °C**, January **0–4 °C**;
    **150 cm**; **SW monsoon plus western disturbances**. ✅ **Bibliography: *"Singh, R.L. (ed.)
    (1971): India: A Regional Geography, National Geog. Society of Indian"* [sic]** — Singh as
    **editor**.

### Concept check

**Question:** Khullar's body text and his own map legend disagree about the name of R.L. Singh's
third division. Set out how you would resolve this, and contrast it with a conflict in the same
lesson that cannot be resolved.

**Model answer:** **The resolvable conflict.** ✅ Khullar's body at folio 164 names division 3
***"Humid South-East"*** and places it across **Bengal, Odisha, Chhattisgarh and Jharkhand**, at
**12–27 °C / 26–34 °C** and **100–200 cm**. ✗ His own **FIG. 5.31 legend** at folio 165 prints
***"HUMID SOUTH WEST"***. Two independent checks settle it. **First, a second local source**: ✅ Majid
*GoI* folio 4.36's **Fig. 4.24, "India — Climate Regions (R.L. Singh)"**, prints the legend **"Humid
South-East"**. **Second, the geography**: Bengal, Odisha, Chhattisgarh and Jharkhand are **eastern**
states, and no reading of the map can place them in the south-west. The conclusion is therefore
firm — **Khullar's legend is the error** — and it is the **only** defect in this topic that an
independent local source closes outright. **The unresolvable conflict.** ✅ Khullar folio 164 names
division 1 ***"Per Humid North-East"***; ✅ Majid folio 4.36's body and map both read ***"Humid
North-East"***. Here there is **no third source**, **no internal contradiction** and **no geographical
test** — both are plausible names for the same wettest division — so the correct treatment is to
**give both readings with their folio citations and choose neither**. The general rule is that a
conflict may be closed only when an **independent check** exists; otherwise it must be **reported
open**, because a fabricated consensus is worse than a disclosed disagreement.

**Misconception to avoid:** That a printed map legend outranks the body text that describes it. A
legend is typeset separately and is one of the likeliest places for an error to survive proof-reading
— which is why this topic also carries a legend that prints *"Moderate Rainfall"* twice (Stamp, FIG.
5.27) and a chapter whose contents page swaps two sections.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 14

**Original question (15 marks, 250 words).** *Evaluate the basis of* R.L. Singh's climatic divisions
of India.

**Model (≤ 250 words):** **Lineage first.** Singh did not begin afresh: *"Following Kendrew and
Stamp"*, he presented **ten** divisions in **1971**, and Majid records that he *"modified the
climatic divisions of Stamp and Kendrew"*, the modifications being made **"on the basis of variations
in temperature"**.

**The basis.** Khullar states it as **the temperature of the hottest and coldest months and the
average annual rainfall**; Majid calls the scheme **"quantitative as well as qualitative"**. Every
division accordingly carries a January range, a hot-month range and a rainfall band — the densest
quantification of any Indian scheme: **Arid** at about **25 cm, locally 10 cm**, January 5–22 °C,
June 20–40 °C, with a very large diurnal and annual range; **Humid Sahyadri and West Coast** at about
**200 cm**, January 19–28 °C; **West Himalayan** at **150 cm**, January 0–4 °C, fed by the south-west
monsoon **plus western disturbances**.

**Evaluation.** The strength is the vocabulary — **arid, semi-arid, sub-humid, humid**, crossed with
*continental*, *littoral*, *sub-tropical*, *tropical* — which carries two dimensions per name and
survives into planning language. The weaknesses are three. The divisions are **not transferable**
beyond India. **Seasonality** appears only parenthetically, for the Littorals and the West Himalayan.
And the **cartography is unreliable**: Khullar's own legend prints *"Humid South West"* for a division
his text calls **Humid South-East**, a reading Majid's map corrects; while division 1 stands
unresolved between *"Per Humid"* and *"Humid"* North-East. *(248 words)*

**Rubric (15 marks):** Ten divisions **3.75** · Quantitative/qualitative basis **3.75** · Legend
correction **3.75** · Lineage from Stamp-Kendrew **2.25** · Naming conflict **1.5**
*(dimension weights 25 · 25 · 25 · 15 · 10)*

| Material | Owner, by exact path |
|---|---|
| The economy, demography and administration of each named division | `Geography/advanced/29_Regional-Development-and-Five-Year-Plans` and the regional owners |
| **Agro-climatic zone delineation** for planning | Economy agriculture owners |
| Western disturbances feeding the West Himalayan division | `Geography/advanced/13_India-JetStream-Western-Disturbances` |

## Lesson 15 — Four maps, four questions: climate, vegetation, agro-climate, hazard

```text
━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST — LESSON 15 ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Owner context : C1 lines 237-240 state the map discipline precisely — "station
                   normal, grid cell, climatic region, vegetation biome, agro-climatic
                   zone and administrative boundary are SEPARATE UNITS" — and it is
                   one of the two strongest passages in either owner. C2 §6 (lines
                   71-79) attempts the same separation for hazard and succeeds
                   analytically, BUT names the source wrongly (U8)
📕 Book evidence : Khullar printed folio 158 — Köppen's vegetation premise, which is
                   what makes the climate/biome confusion possible in the first place
🔍 Live check    : IMD Hazard Atlas of India, https://www.imdpune.gov.in/hazardatlas/
                   index.html — HTTP 200, 10,225 B, RE-CONFIRMED AT GENERATION TIME.
                   Live title read directly: "HAZARD ATLAS OF INDIA"
📰 CA anchor     : the Hazard Atlas itself — a STANDING OFFICIAL RESOURCE, dated to
                   its access date, not to a bulletin
🧭 Route-outs    : Disaster-Management owners (hazard preparedness, vulnerability
                   reduction, warning dissemination, NDMA) · Economy agriculture
                   owners (agro-climatic zone delineation) · Geography/advanced/15,
                   /17, /22, /23 and Environment-and-Ecology forest owners (vegetation
                   biomes)
⚠️ Live gaps     : U8 — C2 calls it the "IMD Climate Hazards and Vulnerability Atlas
                   of India". THE LIVE TITLE IS "HAZARD ATLAS OF INDIA" · B6 — NO
                   RETRIEVED IMD SURFACE CLASSIFIES INDIA INTO CLIMATIC ZONES, and
                   none is implied anywhere
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
```

Progress: 15 / 17 · Stage: Synthesis · Visual grammar: **four-overlay stack** · India hook: **one district, four ratings**

### The intuition, before any term

By this point the edition has drawn five maps of India. A sixth temptation now appears, and it is the
commonest error in applied climate writing: **assuming that the climate map, the vegetation map, the
agro-climatic map and the hazard map are versions of each other.**

They are not. **They answer four different questions**, they are built from four different inputs,
and their boundaries do not coincide. ✅ C1 lines 237–240 says it exactly, and it is worth quoting
because it is the owner at its best:

> ✅ *"Station normal, grid cell, climatic region, vegetation biome, agro-climatic zone and
> administrative boundary are separate units. Indian transition belts and mountains cannot be reduced
> to one permanent sharp code line."*

This lesson turns that sentence into a visual and a discipline.

### The primary visual — the four-overlay stack

```text
   FOUR MAPS OF ONE COUNTRY, OFFSET SO THAT THE NON-COINCIDENCE IS VISIBLE

         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
        ╱                                               ╱│  ① CLIMATE CLASS
       ╱   QUESTION: "What are the long-run            ╱ │     ✅ Köppen / Trewartha /
      ╱     temperature and precipitation              ╱  │     Thornthwaite / Stamp /
     ╱      statistics here?"                         ╱   │     R.L. Singh
    ╱       INPUT: 30-year NORMALS (Lesson 6)        ╱    │     Lessons 7-14
   ╱        BOUNDARY: a THRESHOLD                   ╱     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
      ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │  ② VEGETATION BIOME
     ╱                                               ╱│  │     ↪ Geography/advanced/15,
    ╱   QUESTION: "What actually grows here?"       ╱ │  │        /17, /22, /23 and the
   ╱     INPUT: field survey, remote sensing       ╱  │  │        Env-Ecology forest
  ╱      BOUNDARY: an ECOTONE — and it records     ╱   │ ╱         owners
 ╱       DISTURBANCE as well as climate           ╱    │╱    ►► KÖPPEN'S THRESHOLDS WERE
└───────────────────────────────────────────────┘     ╱        PLACED WHERE VEGETATION
    ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────┐╱         CHANGES — WHICH IS WHY
   ╱                                               ╱│          THESE TWO ARE CONFUSED,
  ╱   QUESTION: "What can be grown here, with     ╱ │          AND WHY THEY STILL DIFFER
 ╱     what inputs, under what plan?"            ╱  │       ③ AGRO-CLIMATIC ZONE
╱      INPUT: climate + SOIL + IRRIGATION +     ╱   │╱          ↪ Economy agriculture
│      CROPPING HISTORY + ADMIN UNITS          ╱    ╱           owners
│      BOUNDARY: often an ADMINISTRATIVE one  ╱    ╱         ►► FOLLOWS DISTRICT LINES.
└───────────────────────────────────────────┘    ╱             Climate does not.
    ┌───────────────────────────────────────────┐╱           ④ HAZARD / VULNERABILITY
   ╱                                           ╱                📄 IMD HAZARD ATLAS OF
  ╱   QUESTION: "What goes wrong here, how    ╱                    INDIA, CAUI, Pune-05
 ╱     often, and who is exposed?"           ╱                  ↪ Disaster-Management
╱      INPUT: EVENT FREQUENCY + population  ╱                      owners
│      + assets + coping capacity          ╱                   ►► A HAZARD IS AN EVENT.
│      BOUNDARY: a RISK CONTOUR            ╱                      A CLIMATE IS A MEAN.
└───────────────────────────────────────┘

   ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
   THE FOUR INPUTS, SIDE BY SIDE — the real content of the lesson

   MAP                INPUT                        BOUNDARY IS DRAWN BY
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   ① Climate class    30-year normals              a THRESHOLD on a statistic
   ② Vegetation biome observed cover               an ECOTONE (+ disturbance)
   ③ Agro-climatic    climate + soil + irrigation  frequently an ADMINISTRATIVE
                      + cropping + administration  line
   ④ Hazard/vulner-   event frequency + exposure   a RISK CONTOUR over a
      ability         + coping capacity            POPULATION, not over air
   ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   ►► NO TWO OF THESE FOUR BOUNDARIES ARE REQUIRED TO COINCIDE, AND THE FOURTH
      IS NOT EVEN A MAP OF THE ATMOSPHERE — IT IS A MAP OF PEOPLE AND ASSETS.
```

**Caption.** Four questions, four inputs, four kinds of boundary. The stack is offset deliberately:
if the four were drawn on top of one another the reader would see a single India and miss the entire
point.

### ✗ U8, applied — the Atlas, under its live title

✗ C2 line 65 names the source *"IMD **Climate Hazards and Vulnerability Atlas of India**"*. 📄 The
live title, re-read at generation time, is different:

| 📄 Verified at `https://www.imdpune.gov.in/hazardatlas/index.html`, HTTP 200, 10,225 B, 2026-10-04 |
|---|
| **Title: "HAZARD ATLAS OF INDIA"** |
| **Attribution:** *"Developed and maintained by **Climate Application and user Interface Group (CAUI)**, India Meteorological Department, **Pune-05**"* |
| **Section — Climate Hazard:** Wind Hazard · Extreme rainfall · Lightning · Dust Storm · HailStorm · Fog · Drought · Cyclone · Thunderstorm |
| **Section — Climate Vulnerability** |
| **Also present:** an e-book, an Atlas Summary and month selectors |
| **Extreme-rainfall periods offered:** 1951-1960 … 2011-2020, plus **1901-2019** and **1951-2019** |
| **Cyclone periods offered:** 1961-1970 … 2011-2020, plus **1961-2020** |

**Note what the period lists reveal**, because it is the lesson's deepest point: the Atlas is
organised in **decadal windows of event frequency** — 1951-1960, 1961-1970, and so on. **A climatic
normal is a thirty-year mean; a hazard layer is a decadal count of events.** The two are not merely
different maps; they are **different statistical objects over different windows**, and no boundary on
one has any obligation to a boundary on the other.

### ✗ B6, applied — the inference this lesson most has to refuse

📄 **No retrieved IMD surface classifies India into climatic zones.**

- 📄 The **normals publication** tabulates **stations** — 405 of them, with monthly and annual values
  (Lesson 6).
- 📄 The **Hazard Atlas** maps **hazards** and **vulnerability**.
- 📄 The **Data Service Portal** exposes a **station-and-month selector**.

**None of the three produces a climatic classification of India**, and **no sentence in this file
implies otherwise** (refusal 9). The temptation is real — IMD is the authoritative agency, the
Köppen-India map is standard, and the inference *"therefore IMD's climate map of India"* is one step
away. **It is a fabrication of officialdom, and it is refused.**

### 🇮🇳 India hook 15 — one district, one Köppen class, four different ratings

✅ Take any district inside Köppen's **Aw** — *most of the peninsular plateau, about 75 cm* (folio
160). It is **one** climate class. Now read it off the other three maps:

```text
   ONE DISTRICT, READ FOUR WAYS

   ① ✅ CLIMATE CLASS ......... Aw. Fixed by a 30-year normal. Changes only when
                                the normal changes (Lesson 16).
   ② ↪ VEGETATION ............ depends on what is actually standing — which may
                                be dry deciduous forest, or may be cropland that
                                replaced it. ✅ Köppen's own premise says NATIVE
                                vegetation; the map records what is there.
   ③ ↪ AGRO-CLIMATIC ZONE .... depends on soil, irrigation availability and
                                cropping history, and its boundary frequently
                                follows a DISTRICT line.
   ④ 📄 HAZARD LAYERS ........ the Atlas carries NINE separate climate hazards —
                                Wind · Extreme rainfall · Lightning · Dust Storm ·
                                HailStorm · Fog · Drought · Cyclone · Thunderstorm
                                — EACH WITH ITS OWN MAP. One district can be high
                                on lightning and low on cyclone.

   ►► THE DISTRICT HAS ONE CLIMATE CLASS AND UP TO NINE HAZARD RATINGS, AND
      NOTHING REQUIRES THEM TO AGREE. "Which map are you reading?" is therefore
      a real question, not a rhetorical one.
```

> **Stated limitation of this hook.** ⚠️ **This is an analytical construction, not a reading from any
> source.** No retrieved page states that a specific Indian district carries divergent ratings across
> the Atlas layers, and **no district is named here**. ✅ What is sourced is the **structure** — nine
> named hazard categories and a separate vulnerability section, re-confirmed 2026-10-04 — and ✅ C2's
> own analytical table at lines 71–79, which makes the same point and which this edition keeps while
> correcting its title. **The hook is a reasoning device, marked as one.**

### The complete causal chain — and where the chain legitimately ends

```text
   ✅ CLIMATE CLASS  ──────────────────────────────┐
   (a long-run statistic, threshold-bounded)       │
                                                   │  may INFORM
   📄 HAZARD FREQUENCY ────────────────┐           │  ▼
   (a decadal event count)             │      ┌────────────────────────┐
                                       ├─────►│  PLANNING DECISION     │
   ⚠️ EXPOSURE — population, crops,    │      │  (cropping calendar,   │
   assets, infrastructure              │      │  warning design,       │
                                       │      │  insurance, siting)    │
   ⚠️ COPING CAPACITY ─────────────────┘      └────────────────────────┘
                                                   │
                                                   ▼
                                     ↪ AND HERE TOPIC 14 STOPS.
                                       Preparedness, vulnerability reduction,
                                       warning dissemination and the NDMA chain
                                       belong to the Disaster-Management owners.
                                       Agro-climatic delineation belongs to Economy.

   ✅ C2's SURVIVING SENTENCE, kept: "Hazard becomes DISASTER through EXPOSURE
      and CAPACITY." ⚠️ (C2 line 75, ⚠️-marked in the owner and kept at that tier.)
   ✅ C2's OTHER SURVIVING SENTENCE, kept: "District risk can differ WITHIN ONE
      CLIMATE CLASS." That is the whole lesson in eight words.
```

### Comparison, objection, reply, residual

| | |
|---|---|
| **Comparison** | ✅ **A climate map** answers *what is normal here* over **thirty years**. 📄 **A hazard map** answers *what goes wrong here, how often* over **decadal windows**. ⚠️ **A vulnerability map** answers *who would be hurt* — and is a map of **people and assets**, not of the atmosphere at all |
| **Objection** | *"Köppen's thresholds were placed where vegetation changes. Doesn't that make the climate map and the biome map the same map by construction?"* |
| **Reply** | **No, and the reason is in Köppen's own premise.** ✅ Khullar folio 158 says the criterion is **native** vegetation. A vegetation map records **what is standing**, which includes cleared, cropped, grazed and planted cover — so the two maps diverge wherever land use has altered the cover, which in India is most places. **Köppen's thresholds were calibrated against the potential biome; the biome map shows the actual one.** They were designed to correspond and are not the same object |
| **Unresolved residual** | ✗ **No retrieved source supplies an agro-climatic zone map of India**, so that overlay is **named and described but never drawn with specifics** anywhere in this file. ✗ And **no district-level hazard values are quoted** — the Atlas's structure is cited, its contents are not |

### Traps specific to this lesson

- ❌ **Using C2's title for the Atlas.** ✗ **U8** — the live title is **"Hazard Atlas of India"**,
  with *Climate Hazard* and *Climate Vulnerability* sections, CAUI, IMD Pune-05.
- ❌ **Inferring an "IMD climate zones of India".** ✗ **B6** — no retrieved surface supplies one.
- ❌ **Reading disaster risk off a climate class.** ✅ C2 line 79 is right: *"Never infer disaster risk
  from climate class alone."*
- ❌ **Equating climate region with vegetation biome.** ✅ Köppen's criterion is **native** vegetation.
- ❌ **Equating climate region with agro-climatic zone.** The latter carries **soil, irrigation and
  administration**.
- ❌ **Comparing a thirty-year normal with a decadal hazard count as if they were the same
  statistic.** Different windows, different objects.
- ❌ **Teaching NDMA, preparedness or warning dissemination.** ↪ Disaster-Management owns them.
- ❌ **Quoting a district hazard value.** None appears here.

### Syllabus, PYQ and answer-use

**Syllabus.** This lesson is where Topic 14's *salient features* content meets its boundary with
three other subjects, and it is the lesson that keeps a GS-I climate answer from drifting into GS-III
disaster management without the owner's content.

**PYQ position.** ↪ **Topic 14 owns no routed PYQ.** The hazard half is owned by the
**Disaster-Management** owners; the climate-change half by
`Environment-and-Ecology/basic/17_Climate-Change-Science-Greenhouse-Effect`, which owns **2025 GS-I
Q4** — *"Climate change and sea-level rise affecting island nations"*, *Discuss*, 10 marks
(`_PYQ-ROUTING-MAINS-GS1-GS2-ESSAY-2024-2025.md` line 63). **Neither is answered here.** The
contribution is the separation itself: a candidate who can say *"a climate class is a thirty-year
statistic and a hazard rating is a decadal event count over an exposed population"* has made the
distinction the examiner is testing for, in one line.

**Answer use.** When an answer must move from climate to consequence, **declare the map change**:
*"Moving from the climatic class to the hazard layer changes the question from what is normal to what
goes wrong and to whom."* That sentence licenses the paragraph that follows and simultaneously proves
the candidate knows it needed licensing.

2. **Four maps, four questions:** what is normal · what grows · what can be grown with what inputs ·
   what goes wrong and to whom.
3. **Four different boundary types:** a **threshold** · an **ecotone** · frequently an
   **administrative line** · a **risk contour over a population**.
4. ✗ **U8: the live title is "HAZARD ATLAS OF INDIA"**, not C2's *"Climate Hazards and Vulnerability
   Atlas of India"*. 📄 Re-confirmed 2026-10-04, HTTP 200, 10,225 B.
5. 📄 **Attribution: Climate Application and user Interface Group (CAUI), India Meteorological
   Department, Pune-05.**
6. 📄 **Nine climate hazards:** Wind Hazard · Extreme rainfall · Lightning · Dust Storm · HailStorm ·
   Fog · Drought · Cyclone · Thunderstorm — **plus a separate Climate Vulnerability section**.
7. 📄 **The Atlas works in decadal windows** — extreme-rainfall periods 1951-1960 through 2011-2020
   plus 1901-2019 and 1951-2019; cyclone periods 1961-1970 through 2011-2020 plus 1961-2020.
   **A normal is a thirty-year mean; a hazard layer is a decadal count.**
8. ✗ **B6: no retrieved IMD surface classifies India into climatic zones.** Tables list stations; the
   Atlas maps hazards.
9. 🇮🇳 ⚠️ **One district has one climate class and up to nine hazard ratings**, and nothing requires
   them to agree. **Analytical construction, no district named.**
10. ✅ **Köppen's climate map and a vegetation map differ because Köppen's criterion is NATIVE
    vegetation** and a biome map records **what is standing**, including disturbance.
11. ✅ **C2's two surviving sentences, kept:** *"District risk can differ within one climate class"*
    and ⚠️ *"Hazard becomes disaster through exposure and capacity."*

### Concept check

**Question:** A planner argues that because two districts fall in the same Köppen class, they should
receive the same drought-preparedness allocation. Identify every map confusion in the argument.

**Model answer:** **Three confusions, each a different map.** **First, class for frequency.** A
Köppen class is a **threshold test on a thirty-year normal** — ✅ Aw means *coolest month above 18 °C,
dry winter, about 75 cm* across most of the peninsular plateau. It is a statement about a **central
tendency**, not about how often a failure occurs. 📄 The **Hazard Atlas of India** maps **Drought** as
one of nine climate hazards in **decadal windows**; a thirty-year mean and a decadal event count are
different statistical objects. **Second, hazard for risk.** Even an identical drought frequency would
not justify an identical allocation, because ⚠️ exposure — population, cropped area, irrigation cover,
assets — and **coping capacity** differ. ✅ C2 states it correctly: *"District risk can differ within
one climate class"*, and ⚠️ *"hazard becomes disaster through exposure and capacity."* **Third,
climate for agro-climate.** Allocation decisions operate on **agro-climatic zones**, which combine
climate with **soil, irrigation availability and cropping history** and whose boundaries frequently
follow **administrative lines** that climate does not. ✅ C1's control clause names all of these as
**separate units**: station normal, grid cell, climatic region, vegetation biome, agro-climatic zone
and administrative boundary. The planner has used one map to answer four questions. **The
preparedness question belongs to the Disaster-Management owners and the zoning question to the
Economy agriculture owners; Topic 14 supplies only the class.**

**Misconception to avoid:** That sharing a climate class implies sharing anything else. The class
answers exactly one question — where the long-run statistics fall relative to a set of thresholds —
and it carries no information about frequency of failure, about who is exposed, or about what the
soil will support.

### Original Mains practice — Lesson 15

**Original question (10 marks, 150 words).** *Argue* that a climatic-region map and a hazard map
answer different questions.

**Model (≤ 150 words):** A climatic region is a **threshold test on a thirty-year normal**: Köppen's
Aw is assigned where the coolest month exceeds 18 °C with a dry winter. It answers *what is normal
here*. A hazard map answers *what goes wrong here, how often, and to whom*. **IMD's Hazard Atlas of
India**, maintained by the Climate Application and user Interface Group, Pune, maps **nine** climate
hazards — wind, extreme rainfall, lightning, dust storm, hailstorm, fog, drought, cyclone,
thunderstorm — plus a separate **Climate Vulnerability** section, and it works in **decadal windows**,
not thirty-year means. The statistical objects therefore differ, and so do the boundaries: a class
boundary is a threshold, a hazard boundary is a risk contour drawn over a **population**. One district
may hold one climate class and several divergent hazard ratings. **Risk cannot be inferred from
class**, because hazard becomes disaster only through exposure and coping capacity. *(147 words)*

**Rubric (10 marks):** Four-map separation **3.5** · Hazard boundary **2.5** · Official title
accuracy **2.0** · Planning consequence **2.0** *(dimension weights 35 · 25 · 20 · 20)*
